Recollection Talks · Consecrated Life

The Ever-Evolving Consecrated Life

Sixteen 45-Minute Recollections for Priests, Religious and Major Seminarians, West Bengal Region — based on Sr Inigo SSA, delivered in the theological voice of Fr. Joseph Lobo SJ

Talk 1 — Consecrated Life Between Calcification and Conversion: A Theological Diagnosis Talk 2 — Identity as Theological Category: Charism, Distortion, and Return Talk 3 — Prophecy as Theological Vocation: Conflict, Cost, and the Domestication of Consecrated Life Talk 4 — Ecological Conversion as Theological Category: Creation, Covenant, and Complicity Talk 5 — The Incarnation as Communication: Presence, Platform, and the Theology of Showing Up Talk 6 — Misericordia: The Grammar of God's Existence and Ours Talk 7 — Carcere Et Vincula: Where Christ Already Waits Talk 8 — The Inviolability of Life: A Theological Case Against Capital Punishment Talk 9 — Signum Vitae: Signpost or Scenery? Talk 10 — Fides in Eremo: The Faith That Finds Water in the Wilderness Talk 12 — The Apostolate That Became a Market: Re-visioning Christian Education Talk 13 — Imago Dei Integra: The Suppressed Half of the Image of God Talk 14 — The Magnificat as Social Manifesto: Women's Dignity as Theological Category and Ecclesial Obligation Talk 15 — Canticum Periculosum: The Song the Powerful Tried to Silence Talk 16 — Into That Freedom: Retreat as Theological Category and the Completion of the Prophetic Arc

Talk 1 — Consecrated Life Between Calcification and Conversion: A Theological Diagnosis

"Before I formed you in the womb I knew you; before you were born I consecrated you." (Jeremiah 1:5)

⏱ TIME GUIDE

Section Duration
Opening: The Lived Contradiction 7 min
Part I — Two Models of Consecrated Life 10 min
Part II — The Theological Diagnosis 12 min
Part III — The Scriptural Constellation 8 min
Part IV — The Prophetic Demand 5 min
Silence & Closing Prayer 3 min

OPENING: THE LIVED CONTRADICTION

(7 minutes)

By way of introducing, let me begin with a well-known theological affirmation — and then with the reality that contradicts it.

We say: consecrated life is a sequela Christi — a following of Christ in the most radical manner available to a human being. The Second Vatican Council in Lumen Gentium 44 describes it as a sign that "more completely" expresses the heavenly goods already present in this age and anticipates the life to come. That is the claim.

And now the reality.

In a five-year period documented by Vatican statistics, nearly fifteen thousand religious left their congregations — not, it must be noted, because they ceased to believe in God, but because they experienced no spiritual fulfillment within the structures designed precisely for that purpose. Parents who know religious life from the inside — who have lived alongside it, offered their sons and daughters to it — are now deliberately steering their children away from it. And in many of our congregations, the question of relevance is not raised at chapter meetings precisely because it is too uncomfortable to raise.

The gap between the claim and the lived reality — that is our problematic this morning.

And I want to say, at the outset, that this is not a problematic of bad people. It is a problematic of an institution that has, in many of its expressions, undergone a specific theological reversal — a reversal that I shall name and attempt to diagnose in the course of this reflection.

In one sentence, if I were to state the thesis of this entire recollection: Consecrated life in Asia today is in crisis not because it has been abandoned by God, but because significant portions of it have substituted institutional maintenance for prophetic mission — and until this substitution is named, diagnosed, and reversed, no programme of renewal will touch the root of the problem.

Let me now develop that.

PART I — TWO MODELS OF CONSECRATED LIFE

(10 minutes)

Sr Inigo SSA, whose reflection forms the basis of our recollection, opens with an image that I find theologically precise: she says consecrated life in Asia today is like two sides of the same coin.

I want to develop this image theologically. Because a coin has two sides, but it has only one substance — and the question is what that substance actually is.

The first model — and it is still alive, still real — is what I would call the kenotic model of consecrated life. It proceeds from a genuine divine encounter. It is characterised by what Pope Francis, in Evangelii Gaudium 20, calls going out of oneself — going toward the periphery, toward the margin, toward the place where the Kingdom is not yet visible. In this model, the vows are not juridical obligations to be observed but radical orientations of a life that has genuinely grasped its own vocation. Poverty is not mere simplicity of lifestyle — it is a theological statement about where ultimate value resides. Chastity is not merely celibacy — it is a form of universal love, unencumbered by possession or domination. Obedience is not submission to a hierarchy — it is an attentiveness to the missio Dei as it moves through history and community discernment.

In this first model, the congregation exists to serve the mission. The institution is at the disposal of the charism.

The second model — and this is what Sr Inigo's statistics document — is the institutional-maintenance model. In this model, the roles are reversed. The mission is at the disposal of the institution. The congregation's primary energy goes to managing its schools, hospitals, and properties — not because these are not good works, but because they have ceased to be expressions of charism and have become self-perpetuating structures. The young men and women who enter find, often after years of generous waiting, that what they are being formed for is essentially a professional role within a religious enterprise, not a prophetic vocation within the missio Dei.

And here is the specific consequence of the second model, which must be stated plainly.

First: the congregation loses the capacity for re-founding. It can reform — it can paint the walls, revise the timetable, add new programmes — but it cannot question the structures themselves, because the structures have become the identity of the group.

Second: a growing disinclination — and Sr Inigo uses precisely this word — develops toward ministry in slums, prisons, rural outposts, and among Dalit and Tribal communities. This is not a moral failure; it is a structural consequence. Institutions are conservative by nature. They tend toward the sustainable, the manageable, the prestigious. And in India, with its specific caste-inflected social hierarchy, "prestigious" and "marginalised" remain, almost by definition, mutually exclusive.

Third — and this is perhaps the most theologically serious consequence — the generation gap within congregations becomes a wound rather than a gift. Sr Inigo observes that congregations are now receiving candidates who are thirty-five to forty years old, people who have lived in the world and carry with them a history that does not fit neatly into the novitiate timetable designed for an earlier social context. And the older members of the community, who carry decades of faithfulness, sometimes hold so tightly to received forms that they inadvertently suffocate the very people they are trying to form. This is not malice. It is the structural logic of a model that has prioritized maintenance over mission.

I repeat the thesis: the crisis of consecrated life is not a crisis of vocations. It is a crisis of models.

PART II — THE THEOLOGICAL DIAGNOSIS

(12 minutes)

Let me now attempt a theological diagnosis. And I want to do this by recovering two words — two words that have undergone the same kind of reversal that has affected consecrated life itself.

The first word is consecrare.

In its Latin root, consecrare is a verb. It means to set apart for — not merely to separate from. The preposition matters. Consecration is not primarily about withdrawal from the world; it is about a directed, purposeful movement toward a reality that the world cannot provide for itself. When the Second Vatican Council in Lumen Gentium 44 speaks of consecrated life as a sign, it means that the consecrated person is not a museum exhibit of holiness — they are a living signal, a sacramentum mundi, a visible transparency to what God intends for all of creation.

The problem is this: over the centuries, particularly from the post-Tridentine period onward, consecrare has gradually been converted from a verb into a noun. Consecration — the act of directed, ongoing, costly orientation — has become the consecrated — a status, a category, a legal condition. And when a verb becomes a noun, it loses its prophetic edge. It becomes manageable. Classifiable. Institutionalisable.

Walter Brueggemann rightly points out, in his analysis of the prophetic imagination, that every institution tends to domesticate the prophetic voices that gave it birth. The founder is eventually canonised — which is good — and then, subtly, the founder's charism is translated into manageable structures, which is precisely the moment when the institution begins to lose what the founder died for. This is not inevitable. But it requires a continuous act of retrieval — what Sr Inigo calls re-founding — to resist it.

The second word is prophētēs.

The Greek prophētēs means, literally, one who speaks pro — on behalf of, or in advance of. The prophet does not merely predict the future. The prophet reads the present in the light of God's project and names what must be named, regardless of the institutional cost. Abraham Heschel, the great Jewish philosopher of prophetic consciousness, says that the prophet is the one in whom God's pathos — God's passionate care for the marginalised — becomes theomorphic: it takes human form and speaks in a human voice.

Now, consecrated life was always meant to be prophetic in this precise sense. Not prophetic as a specialised vocation within the Church, but prophetic as a constitutive dimension of what it means to follow the God who, in Jeremiah 1:5, says: "Before I formed you in the womb I knew you; before you were born I consecrated you." Notice the sequence. The knowledge precedes the formation. The consecration precedes the birth. God's claiming of a life is always prior to the life's own understanding of itself.

But — and this is the diagnostic point — the prophetic dimension of consecrated life has been systematically weakened by what I would call the clericalist-institutional complex. Pope Francis, in his Apostolic Exhortation Evangelii Gaudium 102, names clericalism as one of the most serious pathologies of the contemporary Church. And clericalism — which is not limited to ordained clergy — is precisely the substitution of institutional power and prestige for prophetic mission. When a religious congregation begins to measure its success by the size of its campus, the prestige of its institutions, and the visibility of its members in elite circles, it has — however unconsciously — exchanged the prophetic for the clericalist.

Sr Inigo's seven-fold framework for renewal is, I would argue, a systematic reversal of this clericalist-institutional logic. Let me name the seven movements she proposes, but I want to name them theologically rather than merely descriptively.

From regularity to radicality: this is the movement from observance to discipleship. Observance is the fulfilment of obligations within a system. Discipleship is the continuous conversion of the whole person toward the one who said: "Follow me" — not: "Observe the timetable."

From rigidity to flexibility: this is the movement from law to Spirit. Paul, in 2 Corinthians 3:6, says: "The letter kills, but the Spirit gives life." The Spirit moves where it will (John 3:8). A congregation that cannot move where the Spirit moves has substituted its own administrative convenience for theological attentiveness.

From withdrawal to involvement: this is the movement from sacristy to world — the movement that Gaudium et Spes 1 makes programmatic when it says that the joys and hopes, the griefs and anxieties of the people of this age, especially the poor and afflicted, are the joys and hopes, the griefs and anxieties of the followers of Christ.

From asceticism to advocacy: this is perhaps the most significant movement for the Indian context. Personal asceticism without social advocacy is, in the prophetic literature, a form of religious narcissism. Isaiah 58:3-7 makes this explicit. The people ask: "Why have we fasted and You do not see?" And God answers: "Is not this the fast that I choose: to loose the bonds of injustice, to let the oppressed go free?" Fasting is not for the glory of the faster. It is for the transformation of the conditions that make others fast involuntarily.

From renewal to re-founding: this is the distinction between reform and reformation — or, to use the more theologically precise language of the tradition, between renovatio and metanoia. Metanoia — which has been domesticated in English as repentance, when it actually means a turning of the whole direction of one's life — is not an emotional response. It is a structural reorientation. Re-founding does not mean abandoning the charism. It means returning to the charism so radically that the institutional accretions that have obscured it are cleared away.

From managers to animators: this is the movement from administration to mission — from running an institution to animating a community of disciples. Karl Rahner, in his analysis of priestly and religious solitude, says that the greatest temptation of the religious leader is to confuse being busy with being fruitful. Business — negotium — is the absence of leisure; and leisure — schole — is the condition for genuine creativity and prophetic attentiveness.

From pious to prophetic: and this is the seventh and most difficult movement, because piety is visible and manageable, while prophecy is costly and often unwelcome. Max Weber, from a sociological point of view, is emphatic in distinguishing the priest from the prophet: the priest administers grace within a system; the prophet challenges the system in the name of grace. Weber intended this as a sociological distinction. In the theological tradition, it is a vocation — and the consecrated life was always meant to hold both. The tragedy is that in many of our congregations, the priestly-administrative function has entirely consumed the prophetic function.

PART III — THE SCRIPTURAL CONSTELLATION

(8 minutes)

I want to bring three scriptural texts into this argument — not as proof-texts but as witnesses, each adding a dimension that the others do not supply.

The first witness: Jeremiah 1:5-10.

"Before I formed you in the womb I knew you; before you were born I consecrated you; I appointed you a prophet to the nations."

And Jeremiah's response? "Ah, Lord God! Truly I do not know how to speak, for I am only a boy."

Notice what the divine call does not do. It does not wait for Jeremiah's competence. It does not begin with a formation programme. It does not consult the congregation's chapter. It begins with a prior knowing — a knowing that antecedes every institutional structure — and then it issues a commissioning that is, by its very nature, costly and socially dangerous.

The word prophet — nabi in Hebrew — carries a double resonance: one who is called to speak, and one who is carried along by the divine word, sometimes against one's own preference. Jeremiah, as we know, was not an enthusiast for his own vocation. He was, in the words of Jeremiah 20:9, one in whom the word became "like a burning fire shut up in my bones" — he tried to contain it and could not.

That is the theological texture of consecrated life at its most authentic. Not enthusiasm. Not institutional confidence. A fire that is not generated by the individual, and that the individual cannot finally suppress.

The second witness: Luke 5:1-11.

The scene is well known. Simon Peter and his companions have fished all night — with expertise, with commitment, with the knowledge of professionals — and have caught nothing. Jesus arrives and says: "Put out into the deep water and let down your nets for a catch."

The detail I want to draw your attention to is the word deep — bathos in Greek. Not elsewhere. Not further along the shore. Deep. It is an instruction about orientation, not merely about location. The deeper water is precisely where the professional fisherman's instincts say there is nothing to catch — and where the divine instruction says: go.

Peter complies. The nets break with abundance. And then — the detail that most readings pass over — Peter falls on his knees and says: "Go away from me, Lord, for I am a sinful man." He does not ask for more fish. He does not apply for a government subsidy. He encounters the holy — and is undone by it.

This is the katabasis that precedes all genuine mission: the going down, the recognition of one's own insufficiency, the encounter with the holiness of God that restructures one's sense of self. Without this katabasis, mission becomes management. And management — however competent — cannot do what only mission can do.

The third witness: Acts 1:8.

"You will receive power when the Holy Spirit has come upon you; and you will be my witnesses in Jerusalem, in all Judea and Samaria, and to the ends of the earth."

Note the geographical logic. It begins here — in Jerusalem, in the place of familiarity and comfort and, incidentally, the place of the crucifixion. It moves through Judea — the wider culture, the known world. It passes through Samaria — the place of the other, the theologically suspect, the socially compromised. And it reaches the ends of the earth — the frontier, the periphery, the place where the Church has not yet arrived.

This is the missionary logic of consecrated life: centrifugal, not centripetal. Moving outward, not consolidating inward. And in the Indian context — in West Bengal specifically — the ends of the earth are not geographically distant. They are Purulia. They are the slum-colonies of North Kolkata. They are the Dalit communities in Nadia and Murshidabad who have heard the Gospel and not yet seen its social consequences.

PART IV — THE PROPHETIC DEMAND

(5 minutes)

Sr Inigo's analysis of the post-COVID moment deserves separate theological attention, but I want to name it briefly here, because the pandemic is not merely a medical event. It is — as she rightly reads it — a kairos: a moment of disclosure, in which what has been hidden becomes visible.

Kairos is not merely a favourable moment. It is a moment that demands a response — a response that, if not made, does not simply delay things but actually forecloses a possibility. The tradition of kairos-theology, developed by Paul Tillich among others, insists that chronos — ordinary sequential time — flows within kairos — the depth dimension of time in which God's project is at stake. Missing a kairos is not like missing a bus. It is like missing a moment of grace whose specific shape will not return.

The pandemic disclosed six things that Sr Inigo names with theological precision. I shall summarise them in a different register.

It disclosed that security is an illusion — and that the consecrated life's deepest resource is not institutional stability but theological trust in the God who commands the sea (Mark 4:41).

It disclosed that hierarchy is a fragile arrangement — and that the Church's capacity to respond to crisis depends on the lateral bonds of community and mutuality, not on vertical chains of command that break down when the crisis is non-institutional in character.

It disclosed that the ghetto mentality is not protection but privation — that religious communities which had insulated themselves from the suffering world were precisely those least capable of responding to it with creativity and generosity.

It disclosed that the vows are not private spiritual disciplines — they are public, political, theological statements about what matters and what does not. A community that takes a vow of poverty and lives in structures that generate envy in the poor has not simply failed in personal virtue; it has made a contradictory theological statement.

It disclosed that uniformity is not unity — and that the linguistic, cultural, and theological diversity of our congregations in West Bengal — Bengali, Hindi, Santali, different regional traditions, different theological formations — is not a management problem. It is, theologically understood, a sign of the catholicity of the Church: kath' holou, according to the whole.

And finally, it disclosed that the language gap in formation is a justice issue. When candidates from rural West Bengal are formed entirely in English, when the theological library is inaccessible to those who think and pray in their mother tongue, the institution is not merely being inconvenient. It is — however unintentionally — replicating the colonial logic of a culture that measures intelligence by fluency in a language that is not one's own.

Until and unless consecrated life addresses this formation-justice question structurally — not merely with good intentions — the gap between the claim and the reality will persist.

SILENCE

(3 minutes)

(Speaker pauses. Sustained silence. Do not rush this.)

I want to leave you with one question — not five. One.

Not a question to be answered now. A question to be carried into prayer, into the conversation with God that will happen in the chapel this afternoon and in the hour before sleep tonight.

The question is this:

Which model of consecrated life am I actually living — and am I living it by choice, or by institutional inertia?

Do not answer quickly. Honest answers to theological questions rarely come quickly.

CLOSING PRAYER

God of Jeremiah, God of the burning fire that cannot be suppressed — You named us before we could name ourselves. You consecrated us before we had chosen anything.

And yet we have, in our better moments and our worse, made choices — about where to live, how to spend our days, what to build, what to protect, what to let go.

We confess that we have sometimes preferred the comfort of the institution to the discomfort of the charism. The security of the managed to the risk of the prophetic. The prestige of the visible to the obscurity of the margin.

We do not ask for easy conversions. We know what conversion costs — Jeremiah knew it, Peter knew it, Paul knew it, every serious follower of Yours has always known it.

We ask for honesty. The honesty to name what we are, and the courage to become what You called us to be before the institution had a form, before the congregation had a name, before the novitiate had a timetable.

Send us into deep water. And if the nets break — let them break with abundance.

Amen.

SPEAKER NOTES

Audience: Priests, Religious Women, Major Seminarians — West Bengal Region.

Register: This talk is written in the academic formation talk register of Fr. Joseph Lobo SJ — structured argument, theological rigour, prophetic sobriety, with pastoral warmth in the scriptural and closing sections. It is spoken, not read. The oral texture is built in: signposting, deliberate repetition, rhetorical questions held open.

Tone calibration: The talk does not manipulate emotion. It builds an argument and lets the argument do the work. The silence in Part IV is as important as anything spoken before it.

Pacing guidance: - The Opening section should be delivered with deliberate slowness. Let the gap between claim and reality sit uncomfortably. - The Two Models section (Part I) is the analytical heart. Move with precision, not speed. - The Theological Diagnosis (Part II) is the most demanding section. Do not rush the etymological recoveries — they are doing theological work, not decorative work. - The Scriptural Constellation (Part III) should feel warmer and more narrative. - The Prophetic Demand (Part IV) should be delivered with controlled urgency — not emotionalism. - The Silence is non-negotiable. Three minutes. Do not fill it.

Key theological moves to communicate clearly: 1. Consecrare as a verb, not a noun — ongoing direction, not acquired status. 2. The two models of consecrated life and their structural consequences. 3. The seven movements as theological reversals, not merely programmatic improvements. 4. The pandemic as kairos — a moment that discloses and demands, not merely disrupts.

Connection to Series: - This Talk (Ch. 1): Crisis and Call — What has happened to us, and what is being asked of us? - Talk 2 (Ch. 2–3): Spirituality and Identity — Who is the God we follow, and how does that reshape our self-understanding? - Talk 3: Mission — What does a re-founded consecrated life look like in West Bengal specifically?

Talk 2 — Identity as Theological Category: Charism, Distortion, and Return

"To all who received Him, He gave the right to become children of God." (John 1:12)

⏱ TIME GUIDE

Section Duration
Opening: The Problematic of Identity 7 min
Part I — Two Models of Religious Identity 10 min
Part II — Etymological Recovery: What "Charism" Actually Means 8 min
Part III — The Scriptural Constellation 9 min
Part IV — The Historical Diagnosis and Its Indian Consequence 8 min
Prophetic Demand & Silence 3 min

OPENING: THE PROBLEMATIC OF IDENTITY

(7 minutes)

By way of introducing this second reflection in our series, let me begin with a well-known theological affirmation — and then with the reality that contradicts it in specific and observable ways.

We say: every consecrated person possesses a distinct identity, rooted in a charism, expressed in a specific spirituality, and directed toward a concrete mission. The Second Vatican Council in Perfectae Caritatis 2 affirms that the renewal of consecrated life depends upon a return to the founding sources of each institute — to the spirit of the founders — and upon an adaptation of the institute to the changed conditions of the times.

That is the claim.

And now the reality.

Ask most religious today — priests, sisters, brothers — to describe their identity, and they will tell you: the name of their congregation, the number of their institutions, the year of their founding, and perhaps the habit they wear. They will tell you, in other words, about their coordinates — where they are located on the ecclesiastical map — but not about their substance — what flows from them and gives life. The difference between coordinates and substance is precisely the difference between a labeled bottle and what is inside it.

The consequences of this confusion are not trivial.

First: when identity is reduced to coordinates, the renewal of consecrated life becomes a project of institutional management rather than charismatic re-founding. You polish the coordinates. You update the logo. You revise the website. But the substance — the founding fire that gave everything else its meaning — remains unexamined and increasingly depleted.

Second: when identity is mislocated in external markers — habit, campus, statistical strength, bank balance — the congregation becomes unable to critique these markers even when they work against the mission. The institution protects itself from the charism that gave it birth.

Third: and this is the most serious consequence for the formation context — when young men and women in initial formation absorb a coordinates-based understanding of religious identity, they are being formed for institutional belonging, not for prophetic discipleship. They learn to identify with the congregation; they do not learn to be interrogated by it.

In one sentence, if I were to state the thesis of this reflection: Religious identity is a theological category, not a sociological one — and until consecrated persons recover identity as a dynamic, charismatic, and prophetically demanding reality rather than a static institutional status, the renewal of consecrated life will remain at the level of renovation rather than re-founding.

I shall now develop that thesis in four movements.

PART I — TWO MODELS OF RELIGIOUS IDENTITY

(10 minutes)

Let me identify two models of religious identity that are, in practice, operative in our congregations today. I shall call them, for the sake of precision, the parametric model and the charismatic model.

The parametric model — which is, I would argue, the default model in most of our congregations — defines identity by measurable parameters: numerical strength, institutional expansion, geographical spread, architectural visibility, financial security. Sr Inigo SSA, in the chapter that grounds this reflection, names these parameters with surgical honesty: distinctive dress, the number of convents opened, the size of campuses, the accumulation of financial assets, and what she calls the "frenetic rush of concrete results."

Now, I want to be careful here. These parameters are not intrinsically wrong. A congregation that grows in membership, that builds institutions for the poor, that develops financial stability in order to sustain mission — none of this is theologically suspect in itself. The problem is not the parameters. The problem is when the parameters become the definition of identity rather than the expression of it.

And the specific consequence of the parametric model is what I would call identity anxiety — a chronic institutional preoccupation with measuring, comparing, and defending one's position on the ecclesiastical map. A congregation living primarily in the parametric model is always looking over its shoulder at other congregations. It measures its significance by comparative growth. It interprets decline in membership as a theological crisis rather than as, possibly, a pneumatological purification. And it becomes incapable of the specific freedom that genuine mission requires — the freedom to let go of an institution when the institution has ceased to serve the charism.

The charismatic model — which Perfectae Caritatis 2 points toward, even if it does not name it in these terms — defines identity dynamically. Identity in this model is not a possession but a direction. It is not what a congregation has acquired but where it is consistently oriented. It flows from a founding encounter with God — an encounter that was, as Sr Inigo observes, genuinely disruptive for the founder — through a specific spirituality, toward a concrete mission, and expresses itself in ministries that serve the mission rather than substituting for it.

The distinction between these two models produces, in practice, a series of concrete differences.

In the parametric model, the question asked about any new ministry is: "Will this enhance our visibility and strengthen our institutional position?" In the charismatic model, the question is: "Does this flow from our charism, and does it take us toward those whom our charism sent us to serve?"

In the parametric model, the departure of members from a congregation is experienced primarily as institutional loss. In the charismatic model, it is experienced as an occasion for communal discernment: "What is the Spirit saying to us through this movement?"

In the parametric model, formation produces institutional loyalists. In the charismatic model, formation produces — or is designed to produce — prophetic disciples.

I want to come to the question of formation in greater detail a little later. But first let me turn to the central theological concept of this chapter: the meaning of charism itself.

PART II — ETYMOLOGICAL RECOVERY: WHAT "CHARISM" ACTUALLY MEANS

(8 minutes)

The word charism — charisma in Greek — is derived from charis, meaning grace or gift. But the specificity of charisma over against charis is important and is almost always lost in translation.

Charis is grace in its general sense: the gracious favor of God toward creation and humanity. Charisma — the word Paul uses in 1 Corinthians 12 — is a specific, particular, directed gift: a grace that has a shape, a target, and a purpose beyond the recipient. Paul's entire argument in 1 Corinthians 12:4-11 is that charisms are diverse precisely because the needs they address are diverse, and that their diversity is a sign of the Spirit's wisdom, not a problem to be managed into uniformity.

Now, over the centuries, and particularly in the post-Tridentine period, charisma underwent the same reversal I identified in our first reflection with respect to consecrare: it was converted from a verb into a noun. From a living movement of the Spirit, directed outward toward specific human need, it became a static possession: something a congregation has rather than something a congregation does and becomes.

When charism becomes a noun, several things follow. First: it becomes something to be preserved rather than exercised. The congregation develops a charism-preservation apparatus — constitutions, traditions, ceremonies, formation curricula — all designed to protect what is, in its original nature, a living fire. And the specific irony is that fire cannot be preserved. It can only be fed or allowed to die.

Second: when charism becomes a noun, it becomes justification for institutional continuity regardless of whether that continuity serves the original movement of the Spirit. Congregations continue doing what they have always done — not because their charism calls them to it in the present context, but because it is what they have always done. The charism, in other words, is invoked to legitimate the status quo rather than to interrogate it.

Third: when charism becomes a noun, the founder is canonized — spiritually and sometimes literally — and their charism is enshrined rather than inhabited. The founder becomes a museum exhibit rather than a living interlocutor. And the community periodically visits the museum — on feast days, at chapter meetings, during anniversaries — without allowing the founding vision to make any actual demands on the present.

The recovery of charism as a verb — as a living, directed, prophetically disruptive movement of the Spirit — is what Perfectae Caritatis 2 means when it calls for a return to "the spirit of the founders." Not the form of the founders. The spirit. The founding encounter with God that produced such urgency, such creativity, such willingness to bear social and ecclesiastical cost, that an institution eventually grew around it.

Sr Inigo's description of the founders — people who were "exceedingly disturbed and obsessed" until they responded adequately to their charism — is not hagiographical sentiment. It is a precise theological statement. The disturbance is the mark of an authentic charism at work. When the disturbance ceases — when the congregation becomes comfortable — the question must be asked: has the charism been fulfilled, or has it been suppressed?

In most cases, it has not been fulfilled. It has been suppressed by institutional comfort.

PART III — THE SCRIPTURAL CONSTELLATION

(9 minutes)

I want to bring three texts into conversation — each adding a dimension that the others do not supply.

The first witness: 1 Corinthians 12:4-7.

"Now there are varieties of gifts, but the same Spirit; and there are varieties of service, but the same Lord; and there are varieties of activities, but it is the same God who activates all of them in everyone. To each is given the manifestation of the Spirit for the common good."

The phrase I want to draw attention to is "for the common good" — in Greek, pros to sympheron, which means, literally, toward that which brings together and benefits. A charism is not given for the enhancement of the individual recipient, nor for the institutional enrichment of the congregation that receives it. It is given toward a convergence — a bringing-together of what is scattered, a healing of what is broken, a nourishing of what is depleted.

This means — and the consequence must be stated plainly — that a charism exercised primarily for the congregation's own institutional benefit has been diverted from its theological purpose. It has been taken out of circulation. And when the currency of grace is hoarded rather than circulated, it does to the congregation what hoarded currency does to an economy: it generates internal inflation and external poverty simultaneously.

The second witness: John 1:12-13.

"To all who received Him, who believed in His name, He gave the right to become children of God — who were born, not of blood or of the will of the flesh or of the will of man, but of God."

The anchor text of this series. But I want to read it now against the background of everything we have said about identity.

Note the threefold negation: not of blood, not of the will of the flesh, not of the will of man. John is dismantling three alternative accounts of identity. Identity is not genealogical — it does not come from which family, which caste, which ethnic community one belongs to. Identity is not biological — it does not emerge from natural capacity or human achievement. And identity is not institutional — it does not flow from the will of any human structure, however sacred.

Identity — according to John — is theogenic: born of God. It is entirely from outside. It cannot be manufactured, maintained, or managed. It can only be received. And the one who receives it is given, not a title, but a right — an exousia, which is the same word John uses elsewhere to describe the authority of Jesus himself. Exousia in John is always authority that flows from one's deepest identity, not authority that is imposed from without.

The consecrated person's identity, in this reading, is not their habit, their congregation, their ministerial title, or their institutional position. It is their status as child of God — a status that precedes every institutional affiliation and survives every institutional crisis. And the practical consequence is this: a religious whose primary identity is charismatic rather than parametric cannot be destabilised by institutional decline, because their identity does not reside in the institution.

The third witness: James 2:18.

"Show me your faith without works, and I will show you my faith by what I do."

James is not making a Protestant-Catholic argument about faith and works. He is making a deeper epistemological claim: that identity is only ever known through its expressions. The interior reality — faith, charism, spiritual commitment — is real, but it is not self-evidencing. It must be performed. Not performed in the theatrical sense, but in the philosophical sense: it must be enacted in the public world, in concrete acts that are recognizable to others as expressions of a particular life.

This is what it means to say that charism must produce a recognizable identity in the community being served. The people of Purulia, the slum-dwellers of North Kolkata, the Santali villagers of Birbhum — they do not read constitutions. They do not attend chapter meetings. They experience congregational identity through the specific quality of presence and service they receive. And if that quality is indistinguishable from what a competent secular NGO provides, then the charism — however well-preserved in institutional form — has ceased to produce its proper fruit.

Cardinal Emmanuel Suhard once said that the Christian — and the same applies with greater intensity to the consecrated person — must "live in such a way that one's whole life would be inexplicable if God did not exist." That is the test of charismatic identity. Not: is the congregation growing? But: is this life theologically inexplicable by any other means than divine charism?

PART IV — THE HISTORICAL DIAGNOSIS AND ITS INDIAN CONSEQUENCE

(8 minutes)

Let me now attempt the historical diagnosis. When did the reversal from the charismatic to the parametric model occur, and what drove it?

I would locate the decisive shift in the post-Tridentine period — specifically in the sixteenth and seventeenth centuries — when the Counter-Reformation Church, responding to the Protestant critique, invested heavily in visible institutional presence as a marker of Catholic identity. Schools, hospitals, missions, confraternities — these were not merely charitable works. They were, in the post-Tridentine logic, signs of Catholic vitality against Protestant challenge. The congregation that built the most, educated the most, converted the most, was demonstrating the superiority of the Catholic form of Christian life.

The consequence — unintended but structurally inevitable — was that institutional productivity became the primary metric of congregational success. And once a metric is established, it generates its own institutional logic. Congregations that were founded in the fifteenth century to serve the destitute in streets and gutters found themselves, by the eighteenth century, running prestigious academies for the bourgeoisie — because that is where the institutional growth was.

Edward Schillebeeckx, in his careful historical analysis of ministry in the Church, makes a related observation: the institutionalization of any charism follows a predictable pattern. The founding urgency produces structures to sustain it. The structures develop their own bureaucratic logic. The bureaucratic logic gradually displaces the founding urgency, not through malice but through the structural tendency of institutions to self-perpetuate. Schillebeeckx calls this the "hardening" of charism — and he notes that it is this hardening, not any crisis of faith, that has historically produced the most serious renewals in the Church's life.

Now, the Indian consequence of this historical diagnosis must be stated directly.

The congregations that came to India in the colonial period — many of them in the nineteenth and early twentieth centuries — brought with them this post-Tridentine institutional logic already fully formed. They built schools, hospitals, and training institutions of genuine value. But they built them within a social logic that was also colonial: education in English, for the upwardly mobile, oriented toward the formation of a Westernised Indian Catholic professional class.

The charism — in many cases, a charism genuinely directed toward the poor and marginalised — was gradually re-routed through the prestige institutions toward populations that were not the original intended recipients of the founding vision.

From a sociological point of view, Max Weber is emphatic in noting that religious charisma undergoes "routinization" — a conversion of the original prophetic urgency into stable, reproducible institutional forms. What Weber describes sociologically, theology must evaluate normatively: not all routinization is betrayal, but all routinization requires periodic prophetic critique if the charism is to remain alive.

In the Indian context specifically — in West Bengal, in the Northeast, in the Dalit and Tribal communities of this region — this critique is not abstract. It asks: who is actually benefiting from the institution? Who is being reached that would not otherwise be reached? And who is not being reached — not because they are far away, but because the institutional logic has reoriented the congregation toward populations that generate more sustainable support?

Sr Inigo's framework of genuine identity — charism → spirituality → mission → ministries → identity — is, in the Indian context, a structural critique of every congregation that has allowed the sequence to reverse: that is, every congregation that defines its ministries first, defends them institutionally, and then retrospectively constructs a charism-narrative to justify what is essentially an inheritance of historical social location.

The reversal of this sequence — returning from ministries to mission, from mission to spirituality, from spirituality to charism, from charism to the founding encounter with God — is what Perfectae Caritatis 2 means by "return to the roots." It is not nostalgia. It is not archaism. It is the rigorous theological discipline of asking: what, in this present moment, does our founding charism require of us? And then having the courage to be told.

PROPHETIC DEMAND & SILENCE

(3 minutes)

I want to close with a demand — not a consolation.

Until and unless consecrated persons recover identity as a charismatic category rather than a parametric one — until charism is rehabilitated as a verb rather than a noun, as a living direction rather than a static possession — the renewal of consecrated life in Asia will remain an institutional project rather than a prophetic one. And institutional projects, however well-funded and well-intentioned, cannot do what only a prophetic life can do.

The question I leave with you is this. One question:

In your congregation's present institutional life — which ministries are expressions of your charism, and which are inheritances of your historical social location? And are you willing to ask that question honestly enough to let it change something?

Do not answer now. Sit with it in silence.

(Sustained silence — minimum four minutes. Do not fill it.)

CLOSING PRAYER

*God of the founding encounter — You disturbed our founders until they responded. You made them restless, sleepless, socially inconvenient, theologically suspicious to the comfortable and dangerously hopeful to the poor.

We confess that we have, in many of our works, preserved the coordinates of their obedience while losing the direction of it.

We have kept the name and let the substance thin. We have kept the building and let the fire go out. We have kept the timetable and lost the urgency.

Give us the honesty to name this. Not with guilt — guilt is cheap and changes nothing — but with the theological sobriety of people who know that charism is not our property to manage but Your gift to be exercised.

Disturb us again. Make us exceedingly uncomfortable until we respond adequately.

And let the people of this region — the poor of West Bengal, the tribal communities of Jharkhand and the Northeast, the Dalit families who have waited long enough for the Gospel to reach their lives rather than their doorsteps — let them be the ones who tell us, by their response to our presence, whether our charism is alive.

We ask this in the name of the one who gave us the right to be children of God — not subjects of an institution, not managers of a programme, but children. Free, loved, sent.

Amen.*

SPEAKER NOTES

Register: Academic formation talk — the register of Fr. Joseph Lobo SJ. Structured argument, theological rigour, prophetic sobriety. The warmth enters in the scriptural section and the closing prayer. The overall tone remains intellectually honest and prophetically urgent — not emotionally warm throughout.

Key theological moves: 1. The parametric vs. charismatic model of identity — with structural consequences of each shown, not merely described. 2. Charisma recovered etymologically from noun to verb — from static possession to living direction. 3. The scriptural constellation: 1 Cor 12 (pros to sympheron), John 1:12 (exousia and theogenic identity), James 2:18 (enacted identity as epistemological claim). 4. Historical diagnosis: the post-Tridentine institutionalisation of charism and its colonial inflection in the Indian context. 5. The Indian consequence stated directly: the question of who actually benefits from the institution.

Secular thinkers deployed: - Max Weber: routinization of charism (sociological complement to the theological argument) - Edward Schillebeeckx: historical hardening of charism (historical complement) - Cardinal Emmanuel Suhard: the test of inexplicable life (pastoral application)

Pacing: - The Opening and Part I should move with deliberate precision — building the argument before making pastoral application. - The Etymological Recovery (Part II) is the theological heart. Give it time. The verb-to-noun reversal of charisma is a move that takes a moment to register — and once it registers, it does not leave. - The Scriptural Constellation (Part III) may be slightly warmer in delivery — allow the John 1:12 reading to breathe before unpacking exousia. - The Historical Diagnosis (Part IV) should be delivered with controlled sobriety. The Indian consequence paragraph is the sharpest moment in the talk — do not soften it. - The silence after the closing demand is non-negotiable. Four minutes minimum. The question must be allowed to do its work.

Talk 3 — Prophecy as Theological Vocation: Conflict, Cost, and the Domestication of Consecrated Life

"The Lord warned Israel and Judah through all His prophets and seers: Turn from your evil ways." (2 Kings 17:13)

⏱ TIME GUIDE

Section Duration
Opening: The Problematic of Prophetic Domestication 7 min
Part I — Two Models of Religious Presence: Cultic and Prophetic 10 min
Part II — Etymological Recovery: What Prophētēs Actually Demands 8 min
Part III — The Scriptural Constellation: The Six Conflicts of Jesus 10 min
Part IV — Four Structural Suppressors and the Cost of Prophetic Witness 7 min
Prophetic Demand & Silence 3 min

OPENING: THE PROBLEMATIC OF PROPHETIC DOMESTICATION

(7 minutes)

By way of introducing this third and concluding reflection in the first part of our series, let me begin — as we have consistently begun — with a theological affirmation and then with the reality that contradicts it in observable and structurally locatable ways.

We say: the consecrated life is, by its nature, prophetic. The Second Vatican Council in Lumen Gentium 44 describes consecrated persons as signa — signs — of the Kingdom: visible, public, and necessarily counter-cultural testimonies to a reality that the dominant social order neither produces nor values. Pope Francis, in his address to the Union of Superiors General in November 2013, was characteristically direct: "Religious are prophets." Not devout people. Not professionally religious people. Prophets. And the prophetic function, as the entire biblical tradition understands it, is structurally and inevitably conflictual. Prophets do not exist in comfortable institutional relationships with the powers they are called to challenge.

That is the claim.

And now the reality.

Karl Rahner — whose theological stature in the Catholic tradition of the twentieth century is genuinely beyond dispute — looked at the institutional Church of his day and said something that should stop any serious person of faith cold. He observed that the Church's public face is dominated to a terrifying extent by ritualism, legalism, administration, and what he named "a boring and resigned spiritual mediocrity continuing along familiar lines."

Ritualism. Legalism. Administration. Mediocrity.

These are not the characteristics of a prophetic community. They are the characteristics of a domesticated one. And the question this reflection must confront — directly, theologically, without the consolation of institutional self-congratulation — is this: how did a community founded upon the witness of people who were stoned, beheaded, crucified, and burned alive for refusing to accommodate the dominant social order arrive at a condition accurately described by one of its greatest theologians as boring mediocrity?

The answer is not that the consecrated life has been inhabited by bad people. The answer is that it has undergone a specific, historically diagnosable process that I shall call prophetic domestication — a process by which the conflictual, costly, socially dangerous dimensions of prophetic vocation have been progressively absorbed into institutional life and rendered manageable, predictable, and harmless to the very powers the prophetic tradition was always designed to challenge.

In one sentence, if I were to state the thesis of this reflection: The renewal of consecrated life as genuine prophetic witness requires not merely a recovery of prophetic language or sentiment, but a structural confrontation with the specific institutional mechanisms by which the prophetic vocation has been domesticated — mechanisms that are historically locatable, theologically nameable, and therefore, at least in principle, reversible.

Let me develop this across four movements.

PART I — TWO MODELS OF RELIGIOUS PRESENCE: CULTIC AND PROPHETIC

(10 minutes)

There are two models of religious presence that have coexisted, in tension, throughout the entire history of Christianity. The balance between them determines, more than any other single factor, whether a consecrated community is genuinely prophetic or merely institutionally religious.

I shall call them the cultic model and the prophetic model.

The cultic model understands the primary function of consecrated life as the maintenance and celebration of the sacred: the liturgy, the sacraments, the ordered rhythms of prayer, the spiritual care of the believing community. In this model, the religious is above all a person of God — set apart from the world, elevated above its immediate concerns, performing the sacred duties that the community of faith requires. The cultic model has genuine and indispensable theological legitimacy. The ora of the Benedictine ora et labora is not peripheral to the Church's life. A consecrated community that abandons contemplative prayer in favour of social activism has misunderstood the nature of prophetic witness as seriously as one that never moves beyond the chapel.

But the cultic model becomes a pathology — and this is the critical point — when ritual performance substitutes for moral transformation. When the celebration of the sacred becomes, in practice, a mechanism for avoiding the demands of the sacred. When the liturgy that was designed to form the community for mission becomes instead a protective enclosure that shields the community from the mission's cost.

This substitution has a long and precisely documented history in the biblical prophetic tradition. It is the pathology that Isaiah names in Isaiah 1:11-17, where God says: "I have had enough of burnt offerings... I do not delight in the blood of bulls... Your hands are full of blood. Wash yourselves. Cease to do evil. Seek justice. Rescue the oppressed." It is the pathology that Amos identifies in Amos 5:21-24, where God says: "I hate, I despise your festivals... but let justice roll down like waters, and righteousness like an ever-flowing stream." And it is the pathology that Jesus diagnoses in Matthew 23:23-24, where he says to the Pharisees: "You tithe mint, dill, and cumin, but you have neglected the weightier matters of the law: justice, mercy, and faith."

The prophetic tradition's critique of the cultic is not, I must be careful to say, a critique of worship as such. It is a critique of worship deployed as a substitute for justice — of the sacred instrumentalized as a refuge from the demands of the sacred.

The prophetic model of religious presence understands the primary function of consecrated life not as the maintenance of the sacred but as the interruption of the profane. The prophet does not stand apart from the world in order to be protected from it. The prophet stands at the precise intersection of the divine word and the human situation — and at that intersection, names what the dominant social order is unwilling to name, regardless of the institutional cost. Walter Brueggemann, in his analysis of prophetic imagination, identifies two constitutive functions of the prophetic: the criticizing of the dominant consciousness — naming what is wrong in the present arrangement of power and privilege — and the energizing of an alternative consciousness — opening, for the community, a vision of what God intends in its place.

Sr Inigo's account of the early Christian communities in Acts 2:44-47 and Acts 4:32-35 is an illustration of the prophetic model at its most coherent. These communities did not merely celebrate the Eucharist. They enacted it. They shared property, ate together across the social fault-lines of the Roman Empire — slave and master, Jew and Greek, man and woman at the same table — and created within the hostile social world of their day a visible, structurally embodied alternative. And the Empire's response to this — persecution, precisely because the community was genuinely dangerous to the social order — is itself the confirmation that the prophetic model was operating.

The Constantinian settlement of the fourth century was the decisive structural turning point. The granting of imperial favour, tax exemption, and political visibility to the Church was not merely a historical event. It was a structural temptation of the first order. And the Church, with honourable exceptions and against the protests of its prophetic voices in every subsequent century, largely accepted that temptation. Quality gave way to quantity. The radical community of equals was progressively replaced by a hierarchical institution that mirrored the social structures it was originally designed to challenge. The poor drifted from the centre of the mission to its margins. And the cultic model — manageable, prestigious, politically acceptable, administratively convenient — progressively domesticated the prophetic one.

This is not a counsel of despair about the institutional Church. But it is a condition of honest theological self-understanding. And the recovery of the prophetic model — which Perfectae Caritatis 2 gestures toward, which Evangelii Gaudium 20 insists upon, which the witness of modern martyrs embodies at the cost of blood — requires, first of all, that the domestication be named for what it is.

PART II — ETYMOLOGICAL RECOVERY: WHAT PROPHĒTĒS ACTUALLY DEMANDS

(8 minutes)

Let me now perform the etymological recovery that this argument requires — returning a key word to its original verbal force before it was converted into a manageable institutional category.

The Greek prophētēs — from which the English prophet derives — is a compound: pro (before, or on behalf of) + phēmi (to speak). The prophētēs is one who speaks pro: either in advance of an event, or on behalf of a reality or a person who cannot adequately speak for themselves. The common rendering — the prophet as someone who predicts the future — captures the first meaning but almost entirely loses the second. And it is the second meaning that is theologically decisive.

The prophētēs in the biblical tradition is primarily the one who speaks on behalf of God — and speaks on behalf of those whose cause God's justice demands be heard. The Hebrew nabi — which the Septuagint renders as prophētēs — carries the additional resonance of one who is borne along by the divine word: not a person who chooses to speak as a matter of personal conviction, but a person who is compelled to speak, even against their own preference and comfort, because the word of God has become, in Jeremiah's devastating formulation, "like a burning fire shut up in my bones" (Jeremiah 20:9) that cannot finally be contained.

Now, what has happened to prophētēs in the dominant institutional self-understanding of consecrated life is precisely what happened to consecrare and charisma in the distortions we examined in our first two reflections: it has been converted from a verb into a noun. From a living, costly, publicly enacted vocation into an adjective of institutional self-description. Prophetic appears in congregational constitutions, mission statements, and chapter resolutions — without necessarily involving any of the structural conflict, social cost, or personal risk that the biblical tradition consistently and universally associates with the prophetic vocation.

A congregation can describe itself as prophetic in its documents while its actual institutional life is entirely accommodated to the dominant social and economic order. A religious can speak of their prophetic calling in retreat contexts while their daily life generates no discomfort whatsoever in the powerful — and no concrete solidarity whatsoever with the marginalized. This is prophētēs as noun: a category of self-understanding, a dimension of institutional identity, a label. What the biblical tradition demands is prophētēs as verb: a continuous, costly, structurally embodied orientation toward justice, enacted in specific public acts that carry genuine institutional and personal risk.

Abraham Heschel — whose analysis of the prophetic consciousness in the Hebrew tradition remains, in my judgment, the most theologically penetrating account available — makes a distinction that is essential here. He argues that the prophet is not someone who holds strong opinions about justice as a matter of personal ethical conviction. The prophet is someone in whom the divine pathos — God's passionate, suffering identification with the afflicted — has taken human form. The prophet is not arguing about justice from a position of external moral authority. The prophet is suffering with the one for whom justice has been denied, because the God who suffers with the afflicted has claimed the prophet's entire person for that solidarity.

This is why authentic prophetic witness is invariably recognizable by its cost — not by its rhetoric. The rhetorical dimension of prophecy is real, but secondary. The primary dimension is what I would call the prophetic wager: the willingness to stake one's institutional security, social acceptability, and potentially one's physical safety on the claim that the God of justice is real — and that this reality demands specific, non-negotiable, embodied action here, among these people, in this social situation, now.

PART III — THE SCRIPTURAL CONSTELLATION: THE SIX CONFLICTS OF JESUS

(10 minutes)

I want to build the scriptural argument around a single, structurally important theological observation: Jesus was not crucified by accident or by misunderstanding. He was crucified as the direct, traceable consequence of specific, repeated, deliberately chosen confrontations with the institutional powers of his day. And if the prophetic vocation is — as Ad Gentes 5 explicitly affirms — a participation in the mission of Jesus, then those confrontations are not merely historical data about a first-century Palestinian teacher. They are a structural map of what prophetic witness entails in every social and ecclesial context.

Sr Inigo identifies six conflict-zones in the ministry of Jesus. I want to engage each theologically, attending to the detail that most readings pass over.

The first conflict: with the religious leadership.

Matthew 23:13-33 — the seven "woes" addressed publicly to the scribes and Pharisees — is the most sustained confrontational speech in the entire Synoptic tradition. Seven times, in the Temple precincts, in the presence of the crowds: "Woe to you, scribes and Pharisees, hypocrites." The Greek hypokritēs means, literally, one who plays a role — an actor whose public persona bears no correspondence to their actual person.

The prophetic charge is not that the scribes and Pharisees are wicked. It is that they have perfected the performance of religion — with precision, with institutional visibility, with social credibility — while evacuating from that performance its substance: "justice, mercy, and faith" (Matthew 23:23). The whitewashed tomb is not an image of malice. It is an image of dissociation: a carefully maintained exterior bearing no relationship to the interior reality it is supposed to represent.

The question this text puts to every religious community is not comfortable: what is the actual relationship, in our institutional life, between the exterior — the habit, the timetable, the liturgical calendar, the reputation — and the interior, which is the concrete condition of our engagement with the poor, the marginalized, and the truth?

The second conflict: with political authority.

Before Pilate (Matthew 27:13-14), Jesus does not negotiate. Before Herod (Luke 23:9), he maintains sustained silence. These two responses — the brief refusal of negotiation and the deliberate silence — are not passivity. They are prophetic acts. Dietrich Bonhoeffer, who understood political authority and prophetic response at a cost most of us will never face, said that there are moments when the only authentically Christian response is to refuse the terms that the powerful have set — not to argue within their framework, but to stand outside it entirely, bearing witness to a legitimacy that their authority cannot confer or revoke.

The third conflict: with tradition.

The Sabbath healings (John 5:15-16), the disciples eating without ritual handwashing (Mark 7:5-9), the touching of the leper — these are not instances of casual irreverence toward received tradition. They are deliberate, precisely targeted theological acts. Each one embodies a principle that Mark 2:27 states explicitly: "The Sabbath was made for humankind, and not humankind for the Sabbath." Human dignity is the criterion by which every traditional practice must be evaluated. When a tradition — liturgical, canonical, institutional — functions to preserve an arrangement of privilege rather than to serve human flourishing, the prophetic vocation requires its critique. Not its abandonment — its critique. The prophet does not discard tradition. The prophet holds tradition accountable to its own deepest intention.

The fourth conflict: with his immediate community.

John 6:52, 66 — after the Bread of Life discourse: "many of his disciples drew back and no longer went about with him." Mark 14:50 — at the arrest in Gethsemane: "all his disciples deserted him and fled." The people Jesus had formed, invested in, walked with — at the hour of prophetic cost, they were absent.

Henri Nouwen, in his analysis of the "wounded healer," observes that the prophetic person's most characteristic and deepest wound is almost always the wound of abandonment by the very community that was supposed to share the mission. This is not a reason to abandon the community. But it is a reason to locate one's ultimate grounding not in communal solidarity — which is real and necessary but always, in the end, fragile — but in the relationship with God that the community is meant to express and serve.

The fifth conflict: with his own family.

Mark 3:21 — "his family went out to restrain him, for people were saying, 'He has gone out of his mind.'" The people who knew Jesus longest, loved him most specifically, and had the greatest personal investment in his safety — they were the ones who concluded that he had lost his judgment. The prophetic vocation regularly produces this form of opposition: the opposition of those who love you and are therefore most threatened by what the vocation is costing them. In the Indian formation context — where family bonds are not merely affective but structurally central to social life and social honour — this dimension of the prophetic cost is particularly acute and must be named rather than evaded.

The sixth conflict: with God.

Matthew 27:46 — "My God, my God, why have you forsaken me?" — the opening verse of Psalm 22, spoken from the cross. This is the deepest conflict in the prophetic life: the moment when the very God on whose authority the prophet has staked everything appears to be absent — precisely when the prophet has paid the full cost of fidelity.

It is essential to say what this text is and is not. It is not a text about theological doubt in the abstract. It is a text about the experience — from within a dying human body — of the apparent silence of the God who was always supposed to be present. And its theological weight is that Jesus has spoken it. The prophetic vocation does not protect the prophet from the experience of divine absence. It offers, instead, a tradition of honest witness within which that experience can be held — not resolved, not explained away, but held — in the context of a life genuinely oriented toward God.

The Resurrection is not an explanation of Matthew 27:46. It is the presence of the one who asked the question, on the other side of what appeared to be its definitive and final answer. That has always been enough. It is, in fact, the only thing that makes the prophetic wager genuinely possible.

PART IV — FOUR STRUCTURAL SUPPRESSORS AND THE COST OF PROPHETIC WITNESS

(7 minutes)

If the prophetic vocation is genuine — if it is, as I have argued, not merely a dimension of institutional self-description but a structurally embodied, personally costly orientation — then we must ask: what are the specific institutional mechanisms by which it is being suppressed in our congregations today? Sr Inigo identifies four. I want to name each one in theological register.

The first suppressor: the culture of informed silence.

We live in a moment of unprecedented informational access. The bonded labourer in Murshidabad, the trafficked girl from Malda, the adivasi community in Purulia watching their forest disappear, the undertrial prisoner in Dum Dum Jail who has been waiting seven years for a hearing — their situations are documented. They appear in reports, in WhatsApp forwards, in newspaper articles accessible to anyone with a phone.

And the response of most religious communities to this information is — silence. Not ignorance. Silence. A carefully maintained, institutionally protective, reputationally cautious silence.

Albert Einstein, from a very different discipline, identified the mechanism precisely: the world, he said, will not be destroyed by those who do evil, but by those who watch them without doing anything. In the theological register, silence in the context of documentable injustice is not a neutral absence. It is a choice. And it is a choice with a theological name: complicity.

The second suppressor: the logic of institutionalisation.

I have addressed this in our previous two reflections, but it requires restating in the prophetic register. The institution — the school, the hospital, the social centre — was built to serve the mission. Over time, through the structural logic that Edward Schillebeeckx identified as the hardening of charism, the relationship reverses: the mission is made to serve the institution. Decisions are made not by asking "What does the God of justice require of us here?" but by asking "What will protect our registration, our land, our annual returns?"

A mission that has reorganized itself to protect its institutional interest has, in the most precise theological sense, lost the mission. It retains the form. It no longer inhabits the substance.

The third suppressor: the culture of internal domination.

Sr Inigo names this suppressor with what I consider the talk's most courageous observation: dominant cultural groups within congregations impose their way of being on minority members, producing consequences she calls "disastrous and insensitive." The Hindi-speaking formation house that marginalizes the Bengali-speaking candidate. The urban community that dismisses the rural formation candidate as insufficiently sophisticated. The senior member who treats the junior not as a companion in mission but as a subordinate to be managed.

The theological consequence is not merely a community relations problem. A congregation that practices cultural elitism within its walls while speaking prophetically about caste discrimination in the street has produced a contradiction that will, in time, discredit both the internal practice and the external witness. Prophetic integrity is not divisible. You cannot be prophetic outside the convent and hierarchical within it.

The fourth suppressor: the culture of individualism and disposability.

The convergence of social media culture — the performance of self, the carefully curated institutional identity — with the broader throwaway culture that discards goods, relationships, and people when they cease to be useful, produces a spiritual posture that is, in its deepest structure, incompatible with prophetic vocation. The throwaway culture is not merely an ethical failure. It is, as Pope Francis argues in Evangelii Gaudium 53, the concrete social expression of a market logic that assigns value to human beings by their productive utility — a logic that is, in the most direct sense, the logic of anti-prophecy. The prophetic tradition is built upon the infinite, non-negotiable value of the human person before God — a value that no market, no institution, no social utility can confer or revoke.

Now, the cost of resisting these suppressors. Sr Inigo names the modern martyrs — Romero, Rani Maria, Valsa John, the martyrs of Kandhamal — not as exceptional figures whose witness is beyond ordinary emulation, but as the visible tip of a prophetic cost that every serious follower of Jesus is asked to pay in less dramatic but no less real forms. Not every prophet sheds blood. But every prophet sheds something: reputation, security, institutional advancement, the approval of the powerful, the comfort of accommodation.

Tertullian, in the third century, watching the martyrs die, observed that the blood of the martyrs is the seed of the Church. The principle holds beyond physical martyrdom. Every act of genuine prophetic witness — every decision to name what must be named, to stand where the institution finds it inconvenient to stand, to be present with those the dominant social order renders invisible — plants something. It does not always flower in the planter's lifetime. It does not need to. The prophetic vocation has never required visible results as its justification.

The justification of prophetic witness is the reality of the God who sends it.

PROPHETIC DEMAND & SILENCE

(3 minutes)

Let me close this third reflection — and with it the first part of our series — with a demand rather than a consolation.

We have walked, across three reflections, a single theological arc: from the crisis of mission ("Am I still alive?"), through the crisis of identity ("Do I know who I am?"), to the crisis of courage ("Am I genuinely prophetic — or have I been domesticated?"). These are not three separate questions. They are three dimensions of a single question about the integrity of consecrated life. You cannot be prophetic without being genuinely rooted in your charism. You cannot be genuinely rooted in your charism without being alive to the God who gave it.

The text of 2 Kings 17:13 — "Turn from your evil ways" — is addressed in its original context not to the wicked, the apostate, or the enemies of Israel. It is addressed to God's own people, who had built the institutions, performed the rituals, and maintained the forms — while losing the substance that gave the forms their meaning. The prophets warned them. They did not listen. And the consequences were institutional, social, and spiritual collapse.

The parallel is not comfortable. It is, however, precise.

The question I leave with you is this — one question, for this afternoon's prayer and tonight's silence:

What is the specific act of prophetic witness that your social context, your ministry setting, and your congregation's charism require of you — and what is the specific cost you have been unwilling to pay?

(Sustained silence — minimum four minutes. Do not fill it.)

CLOSING PRAYER

*God of Jeremiah, God of Amos, God of the ones who were driven to the brow of the hill and led to the edge —

You have always needed people willing to say what others would not say, to stand where others would not stand, to remain present where others found reasons to be elsewhere.

We confess that we have, in many of our choices, preferred the cultic to the prophetic: the beautiful liturgy over the costly solidarity, the institutional reputation over the inconvenient truth, the managed charity over the dangerous justice.

Forgive us not with words but with the same burning fire You shut up in Jeremiah's bones.

Make us inconvenient. Make us structurally uncomfortable to the arrangements that keep the poor poor and the powerful powerful and the silence of complicity mistaken for the peace of God.

Give us the courage of Romero, who knew what was coming and said Mass anyway. Give us the fidelity of Rani Maria, who organized the powerless even when the powerful came for her on a bus. Give us the clarity of John Chrysostom, who knew that the tabernacle and the beggar at the door are not two separate theological problems.

In West Bengal — in Murshidabad, in Purulia, in Malda, in the corridors of Dum Dum Jail, in the formation houses where the next generation of prophets is either being formed or being domesticated —

Let us be found on the right side. Not the comfortable side. The right side.

Amen.*

SPEAKER NOTES

Register: This is the culminating talk of Part I — the theological register remains that of Fr. Joseph Lobo SJ: structured argument, prophetically sober tone, historical precision. The pastoral warmth enters most fully in the Scriptural section and the closing prayer. The overall tenor of this talk should be the most urgent of the three — this is where the argument demands a personal response.

Key theological moves:

  1. The cultic model vs. prophetic model of religious presence — with the Constantinian settlement named as the historical turning point at which the balance shifted decisively.
  2. Prophētēs recovered etymologically from adjective/noun (institutional self-description) to verb (living, costly, structurally embodied orientation) — with Heschel's concept of divine pathos as the theological engine.
  3. The six conflicts of Jesus as a structural map of prophetic witness — not hagiographical data but a theological framework for what following Jesus actually entails institutionally and personally.
  4. The four structural suppressors — informed silence, institutionalization, internal domination, individualism/disposability — named in theological register rather than merely as cultural observations.
  5. The prophetic cost: from the modern martyrs (Romero, Rani Maria, Kandhamal) to the daily, unglamorous prophetic wager — making the connection between extraordinary witness and ordinary fidelity.

Secular and theological thinkers deployed: - Karl Rahner: prophetic diagnosis of institutional mediocrity - Walter Brueggemann: criticizing and energizing as constitutive prophetic functions - Abraham Heschel: divine pathos as theomorphic in the prophet - Dietrich Bonhoeffer: refusal of the powerful's framing as prophetic act - Henri Nouwen: the wound of abandonment in prophetic life - Edward Schillebeeckx: hardening of charism (from previous reflections, applied here) - Albert Einstein: the mechanism of informed silence (secular complement to theological complicity)

Church documents cited: - Lumen Gentium 44 (consecrated persons as signa) - Perfectae Caritatis 2 (return to founding sources) - Evangelii Gaudium 20, 53 (prophetic going out; throwaway culture) - Ad Gentes 5 (prophetic vocation as participation in the mission of Jesus) - Pope Francis, address to Union of Superiors General, November 2013

Pacing: - The Opening should be delivered with the same unhurried precision as the previous two talks — but with slightly greater urgency in tone. This is the culmination. - Part I (Two Models) — move with deliberate precision. The sixth conflict (Matthew 27:46) requires the most care — it is the most personally searching. - Part IV (Suppressors and Cost) — the most confrontational section. The diagnostic tone must be maintained throughout: "this is what is happening" — not "this is what you are doing." - The modern martyrs must be named slowly, reverently, with genuine pause between each name. - The silence after the closing demand is non-negotiable.
