Matthew 25:36 is not an ambiguous text. Jesus does not say that he was with the poor in a general sense, awaiting our generalized response. He names specific places and specific conditions. Hungry. Thirsty. A stranger. Naked. Sick. And: in prison.

Six conditions. The Church has built substantial institutions to address five of them. Food programmes, water wells, refugee assistance, clothing drives, hospitals, hospices. The record on the sixth is, to put it with precision, thin.

Not non-existent. Prison Ministry India, recognized by the Catholic Bishops' Conference, does extraordinary work. Sr. Inigo SSA spent fourteen years inside Tihar. Individual priests, sisters, and laypeople have given years to this ministry. The work exists.

But it exists at the margins of our institutional priorities, our formation programmes, and our collective pastoral consciousness. Most seminarians in India complete their entire theological education without ever entering a prison. Most parishes have no awareness that Prison Ministry Sunday exists, that it falls on the Sunday before 15 August, and that it has been approved by the CBCI since 2004.

The question this talk raises is not whether prison ministry is good. Of course it is good. The question is structural. Why has the Church, which so readily quotes Matthew 25, so consistently left verse 36 unimplemented?

In one sentence, if I were to state the thesis of this talk: the Church's absence from the prison is not primarily a strategic failure. It is a theological one. It reveals a Christology that has quietly decided where Christ is and is not present, and has drawn that boundary at the prison gate.

Let us examine that boundary.

Part 1 | 8 Minutes Two Theologies of the Imprisoned

There are two operative theologies of the imprisoned person at work in our communities and our society. Neither is usually stated explicitly. Both produce entirely observable pastoral and institutional consequences.

Model A: The Prison as the Space of the Irredeemable

The first theology begins from a premise that is rarely articulated but widely practised: the person who is in prison has forfeited, by their choices, a portion of their claim on our pastoral attention and our institutional resources.

This theology has several characteristics. It is individualist: it locates the cause of imprisonment entirely in the choices of the individual, ignoring the structural conditions, the poverty, the caste location, the lack of legal representation, the circumstances that shape those choices. It is consequentialist: the prison is deserved, and therefore our pastoral obligation is proportionally reduced. It is comfortable: it allows us to feel that justice has been served and that our energy is better directed toward those whose suffering is more clearly innocent.

This theology rarely identifies itself as theology. It presents itself as common sense. As realism. As the necessary prioritization of limited pastoral resources.

But its consequences are precisely observable. When 60 percent of the inmates in Indian prisons are under-trial prisoners, people who have not been convicted of any offence, who are awaiting a hearing that may not come for years, and we still do not go, then we have allowed common sense to substitute for the Gospel.

When the Dalit youth of Birbhum or the Adivasi family from the tribal belt of Jharkhand, who had neither money nor connections for legal representation, sit in the Dum Dum Correctional Home awaiting a trial that their counterparts with resources would never have faced, and we describe this as justice receiving its due, then we have confused the existing social order with the order of the Kingdom.

Model B: The Prison as a Locus Theologicus

The second theology begins from a very different premise. It begins from Matthew 25. Not as a metaphor, not as an encouragement toward vague solidarity, but as a Christological claim. Christ is in the prison. Not metaphorically. Not in the general sense that God is everywhere. But in the specific sense that the encounter with the imprisoned person is an encounter with Christ.

The tradition has a technical term for this: locus theologicus. A place of theological encounter. A place where God is disclosed. The tradition identifies the poor, the suffering, and the marginalized as loci theologici not because God is absent elsewhere but because the encounter with human vulnerability has a particular capacity to disclose who God is and what the Gospel demands.

The locus theologicus of Matthew 25 is not the comfortable, the respectable, or the already-converted. It is the prisoner. The stranger. The naked. The sick. These are the places where God invites encounter precisely because they are the places from which the respectable have withdrawn.

The consequences of Model B are also observable, where it has been practised. Sr. Inigo SSA, in fourteen years inside Tihar, did not find people who were beyond redemption. She found Raju, who built a library. Sudhir, who learned computer skills. Dilip, who passed his Class X examinations from his cell. Ajay, who discovered he was a painter. Antony, who said: "Outsiders think we are lost. No. We have found ourselves here. Rather, Jesus found us."

Jesus found us. In prison. Which is exactly where Matthew 25 said he would be.

Part 2 | 7 Minutes Etymological Recovery: Three Words to Reclaim

Let me attend to three words that are central to this ministry and have each been domesticated in ways that need to be reversed. The method here is the same one I applied in our last talk on mercy: returning a tamed noun to its original verbal force.

First Word: Solidaritas

We speak of solidarity with the poor. The word appears in Catholic social teaching with such frequency that it has become, in Brueggemann's phrase, a tamed noun: something we affirm in principle without it generating any specific cost.

Solidaritas comes from the Latin solidus, which means solid, whole, complete, unbroken. It was also the name of a Roman gold coin, something of reliable, tested, uncompromised value.

Solidarity, therefore, is not sympathy from a distance. It is not the expression of concern, however genuine, from a position of security. Solidarity is the commitment of one's whole self, one's solidus, one's tested completeness, to another person's situation. It is what is expressed when John XXIII walked into a Roman prison and said: "I, John, your brother. I have come to see you because you cannot come to see me." Not: I have come to help you. Not: I have come to minister to you. "I am your brother." Complete identification. Total.

That is solidarity as solidaritas. Uncompromised. Whole.

Second Word: Captivus

The word captive, captivus in Latin, comes from capere, to take, to seize. The captive is one who has been seized and held. But in the theological imagination of the Hebrew and Christian scriptures, the captive is precisely the one whom God acts to release.

The Exodus narrative is, at its structural core, a story of captivity and liberation. The whole movement of the Passover, from Egypt to Sinai, is God's response to what Exodus 3:7-9 describes as God having seen, heard, and known the suffering of the people in captivity: "I have heard their cry on account of their taskmasters. Indeed I know their sufferings, and I have come down to deliver them."

God's response to captivity, in the foundational narrative of Israel's faith, is not passive sympathy. It is active, costly, downward movement. God comes down. God enters the place of captivity. God takes the side of the seized.

Isaiah 61:1-2, the text that Jesus reads in the Nazareth synagogue (Luke 4:18), carries the same movement. The anointed one is sent specifically to the captives, the prisoners in darkness. Not to the already-free. To the captives. This is not incidental to the mission. It is named first.

Third Word: Visitatio

The word visit, visitare in Latin, comes from visere, to go and see, to inspect, to attend to with care. We use the word visit casually: visiting a friend, visiting a city. But in its biblical and theological usage, the word carries enormous weight.

The visitation of Mary to Elizabeth (Luke 1:39-56) is not a social call. It is an act of presence at a moment of vulnerability. Elizabeth is old, pregnant unexpectedly, in need of support. Mary goes to her. She enters Elizabeth's space. She stays. The encounter produces the Magnificat, the Church's great hymn of liberation for the poor.

In Matthew 25:36, when Jesus says "you visited me," the word used in the Greek is epeskepsasthe, from episkeptomai, to look upon, to attend to, to care for. This is the same root from which we get episkopos, bishop, the one who looks over the flock with care. Every act of visitation in the New Testament is, in miniature, an episcopal act: an act of attending, with personal presence, to someone in vulnerability.

Part 3 | 10 Minutes The Scriptural Constellation

Let me now read several scriptural texts together, each adding a dimension the others do not contain. Prison is not a marginal theme in the Bible. It runs through both Testaments as one of the primary sites of God's action.

Text 1 — Isaiah 61:1-2 and Luke 4:18-19: The First Named Priority

When Jesus stands in the synagogue at Nazareth and reads from the scroll of Isaiah, he is not selecting a text randomly. He is declaring his mission. The Spirit of the Lord is upon me. He has anointed me to bring good news to the poor. He has sent me to proclaim release to the captives and recovery of sight to the blind, to let the oppressed go free, to proclaim the year of the Lord's favour (Luke 4:18-19).

The year of the Lord's favour is the Jubilee: the year of systemic reset, when debts are cancelled, prisoners are released, land is returned, and the structural distortions of accumulated inequality are undone. Jesus proclaims the Jubilee not as a future hope but as a present reality. "Today this scripture has been fulfilled in your hearing" (Luke 4:21).

Notice what is named first among the specific beneficiaries: the captives. Not the poor in general, though they follow immediately. The captives. The imprisoned. The held. They are the first specific group Jesus names as the target of his anointing.

If this is the first word of Jesus's mission statement, it cannot be the last priority of the Church's pastoral agenda.

Text 2 — Matthew 25:34-40: The Judgment Criterion

The scene in Matthew 25 is eschatological: the final judgment. And what is the criterion by which the nations are judged? Not orthodoxy of doctrine. Not regularity of liturgical practice. Not institutional achievement. The criterion is entirely relational: did you attend to the specific vulnerability of the specific person in front of you?

And the list of specific vulnerabilities, read carefully, is a list of people whom the respectable have good reasons for avoiding. The hungry inconvenience your schedule. The stranger makes you culturally uncomfortable. The naked embarrasses you. The sick repel you. The prisoner carries the weight of moral judgment.

Each item on the list is someone the righteous have a plausible reason to pass by. The genius of the text is that Jesus identifies himself specifically with the people most likely to be passed by.

"Lord, when did we see you hungry and feed you? When were you a stranger and we welcomed you?" (Matthew 25:37-38). The righteous are genuinely surprised. They did not know they were serving Christ. They were simply serving the person in front of them.

This is theologically precise. The encounter with the imprisoned person does not require theological sophistication. It requires presence. It requires showing up. And it constitutes, according to Jesus, the encounter with Christ himself.

Text 3 — Acts 16:22-34: Paul's Midnight in Philippi

Paul and Silas are in prison at Philippi. They have been beaten. Their feet are in stocks. It is midnight. And then the text records something remarkable: "About midnight Paul and Silas were praying and singing hymns to God, and the prisoners were listening to them" (Acts 16:25).

They are singing. In prison. At midnight. With bloody backs and bound feet.

The earthquake comes. The doors open. The stocks fall. And the jailer, waking in terror, draws his sword to kill himself, believing the prisoners have escaped. And Paul calls out from the darkness: "Do not harm yourself, for we are all here" (Acts 16:28).

They stayed. They could have fled. They stayed. And the jailer, whose entire professional and social identity was bound to keeping them imprisoned, falls trembling before them and asks: "What must I do to be saved?" (Acts 16:30).

The ministry in prison, in Acts 16, converts not only the imprisoned but the jailer. The one who comes to serve discovers that the encounter transforms the servant as much as the served.

Sr. Inigo discovered the same truth in Tihar. She did not go to transform the prisoners. She went to be with them. And she was transformed.

Text 4 — Luke 23:39-43: Paradise Promised on a Cross

Two criminals are crucified alongside Jesus. One mocks. One asks. And to the one who asks, Jesus says: "Truly I tell you, today you will be with me in paradise" (Luke 23:43).

This man has committed real crimes. He says so himself: "We are getting what we deserve for our deeds" (Luke 23:41). There is no suggestion of innocence, no legal exoneration, no programme of rehabilitation completed. There is simply the turning of one human being toward another in the final extremity, and the response of Jesus is immediate, total, and unconditional.

Today. Paradise. Without conditions.

This is the theology of the prison ministry in its most concentrated form. The person who is most fully condemned, most fully outside every system of institutional support, most fully without social standing or future, is the precise person to whom Jesus makes the most complete promise in the entire Gospel.

Text 5 — Hebrews 13:3: A Direct Apostolic Command

Hebrews 13:3 is one of the most direct commands in the New Testament regarding the imprisoned: "Remember those who are in prison as though you were in prison with them; those who are being tortured, as though you yourselves were being tortured."

As though you were in prison with them. Not: pray for those in prison. Not: support charitable organizations that work with the imprisoned. Be with them in your imagination with sufficient vividness that your comfort becomes their discomfort and your freedom becomes their captivity. Let the boundary between their situation and yours become permeable.

This is not a counsel for those with a particular charism. This is addressed to the whole community of Hebrews. It is a communal obligation, not a specialist vocation.

Part 4 | 8 Minutes The Historical Diagnosis: How the Church Left the Prison

Let me now name the historical process by which the Church moved from robust solidarity with the imprisoned to its current position of marginal charitable concern. Naming this process is essential, because it reveals that our current situation is historically contingent, not theologically necessary. It can be reversed.

The Early Church: Visiting the Imprisoned as Standard Practice

The Acts of the Apostles does not treat imprisonment as exceptional. It records multiple imprisonments of apostles and disciples: Peter, Paul, Silas, John. And in each case, the community gathers in prayer, the imprisoned continue their ministry from within the prison, and the encounter with imprisonment becomes a site of evangelization.

The Didache, one of the earliest non-canonical Christian texts from approximately the late first or early second century, lists visiting prisoners among the standard obligations of the Christian community. The North African tertullian, writing in the late second century, describes Christians visiting prisons as a matter of course, bringing food, clothing, and support.

The early Church did not debate whether to go to the prisons. It went.

The Constantinian Shift: From Solidarity to Alignment

The shift begins in the fourth century with the Constantinian settlement. As the Church moved from a persecuted minority to the religion of empire, its relationship to the instruments of state power changed fundamentally. The Church went from being the community of those whom the empire imprisoned to being, in significant respects, an ally of the imperial structures that managed imprisonment.

The philosopher Michel Foucault, in his analysis of the prison in Discipline and Punish (1975), argues that the modern prison is not primarily a site of rehabilitation. It is a mechanism of social control: the power of the state to discipline, to normalize, to manage bodies. The Church, he suggests, provided the template for many of these disciplinary mechanisms in its monastic traditions and penitential systems (Foucault, Discipline and Punish, 1975, Part III).

Whether or not one accepts Foucault's full argument, his diagnosis of the prison as a mechanism of power rather than a site of rehabilitation illuminates something real about why the Church, once aligned with power, found it increasingly difficult to stand in solidarity with those over whom power was exercised.

The Indian Context: Colonial Inheritance and Caste

In India, this historical problem is compounded by two additional factors.

The first is the colonial inheritance. The prison system that exists in West Bengal today is substantially the system built by the British colonial government, designed not for the rehabilitation of offenders but for the deterrence of resistance and the management of a subject population. The Prisons Act of 1894, still partially operative in various state statutes, was written for a colonial context. The Church received this system as it was and, with notable exceptions, did not significantly critique it.

The second is caste. Research by the National Law University Delhi and the Human Rights Commission consistently shows that Dalit and Adivasi communities are vastly overrepresented in Indian prisons relative to their share of the population. In West Bengal, the pattern holds: the Santali youth from Birbhum, the Bauri family from Bankura, the Rajbongshi labourer from Cooch Behar, enter the correctional system at rates that far exceed their proportion of the general population, and exit it years later without adequate legal support, having been educated, in Sr. Inigo's phrase, in criminality rather than reformed from it.

Nelson Mandela, who spent twenty-seven years in prison on Robben Island, said: "A nation is not known by how it treats its highest citizens, but by how it treats its prisoners." The same standard applies to the Church. We are not known by the quality of our liturgy in the cathedral, though that matters. We are known by whether we go to Dum Dum.

The question is not whether our liturgy is beautiful. It is whether we have heard, in the cry of Psalm 142:7, the voice of Christ: "Lead me out of prison, that I may praise your name."

Part 5 | 5 Minutes The Prophetic Demand: What This Requires of Us

I want to be precise about what this talk is asking of us. It is not asking every priest, sister, or seminarian in this room to become a full-time prison chaplain. That is a specific vocation, and it requires specific gifts, specific training, and specific institutional support.

What this talk is asking is something both simpler and more serious. It is asking a change not primarily of activity but of imagination. Of the theology that governs our sense of where Christ is.

First: A Theological Conversion

The starting point is not a programme. It is a conviction. The conviction that Christ is already in Dum Dum, already in Presidency, already in Berhampore and Jalpaiguri, ahead of us, waiting. Not waiting for our programmatic response. Waiting for our presence.

Pope Francis, in Evangelii Gaudium 186, describes the preferential option for the poor not as one among several pastoral priorities but as the primary lens through which the whole of the Church's mission must be seen. "Each individual Christian and every community is called to be an instrument of God for the liberation and promotion of the poor" (EG 187). The imprisoned are the poor in their most concentrated and abandoned form. They cannot come to us. They cannot attend our retreats. They cannot receive the pastoral services we offer to the free.

If we wait for them to come to us, we have reversed the direction of the Incarnation.

Second: Concrete Institutional Steps

At the institutional level, there are specific, achievable steps.

Prison Ministry India, the CBCI-recognized organization, provides trained volunteers, established permissions, and organized access to correctional facilities. Connection with PMI is not complicated. It requires a decision, not a charism.

Prison Ministry Sunday, approved by the CBCI and observed on the Sunday before 15 August each year, is an opportunity for every parish and seminary in West Bengal to make the imprisoned visible in the consciousness of the community. When was it last observed in your parish? When was it last mentioned in your seminary?

Benedict XVI, in Deus Caritas Est 28, makes a point that is directly applicable here: the Church's charitable activity must never be reduced to ideology or to sentiment. "Charity," he writes, "is love received and given. It must... respond to the immediate need" (DCE 28). The immediate need of the undertrial in Dum Dum is legal representation, family contact, and human presence. These are concrete, achievable forms of charitable response.

Third: The Spirituality of Maximilian Kolbe

The patron saint of Prison Ministry India is St. Maximilian Kolbe, who died in Auschwitz in 1941 not because he was condemned but because he stepped forward when another prisoner was condemned and said: Take me instead.

He took the place of another. He entered the starvation cell in the place of a man who had a family, who begged for his life and was denied. Kolbe volunteered. He went in.

This is the spirituality of prison ministry in its most radical form: not managing the imprisoned from outside the gate, but entering their space, sharing their vulnerability, choosing proximity over safety. Gaudium et Spes 27 states that human dignity must be respected in everyone without exception: whatever is opposed to life itself, whatever violates the integrity of the human person, must be overcome.

The violation of human dignity that occurs in the under-trial's five-year wait for a hearing that never comes is not an abstraction. It is a specific person in a specific cell who has been forgotten by the system and has not been visited by the Church.

Until and unless the Church's relationship to the imprisoned shifts from charitable distance to incarnational solidarity, from visiting the prison as an occasional programme to entering it as a brother and sister, Matthew 25:36 remains a text we quote and a judgment we have not yet faced.

The choice before each of us is precise. Christ is in the prison. He said so. He was there before Sr. Inigo arrived. He was there before Kiran Bedi introduced Vipassana. He is there now, in Dum Dum and Presidency and Berhampore and Jalpaiguri, in every cell where a human being sits, forgotten and afraid.

The question Matthew 25 asks is simple, and it has only two answers.

Did you come?

Personal Reflection | 2 Minutes Silence One Question

I want to place before you a single question. Not a general one about prison ministry as a concept. A specific one about your life.

Is there a person, known to you or once known to you, who is in some form of imprisonment, physical, social, familial, systemic, from whom you have withdrawn your presence because their situation makes you uncomfortable, or because you do not know what to do, or because you have privately concluded that their circumstances are, in some measure, their fault?

Sit with that.

[Allow two minutes of silence]

Closing Prayer | 3 Minutes

Lord Jesus,

You were executed between two criminals. You called yourself captive in Matthew 25. You promised paradise to a man being justly executed and asked for nothing in return. You proclaimed, at the beginning of your public ministry, that the Spirit had anointed you specifically for the captives.

We confess that we have visited you in the tabernacle and preferred you there. We have found you in the beauty of the liturgy and been content with that encounter. We have quoted Matthew 25 and then crossed to the other side of the road when the road led to Dum Dum.

Give us the feet of John XXIII, who walked into a prison and said: I am your brother. Give us the eyes of Sr. Inigo, who looked at Shekhar's wall of pain and refused to retreat from it. Give us the courage of Maximilian Kolbe, who walked into the starvation cell and transformed it into a place of praise.

For the undertrial who has waited five years in Dum Dum for a hearing that has not come. For the Santali youth from Birbhum who had no lawyer and no family to advocate for him. For the man in Presidency Correction Home who has not been visited since his mother stopped coming, who has concluded, as Ranga concluded on his last morning, that he has no one in the world.

Let us be the answer to Psalm 142. Let us be the ones through whom the captive is led out to praise your name.

In Dum Dum, in Presidency, in Berhampore, in Jalpaiguri: let your Church go in. Let your Church be found there, ahead of the agenda, ahead of the programme, ahead of the institution, already sitting with the one who has been told by everyone that they have no one left.

Because you are already there. You told us.

Amen.

Growing Series Arc — Seven Talks

Part III continues. Having built the foundations of identity, spirituality, and mercy in Part II, we now walk into the specific territories where that service becomes concrete, costly, and recognizable as belonging to Jesus of Nazareth. Talk 8 will move to the next Chapter 8 of Sr. Inigo's text.

Morning Star Regional Seminary | Barrackpore, West Bengal

Fr. William Anthony MSFS | Talk 7: Carcere et Vincula

Talk 8 — The Inviolability of Life: A Theological Case Against Capital Punishment

"I have come that they may have life — life in all its fullness." (John 10:10)

"You have no power over me unless it is given to you." (John 19:11)

⏱ TIME GUIDE

Section Duration
Opening: The Problematic of State Execution 7 min
Part I — Two Theologies of Punishment: Retributive and Restorative 10 min
Part II — Etymological Recovery: What Imago Dei Actually Claims 8 min
Part III — The Scriptural Constellation 9 min
Part IV — The Historical Diagnosis and Its Indian Consequence 8 min
Prophetic Demand & Silence 3 min

OPENING: THE PROBLEMATIC OF STATE EXECUTION

(7 minutes)

By way of introducing this eighth reflection in our series, let me begin with a theological affirmation of the most fundamental kind — and then with a reality that contradicts it in ways the Church has taken far too long to name with the clarity the Gospel demands.

We say: every human being is made in the image of God — the imago Dei of Genesis 1:27. This is the foundational anthropological claim of the entire Judaeo-Christian tradition. It is not qualified by behavior, by criminal record, by the severity of the offense committed, or by the judgment of any human tribunal. The imago Dei is ontological — it belongs to the structure of the human person as created and loved by God, prior to and independent of everything the person does or fails to do. And Jesus, in John 10:10, states his entire mission in terms of this anthropological conviction: "I came that they may have life, and have it in fullness." Not some of them. Not the ones who deserve it. All of them. In fullness.

That is the claim.

And now the reality.

On the morning of March 20, 2020 — three days before India entered its first COVID lockdown — four men were executed by hanging in Tihar Jail. The Nirbhaya case. A crime of exceptional brutality, a judicial process of seven years, and a sentence carried out as the country held its breath. The public response, across social media, in television studios, in ordinary living rooms, was celebration. People who would otherwise describe themselves as compassionate, as reform-minded, as followers of a Gospel of mercy — celebrated.

I am not here to defend the crime. What was done to that young woman was monstrous. No serious person argues otherwise. I am here to ask the question that the celebration obscured: does the monstrousness of a crime confer upon the state the authority to take the life of the person who committed it? And I am here to note that the Church — which follows a man who was himself executed by the state, legally, officially, with the full procedure of Roman judicial authority — has now answered that question with formal doctrinal clarity.

Pope Francis, incorporating into the Catechism of the Catholic Church the development of doctrine articulated in paragraph 2267 (revised 2018), said: "The death penalty is inadmissible because it is an attack on the inviolability and dignity of the person. The Church works with determination for its abolition worldwide."

Not "the Church has reservations." Not "the Church encourages pastoral discernment." Inadmissible. The Church works with determination for its abolition worldwide. That is a doctrinal position, formally incorporated into the Catechism. And it has been, in the formation houses and pulpits of India, almost entirely unpreached.

The gap between that doctrinal clarity and its absence from our pastoral practice is the problematic of this reflection.

In one sentence, if I were to state the thesis: The Church's opposition to capital punishment is not a political position contingent on social context but a theological conviction flowing directly from the imago Dei, from the Christological claim of John 10:10, and from the ecclesiological self-understanding of a community that follows a God who was executed by the state and raised to vindicate every human being the world has discarded — and the consecrated person's silence on this matter is not neutrality but a form of complicity.

Let me develop this across four movements.

PART I — TWO THEOLOGIES OF PUNISHMENT: RETRIBUTIVE AND RESTORATIVE

(10 minutes)

There are two operative theologies of punishment that have coexisted, in tension, throughout the history of Christian civilization — and indeed throughout the history of human legal thought. Their conflict is not merely juridical. It is theological. And the position one takes on capital punishment is, ultimately, determined by which theology of punishment one inhabits.

I shall call them the retributive theology and the restorative theology of punishment.

The retributive theology understands the purpose of punishment as the proportionate return of harm for harm. In this model, justice is fundamentally a matter of equivalence: the criminal has taken something of value — life, property, security, dignity — and justice requires that something of equivalent value be taken from the criminal in return. The scales of justice are the symbol of this theology: balance, equivalence, reciprocity. The ultimate logic of retributive theology, when applied consistently, is capital punishment: you have taken a life; therefore your life is taken. The equation is resolved. Justice is served.

The retributive theology has deep roots in human legal tradition. It has received theological articulation from serious thinkers. And it responds to a genuine moral intuition: that wrongdoing has consequences, that there is a proper proportion between offense and response, and that the failure to hold offenders accountable is itself a form of injustice.

But the retributive theology, I want to argue, contains a structural theological error that the Christian tradition must name. The error is this: it locates the purpose of punishment entirely in the past — in the offense committed — and has no theological account of the future of the person being punished. The offender, in the retributive model, is defined entirely by what they have done. Their identity is the crime. And if the crime is sufficient, the identity is forfeit. The retributive theology, taken to its limit, is a theology of despair about the human person: it holds that some people are, finally and irreversibly, beyond the reach of the God who creates, redeems, and transforms.

This is the point Sr Inigo names with surgical precision: condemning someone to death is an act of despair. It is society saying — formally, legally, irrevocably — that this person has no future, that transformation is impossible, that the God who makes all things new has reached the limit of His capacity in this particular case.

The restorative theology of punishment understands its purpose entirely differently. Punishment, in this model, exists not to balance scales but to restore persons — to the community, to themselves, and to the God in whose image they were made. This theology does not minimize the offense. It is not naive about the severity of what has been done or the genuine need to protect the community from further harm. But it holds that the purpose of any response to wrongdoing must be oriented toward the future: toward the transformation of the offender, the healing of the victim, and the restoration of the social fabric that the crime has torn.

This is the theology consistent with the entire prophetic and Wisdom tradition of the Hebrew Scriptures. Ezekiel 18:23 — "Have I any pleasure in the death of the wicked, says the Lord God, and not rather that they should turn from their ways and live?" The question is rhetorical. God has no pleasure in the death of the wicked. God's purpose is always turning — metanoia, the complete reorientation of a life — and life. The restorative theology takes this divine orientation seriously and builds its account of punishment upon it.

And it is the theology consistent with the New Testament, where the model of response to wrongdoing is never the balance sheet but always the embrace. The father who ran toward the returning son before the son had finished his rehearsed speech of remorse. The Jesus who said to the woman caught in adultery — whose guilt was not in question, whose execution was legally mandated — "Neither do I condemn you. Go your way, and from now on do not sin again" (John 8:11). The Jesus who said, from the cross, to a convicted criminal who had no time left for rehabilitation: "Today you will be with me in paradise" (Luke 23:43).

The restorative theology is not soft on crime. It is hard on despair. It refuses the conclusion that any human person is finally beyond the reach of the God who breathed life into them in the first place.

PART II — ETYMOLOGICAL RECOVERY: WHAT IMAGO DEI ACTUALLY CLAIMS

(8 minutes)

Let me now perform the etymological recovery that this argument requires — returning a foundational theological phrase to its original and most demanding force.

The Latin imago Dei — image of God — translates the Hebrew tselem Elohim of Genesis 1:27: "God created humankind in his image; in the image of God he created them; male and female he created them." The Hebrew tselem means, literally, a sculptured figure, a representation — the kind of image that a king would erect in a conquered territory to assert his sovereignty and presence. In the ancient Near Eastern context, the tselem of the king was placed in territories the king could not physically occupy, as a sign that his authority extended there nonetheless.

When Genesis uses this term for the human person, the claim is breathtaking in its scope: every human being is a living tselem of God — a physical, embodied, particular, unrepeatable representation of the divine presence in the world. Not a symbol. Not a metaphor. A tselem — a sculptured figure bearing the king's own form, asserting the king's presence wherever it stands.

Now, what has happened to imago Dei in the dominant discourse of criminal justice — and, more disturbingly, in the implicit theological anthropology of communities that support capital punishment — is, once again, a conversion from a relational and unconditional claim to a conditional one. The imago Dei has been quietly rewritten as a property that can be lost — that is forfeited by sufficiently serious wrongdoing, revoked by criminal behavior, suspended by judicial sentence.

But the text of Genesis 1:27 contains no such qualification. It does not say: God created humankind in his image — unless they murder, unless they rape, unless they commit crimes of sufficient severity. The tselem does not depend on the behavior of the image. It depends on the act of the God who created it. And that act — the act of divine creation, of divine breathing-into, of divine loving into existence — is not revocable by human tribunal.

This is the precise theological claim that John 19:11 makes in the context of Jesus's own execution. Standing before Pilate — who holds the formal legal authority to condemn him — Jesus says: "You have no power over me unless it is given to you." This is not a claim about Jesus's particular divinity only. It is a claim about the nature of all human power over human life: it is derived, borrowed, delegated — and it is therefore accountable to the one who delegated it. The state's power to punish is real. But it is not absolute. And the imago Dei is the theological limit of that power: the state may not execute what God has created in God's own image, because in doing so it destroys not merely a human life but the tselem of the God who made it.

This is the theological ground of the doctrinal position incorporated into Catechism 2267: the death penalty is inadmissible — not merely impractical, not merely disproportionate in most circumstances, but inadmissible — because it violates the inviolability of the imago Dei.

PART III — THE SCRIPTURAL CONSTELLATION

(9 minutes)

I want to bring four scriptural texts into this argument — each adding a dimension the others do not supply, and together constituting a scriptural theology of the inviolability of human life.

The first witness: Genesis 1:27 with Genesis 9:6.

Genesis 1:27 establishes the imago Dei as the ground of human dignity. But Genesis 9:6 — the text most frequently cited in favour of capital punishment — requires careful attention: "Whoever sheds the blood of a human, by a human shall that person's blood be shed; for in his own image God made humankind."

The irony of this text is that it is deployed as a justification for capital punishment when its explicit theological rationale is the imago Dei — the very principle that, properly understood, argues against it. The text is saying: the reason for the gravity of taking human life is that every human being bears the divine image. The imago Dei is the reason murder is an offense of the highest order. And the Church's development of doctrine asks precisely this: if the imago Dei is the reason murder is so grave, how can the imago Dei be destroyed in the act of executing the murderer? You cannot simultaneously claim that human life is sacred because it bears the divine image — and then destroy that image in the name of justice.

The second witness: Ezekiel 18:23.

"Have I any pleasure in the death of the wicked, says the Lord God, and not rather that they should turn from their ways and live?"

This text is the most direct scriptural statement of the restorative theology of divine response to wrongdoing. God's pleasure — the theological term for what God desires, what God is oriented toward — is not the death of the wicked but their metanoia and their life. This is not a sentiment. It is a theological claim about the telos of divine justice: its goal is always the restoration of the person to the community of the living, not their elimination from it. Any human system of justice that takes as its final act the elimination of the person has diverged, at that point, from the telos of divine justice as Ezekiel names it.

The third witness: John 8:2-11.

The woman caught in adultery. The legal case is clear. The Torah's prescription is explicit — Leviticus 20:10 mandates death. The crowd has gathered. The stones are ready. And Jesus bends down and writes in the dust.

Much has been written about what he wrote. I want to draw attention to what he did not do. He did not negotiate with the crowd. He did not suggest a lighter sentence. He did not argue that the prescribed penalty was disproportionate. He drew the crowd's attention to the moral continuity between themselves and the woman before them — "Let anyone among you who is without sin be the first to throw a stone" — and then, when they had left, he declined to execute the law that every legal and religious authority in the room agreed applied to her.

"Neither do I condemn you." This is not a statement about the woman's innocence. It is a statement about the purpose of the encounter with Jesus: not condemnation but restoration, not the execution of the law but the invitation to a new life. And it is a statement that carries an implicit Christological claim: the one who has the right to judge, declines to do so in the direction of death, and redirects in the direction of life.

The fourth witness: John 10:10.

"I came that they may have life, and have it in fullness."

This is Jesus's own self-description of his mission. Not: I came to distinguish the deserving from the undeserving and distribute life accordingly. Not: I came to ensure that consequences are proportionate to offenses. I came that they may have life — in fullness. The zōē of John's Gospel — the life Jesus offers — is not merely biological survival. It is life in its complete theological dimension: restored relationship with God, with the community, with oneself. It is the life that Ezekiel's God desires for every wicked person who turns. It is the life that the thief on the cross received in his final moments. It is the life that the father celebrated when the prodigal returned.

Capital punishment is the formal, legal, institutional declaration that John 10:10 does not apply to this person. That this life — in fullness — is no longer a possibility for them. That the God who came to give life has, in this case, been overruled by the state.

The Church's doctrinal position, expressed in Catechism 2267, is simply the formal theological refusal of that declaration.

PART IV — THE HISTORICAL DIAGNOSIS AND ITS INDIAN CONSEQUENCE

(8 minutes)

The historical diagnosis I want to offer is this: the Church's toleration — and in some periods, active endorsement — of capital punishment was a consequence of the same post-Constantinian theological compromise I identified in our third reflection.

When the Church became the established religion of the Roman Empire in the fourth century, it progressively inherited the Empire's legal and penal logic — including the retributive logic of Roman law. Aquinas, following Augustine, argued that the state had the right to execute criminals for the common good, as a diseased limb is amputated from the body for the health of the whole. This position was not without theological seriousness. But it was a position formed within, and accommodating itself to, the logic of a state that the Church was no longer challenging from outside but inhabiting from within.

The development of doctrine that Pope Francis incorporated into Catechism 2267 is not a reversal of the tradition. It is, as the Catechism itself explains, a development: "an increasing awareness that the dignity of the person is not lost even after the commission of very serious crimes." The imago Dei was always there. The full force of its implications — that the dignity of the criminal is inviolable, that the state's power over human life is not absolute — was not fully drawn out until the twentieth and twenty-first centuries' deepening engagement with human rights theology.

Cardinal Avery Dulles, in his analysis of the death penalty question, acknowledged this development honestly: the tradition was not uniform, and the direction of serious theological reflection in the contemporary period has been consistently away from retributive justifications for capital punishment and toward the position now formally incorporated into the Catechism. This is not Francis imposing a personal opinion. It is the formal recognition of a development that the best theological reflection of the last century had been moving toward.

Now, the Indian consequence of this diagnosis must be stated in three registers.

First: the Indian Penal Code's provision for capital punishment is a colonial inheritance — written by Lord Macaulay's commission in 1860, imposed by the British on a colonized people, and subsequently abandoned by Britain itself in 1969. We are using a penal instrument that the colonizers found incompatible with a civilized society and removed from their own legal system — as if it were a native Indian value. The post-colonial consciousness that shaped independent India's self-understanding should have included, as a matter of intellectual consistency, the interrogation of this colonial legal inheritance. That it has not is itself a cultural and political phenomenon worth examining.

Second: the phenomenon of mob violence and lynching — which has become, in contemporary India, a disturbing feature of public life — is capital punishment without a court. It is the retributive logic enacted without the procedural restraints of due process, without evidence, without a defense, without the theoretical possibility of acquittal. A person is accused — of theft, of transporting a particular food item across a state border, of belonging to the wrong religious community — and killed by a crowd that has appointed itself judge, jury, and executioner. The crowd films it. Sometimes political figures praise it. The imago Dei is destroyed in public, in daylight, with collective enthusiasm.

The Church cannot be silent about this. The silence of religious communities in the face of this phenomenon — in a country where Christians are themselves among the targets of communal mob violence, in Manipur, in Odisha, in Kandhamal — is not merely a moral failure. It is a failure of theological nerve: the failure to apply, in the public sphere, a doctrine that has been formally incorporated into the Catechism.

Third: Sr Inigo's concept of the "hidden death penalty" requires the most serious attention from every formation community in this region. Life imprisonment under conditions of maximum security — solitary confinement, permanent incarceration without possibility of parole, the systematic removal of every reason to hope — is, as Pope Francis explicitly stated, structurally not different from the death penalty. It is death administered slowly. And the psychological literature on its effects is unambiguous: paranoia, severe depression, chronic deterioration of mental and physical health, and — frequently — suicide.

But the hidden death penalty does not operate only in prisons. It operates — and this is the question Sr Inigo asks every community to sit with — in religious houses where a person has been judged, written off, excluded from community life, deprived of recognition and relationship, crushed by the accumulated weight of institutional contempt. The novice who has been dismissed as unsuitable and treated accordingly for three years before the formal dismissal. The older member who has been rendered invisible by the community's indifference. The person whose mistake became their permanent identity in the community's collective memory.

These are not equivalent to judicial execution. But they participate in the same spiritual logic: the logic that says this person is done, has no future, is beyond redemption, is no longer worth the investment of attention and care. And that logic — wherever it operates — is a theological error. Because the God who said "I came that they may have life — in fullness" did not except the person in the cell, or the person in the back room of the convent, or the person in the seminary whose name the Rector has already mentally crossed off the list.

PROPHETIC DEMAND & SILENCE

(3 minutes)

Let me close with a demand — and then with the silence it requires.

There is a detail about the crucifixion that the Church has meditated on for two thousand years without drawing its full consequence. Jesus was executed by the state. Legally. Officially. With the procedural authority of Roman judicial power. The sentence was carried out correctly. And the sentence was wrong. The most famous execution in history was a judicial murder. And the one executed was the Son of God.

Which means that when we look at the cross — the symbol we wear, the image before which we pray, the sign beneath which we profess our vows — we are looking at what state execution did to God.

We follow a God who was killed by a government, buried in borrowed earth, and raised to vindicate every human being the world has discarded as beyond hope.

Until and unless consecrated persons in India are willing to preach, form, and advocate in accordance with Catechism 2267 — until the Church's formal doctrinal position on the inadmissibility of capital punishment is as present in our pastoral practice as our positions on matters far less central to the Gospel — our advocacy for human dignity will remain selective, and therefore finally incredible.

The question I leave with you is one:

In your ministry setting, what would it cost you — institutionally, socially, politically — to preach and act consistently with the Church's formal teaching that the death penalty is inadmissible and that every human person, without exception, bears the inviolable image of God? And are you willing to pay that cost?

(Sustained silence — minimum four minutes. Do not fill it.)

CLOSING PRAYER

God of the imago Dei* — You pressed Your own form into every human being You ever made. Not the deserving ones. Every one.

You breathed life into flesh and called it very good — and You have not revoked that breath, not even for the ones whose actions made us wish You had.

We confess that we have been slow to draw the full consequence of this. We have preached Your image in theory and stayed silent when it was destroyed in practice — by the rope, by the lethal injection, by the mob's fist, by the life sentence that removes every reason to hope, by the community's contempt that kills without a weapon.

Forgive us the silence that made us complicit.

Give us the courage of the doctrine You have placed in our hands: that every person in every cell still carries Your sculptured form. Still belongs to You. Still has a future that no court and no community has the authority to revoke.

Let us be a Church that protects life as you gave it — unconditionally, persistently, at whatever cost to our own comfort and acceptability — because the one we follow was himself executed by the state, and raised to vindicate everything the state declared finished.

Amen.*

SPEAKER NOTES

Register: Academic formation talk — the register of Fr. Joseph Lobo SJ. The theological argument must be rigorous and structurally coherent; the prophetic urgency must be unmistakable; the pastoral warmth enters in the scriptural section and the closing prayer. This is the talk with the most explicit political-doctrinal content in the series. The Lobo method of making the institutional Church's own documents do the prophetic work is particularly important here: the speaker is not expressing a personal liberal political opinion — they are presenting formally incorporated Catholic doctrine.

Key theological moves:

  1. The retributive vs. restorative theology of punishment — with the structural theological error of the retributive model named precisely: it has no account of the future of the person, which means it is, at its limit, a theology of despair about the human person.
  2. Imago Dei recovered etymologically — tselem as the sculptured figure asserting the king's presence, unconditional and irrevocable — showing that the imago Dei cannot be forfeited by criminal behavior without contradicting the very anthropological claim that makes murder an offense.
  3. The scriptural constellation: Genesis 1:27 with 9:6 (the imago Dei as the reason capital punishment is self-contradictory), Ezekiel 18:23 (divine telos as metanoia and life, not death), John 8:2-11 (Jesus's explicit refusal of legal execution in the direction of restoration), John 10:10 (the mission of Jesus as the fullness of life for all).
  4. The historical diagnosis: the post-Constantinian inheritance of Roman retributive legal logic; the colonial provenance of the Indian Penal Code's capital punishment provision; the development of doctrine in Catechism 2267 as a recovery, not a reversal.
  5. The three-fold Indian consequence: the colonial inheritance of the IPC, mob violence and lynching as capital punishment without procedure, and the "hidden death penalty" within religious communities themselves.

Church documents cited precisely: - Catechism of the Catholic Church 2267 (revised 2018): "inadmissible... attack on the inviolability and dignity of the person" - Pope Francis: "The death penalty is inadmissible... The Church works with determination for its abolition worldwide" - Pope Francis on life imprisonment: formally stated alongside the death penalty as structurally problematic

Secular and theological thinkers deployed: - Cardinal Avery Dulles: honest acknowledgment of the tradition's non-uniformity and the direction of contemporary theological reflection (theological complement) - Thomas Aquinas/Augustine: the tradition's prior position stated fairly before the development of doctrine is introduced — consistent with Lobo's practice of never caricaturing the position being critiqued - Mahatma Gandhi: "An eye for an eye makes the whole world blind" — the retributive logic as the logic of the vendetta (Indian contextual complement)

Pacing: - The Opening must establish both the doctrinal clarity (Catechism 2267) and the pastoral gap (the fact that it has been almost entirely unpreached) before the thesis is stated. - Part I (Two Theologies) — the retributive model must be treated fairly before its structural error is named. The Lobo method never caricatures the position being critiqued. - Part II (Etymological Recovery) — tselem as sculptured figure is the most theologically generative move in the talk. The argument that you cannot simultaneously ground the gravity of murder in the imago Dei and then destroy the imago Dei in the act of executing the murderer needs to be given time to land. - Part III (Scriptural Constellation) — the Genesis 9:6 reading (the text most cited for capital punishment turned against it by its own theological rationale) is the sharpest exegetical moment. Do not rush it. - Part IV (Indian Consequence) — the three registers must be stated with controlled sobriety. The hidden death penalty within religious communities is the most personally searching moment of the talk. Name it without hedging. - The closing paragraph about the cross as state execution is the emotional and theological culmination. Slow down. Let it land completely before the demand. - Four minutes of silence minimum after the demand.

Complete series arc to this point:

Talk Chapter Theological Movement Central Etymological Move
Talk 1 Ch. 1 Inward — Mission Consecrare as verb
Talk 2 Ch. 2 Downward — Identity Charisma as verb
Talk 3 Ch. 3 Forward — Courage Prophētēs as verb
Talk 4 Ch. 4 Outward — Creation Ktisis as relational term
Talk 5 Ch. 5 Through — Communication Logos as flesh, not content
Talk 6 Ch. 6 Toward — Mercy (from previous reflection)
Talk 7 Ch. 7 Into — Solidarity (from previous reflection)
Talk 8 Ch. 8 Against — Dignity Imago Dei / Tselem as unconditional and irrevocable

The etymological arc of the series: every key word has been recovered from institutional noun back to living, demanding, costly verb or relational claim. Consecrare, charisma, prophētēs, ktisis, logos — and now tselem: the sculptured image of God that no court and no community has the authority to revoke.

Talk 9 — Signum Vitae: Signpost or Scenery?

Prophetic Clarity as a Form of Consecrated Life

45-Minute Recollection for Priests, Religious, and Major Seminarians — West Bengal Region

Primary Anchor — Deuteronomy 30:19

"I have set before you life and death. Choose life."

Secondary Anchor — John 10:10

"I have come that they may have life — life in all its fullness."

Time Guide

For the Speaker

Register: Lobo Register 3 (Formation Talk). The conceptual pair in Part 1 (signpost versus scenery) is the structural spine of the entire talk. Return to it at every transition. The etymological recovery in Part 2 is intellectually the heart. The historical diagnosis in Part 4 is the most personally challenging section for a religious audience and must not be softened. The prophetic demand in Part 5 should feel like relief after the weight of Part 4, not a further burden.

Tone: Prophetically urgent throughout. The canvas of this talk is deliberately the widest in Part III. Resist the temptation to soften the diagnosis of caste, displacement, and institutional indifference. The audience is capable of receiving the truth. Give it to them at full strength.

Key Biblical Texts: Dt 30:15-19; Jn 10:9-10; Gen 1:26-27; Is 10:1-2; Am 5:21-24; Mic 6:6-8; Mt 25:40; Lk 4:18-19.

Key Documents: Gaudium et Spes 1, 27, 29; Evangelii Gaudium 187-188; Vita Consecrata 73, 84; Laudato Si 49; Sollicitudo Rei Socialis 36-38 (John Paul II); Centesimus Annus 11; Catechism 1928-1942 (social justice).

Key Thinkers: Walter Brueggemann (The Prophetic Imagination, 2001); B.R. Ambedkar (Annihilation of Caste, 1936); Gustavo Gutierrez (A Theology of Liberation, 1971); Hannah Arendt; Mahatma Gandhi; St. Irenaeus; Socrates; Sr. Inigo SSA.

West Bengal Specifics: Murshidabad bonded labour and caste discrimination; Birbhum Dalit land and wage disputes; North Bengal tea garden workers (Jalpaiguri, Darjeeling foothills); Santali communities of the Rarh region; adivasi land displacement linked to Jharkhand corridor; Kolkata peri-urban substance abuse among youth; manual scavenging still reported in parts of West Bengal.

Opening | 5 Minutes The Problematic

By way of beginning, I want to place before you a contradiction that this talk will attempt to name with precision.

We say that we have consecrated our lives to the mission of Jesus. And Jesus stated that mission with unambiguous clarity in Luke 4:18: the Spirit has anointed him to bring good news to the poor, to proclaim liberty to the captives, to set free the oppressed. We repeat this text. We teach it. We embed it in our congregational mission statements and our pastoral plans.

We say, further, that God has established an absolute standard by which human life is to be measured and promoted. Genesis 1:26-27 is unequivocal: both male and female are made in the image of God, without qualification, without hierarchy, without condition attached to caste or class or tribal identity. Every human life, by this standard, carries a dignity that is not conferred by society and cannot be revoked by it.

And we say, thirdly, that our consecrated life positions us in a specific relationship to the world: not as its administrators, not as its managers, but as its prophets. Vita Consecrata 84 calls the consecrated person to be a prophetic witness, a sign of contradiction against every structure that diminishes human dignity.

We say all of this.

And then the following things occur, without dramatic intention, without conscious reversal of conviction, simply by the accumulated weight of institutional routine.

The Dalit tea garden worker in the Jalpaiguri foothills who has not received a living wage in twelve years is within thirty kilometers of our institutions. We pass the tea gardens on the road. We do not stop. We have a programme to attend.

The Santali youth of the Rarh region whose ancestral land has been acquired for a development project, who has received no compensation and no legal assistance, speaks to no one in our network because there is no one in our network who speaks Santali or understands the land rights framework.

The girl in peri-urban Kolkata who is being trafficked by someone she trusted is passed over by an institutional Church that has robust programmes for the already-safe and thin capacity for the acutely endangered.

The Dalit woman in Murshidabad who is still, in the twenty-first century, carrying human waste on her head, has never once been visited by a religious congregation that professes the dignity of the human person made in the image of God.

This gap produces three specific consequences.

First: the Church's prophetic voice on life issues becomes selective in a way that loses credibility. We speak with authority about the unborn but with relative silence about the Dalit woman alive in Murshidabad whose conditions of life constitute, by any theological standard, a systemic assault on God's image in her.

Second: our institutions, built for the service of the poor, gradually and without announcement recalibrate toward the manageable middle. The school that was founded for Dalit children now serves primarily the lower-middle class because they pay fees and the Dalit children cannot. This is not malice. It is institutional gravity. But its result is identical to malice.

Third: the religious person becomes, in the precise image of Sr. Inigo's chapter, not a signpost pointing toward life but scenery. A familiar, unremarkable feature of the landscape. Present. Identifiable by habit and institution. Pointing nowhere in particular.

In one sentence, if I were to state the thesis of this talk: prophetic clarity, the capacity to say an unambiguous Yes to life and an unambiguous No to the forces that destroy it, is not one charism among many in consecrated life. It is the definition of consecrated life. And we have largely lost it.

Let us examine how, and what is required to recover it.

Part 1 | 8 Minutes Two Kinds of Religious Presence in the World

Let me identify two fundamentally different models of what it means to be a religious person in the world. They share the same vocabulary. They produce entirely different communities and entirely different relationships to the poor.

Model A: The Religious as Scenery

In the first model, the religious person is what Sr. Inigo calls scenery. They are present in the landscape of society. Their presence is noted and, in a general way, valued. They run institutions that are respected. They appear at civic functions. They have a seat at important tables.

But scenery does not point anywhere. Scenery is simply there. It provides aesthetic pleasure, or a sense of cultural continuity, or institutional stability. It does not disturb. It does not name what is wrong. It does not take sides.

The characteristics of scenery are observable. It does not generate conflict because it does not take positions. It is comfortable with every regime, because every regime needs the institutional services it provides. It speaks of justice in a sufficiently general way that no specific injustice is named, and no specific power is challenged. It is, in Walter Brueggemann's phrase, an agent of the royal consciousness: supporting the social arrangements that produce comfort for those within the system (Brueggemann, The Prophetic Imagination, 2001, ch. 2).

The scenery model is not the product of cowardice alone, though cowardice plays its part. It is the product of institutional gravity. Institutions that depend on donors, on government recognition, on social approval, develop a rational incentive to avoid controversy. And prophetic witness is always controversial. There is, therefore, a structural pressure in every institutionalized religious congregation toward the scenery model. It requires deliberate, sustained, structurally supported counter-pressure to resist it.

Model B: The Religious as Signpost

The second model takes its image from Sr. Inigo's chapter title: the religious as signpost at the crossroads.

A signpost has three defining features that distinguish it precisely from scenery.

It points. Not in every direction, and not nowhere. It points in a specific, named direction. It does not say: life is complex, there are many valid perspectives, we must hold the tension carefully. It says: this way. The signpost's clarity is not arrogance. It is service. The traveler at the crossroads needs to know which way to go, not a meditation on the hermeneutical complexity of direction-finding.

It is planted at the point of greatest confusion. Not in the safe places where the road is clear. At the junction where people are lost. The signpost is placed precisely where disorientation is most acute: where the paths diverge, where the choice becomes unavoidable, where someone must decide.

It points beyond itself. The signpost does not draw attention to its own beauty, its own history, its own institutional achievements. It points. The moment it begins pointing at itself it has ceased to be a signpost and has become a monument. A monument commemorates. A signpost directs.

John 10:9-10 sets the framework for the entire chapter with a directness that should be allowed to disturb us. Two figures. One gate. The thief comes to steal, to kill, to destroy. Jesus comes to give life, and to give it in all its fullness.

Every human situation, every social arrangement, every institutional structure, every political decision, every cultural norm falls on one side or the other of this line. Either it promotes life in its fullness, or it diminishes life. Either it participates in the mission of Christ, or it participates, however unconsciously, in the mission of the thief.

The signpost's task is to identify which side of the line each situation occupies, and to name it clearly. Without diplomacy. Without the false balance that treats the oppressor and the oppressed as equally valid perspectives. Without the institutional caution that silences the prophet because silence is safer than speech.

Part 2 | 7 Minutes Etymological Recovery: Three Words to Reclaim

Let me attend now to three words that are structurally central to this talk and have been domesticated in ways that must be reversed.

First Word: Vita — Life

The Latin word vita, from which we derive vital, vitality, vitamin, means simply: life. But when Jesus uses the word in John 10:10, it is the Greek zoe, and he amplifies it with a crucial qualification: zoe perissos, life overflowing, life in abundance, life that exceeds the minimum necessary for survival.

The English phrase life in all its fullness does not quite capture the radical excess of perissos. The word means to be over and above, to overflow, to exceed the container. Jesus is not promising biological survival. He is promising a quality of existence that is structurally incompatible with poverty, with indignity, with caste oppression, with bonded labour, with the theft of ancestral land, with the carrying of human waste on a human head.

Over the centuries, zoe perissos has been domesticated in two directions. In one direction, it has been spiritualized: life in fullness becomes eternal life, a promise deferred to the next world, which conveniently releases us from any obligation to pursue it in this one. In the other direction, it has been individualized: life in fullness becomes personal flourishing, private salvation, the interior peace of the believer, unconnected to the social conditions that determine whether zoe perissos is actually possible for the human beings around them.

When Jesus announces life in abundance in the same breath as the proclamation of the Jubilee in Luke 4, he is making a structural claim. The Jubilee is not a spiritual metaphor. It is a social arrangement: the release of prisoners, the cancellation of debts, the return of land, the restoration of relationship between the prosperous and the dispossessed. Life in fullness has a political economy. It requires social conditions. And those social conditions are the signpost's business.

Second Word: Apartheid

Sr. Inigo makes a move in Chapter 9 that requires careful examination because it is so productive. She detaches the word apartheid from its specific South African political history and restores it to its etymological meaning.

Apartheid is Afrikaans: apart plus the suffix -heid, meaning the condition of being apart, the state of separation. In South Africa it named a legal, political, racial system of enforced separation. That system was dismantled in 1994. But Sr. Inigo's claim is that the condition of being apart, the interior state of separation from the suffering of those around us, is not dismantled by political change. It is a habituated pattern of perception. A trained inattentiveness. A cultivated capacity not to see.

This interior apartheid, practised without consciousness of what one is doing, is what allows a religious congregation to conduct its scheduled prayers, its annual retreats, its community meetings, its institutional governance, three kilometers from a community in acute distress without any awareness of the proximity. The distance is not geographical. It is perceptual. It is the apartheid of consciousness: a separation so thorough that the suffering does not register as information requiring a response.

Socrates said that the unexamined life is not worth living. He meant this philosophically: genuine humanity requires self-knowledge, critical reflection, the willingness to interrogate one's own assumptions. Sr. Inigo applies the same standard to consecrated life: the religious community that does not examine its own apartheid of consciousness against the standard of the Gospel is at risk of practising separation while speaking the language of solidarity.

Third Word: Signum — Sign

The Latin signum means a mark, a sign, a signal, a distinguishing indicator. Vatican II's theology of religious life, developed most fully in Lumen Gentium 44 and Vita Consecrata 1, uses the word eschatological sign to describe what religious life is meant to be in the world: a visible indicator, pointing beyond the present order toward the Kingdom that is coming and has already begun.

The sign, like the signpost, is defined by its referent. It points beyond itself. A road sign that points toward itself has failed as a sign. A religious community that has become, in public perception, primarily a provider of institutional services has failed as a sign, not because the services are wrong, but because the sign has stopped pointing.

Part 3 | 10 Minutes The Scriptural Constellation

Let me now read several scriptural texts together, not as proof-texts for a pre-determined conclusion but as a constellation, each text adding a dimension the others do not contain, converging on a single comprehensive demand.

Text 1 — Deuteronomy 30:15-19: The Structure of the Choice

The context of Deuteronomy 30 is the threshold moment of Israel's history: the people stand at the border of the Promised Land, about to enter a new social order. Moses speaks the final words of the covenant. And the covenant is formulated as a choice: I have set before you today life and prosperity, death and adversity. Choose life so that you and your descendants may live (Deuteronomy 30:15, 19).

The structure of this choice is not a general invitation to be virtuous. It is an invitation to organize one's entire social life around the promotion of life. The specific content of choosing life in Deuteronomy includes: caring for the widow, the orphan, and the foreigner (Deuteronomy 10:18); releasing debt every seven years (Deuteronomy 15:1-2); paying the day labourer before sunset (Deuteronomy 24:15); leaving the corners of the field unharvested for the poor (Deuteronomy 24:19-21).

The choice of life in Deuteronomy has a social economy. It is not a spiritual attitude. It is a way of arranging human community such that every member of it, including the most vulnerable, has access to the conditions of life in fullness.

When God says choose life, God is describing a choice that has structural consequences. The religious congregation that chooses life cannot limit that choice to the interior dispositions of its members. The choice must be visible in how it pays its workers, how it structures its fees, how it allocates its resources, and whose suffering it allows to interrupt its schedule.

Text 2 — Amos 5:21-24: When Worship Is Rejected

Amos 5 is one of the most confrontational texts in the Hebrew prophetic tradition. God speaks directly: I hate, I despise your festivals. I take no delight in your solemn assemblies. Even though you offer me your burnt offerings, I will not accept them. Take away from me the noise of your songs. I will not listen to the melody of your harps (Amos 5:21-23).

This is not God rejecting worship. It is God rejecting worship that is offered as a substitute for justice. The text continues with the verse that has become the signature of the prophetic tradition: But let justice roll down like waters, and righteousness like an ever-flowing stream (Amos 5:24).

The prophetic challenge to liturgical religion is precisely the signpost challenge. It asks: does your worship point toward anything? Does the beauty of your liturgy generate a corresponding commitment to the conditions that allow all people to live with dignity? Or has the liturgy become scenery: aesthetically satisfying, spiritually nourishing, and pointing nowhere in particular?

In the West Bengal context, where the aesthetic beauty of the Church's liturgical life stands in unacknowledged proximity to the conditions of the Santali community whose ancestral land has been acquired, whose youth have been trafficked into urban labour, and whose liturgy is never celebrated in Santali in any Catholic church within thirty kilometers of their village, the Amos text is not historical. It is current.

Text 3 — Micah 6:6-8: The Summary of the Whole Law

Micah 6 is structured as a legal proceeding in which God brings a case against Israel. The people ask: with what shall I come before the Lord? Shall I offer thousands of rams? Shall I give my firstborn for my transgression? (Micah 6:6-7).

The prophetic response is the most compressed summary of the demands of the covenant in the entire Hebrew Bible: He has told you, O mortal, what is good; and what does the Lord require of you but to do justice, and to love kindness, and to walk humbly with your God? (Micah 6:8).

Three verbs. To do justice: not to feel compassionate about injustice, but to do something that changes it. To love kindness: the word is hesed again, the covenant loyalty we examined in Talk 6, the love that does not calculate. To walk humbly with God: to take one's direction from God's own direction, not from the institutional consensus or the donor's preference.

The signpost stands at the crossroads between the God of Micah 6:8 and the comfortable religion of thousands of rams. Its task is to point clearly toward the God who asks for justice, for hesed, for humble walking, and to name clearly the religion that substitutes expensive ritual for structural change.

Text 4 — Genesis 1:26-27: The Foundation of the Case

Every claim in this talk rests ultimately on a single theological foundation that Genesis 1 establishes without negotiation: both male and female are made in the image of God, the imago Dei, without qualification attached to caste, class, gender, tribe, or social function.

The theologian Gustavo Gutierrez, in A Theology of Liberation (1971), argues that this foundational claim produces an obligation that is not optional: if every human person bears the image of God, then every diminishment of a human person, every condition that prevents them from living as the image-bearer they are, constitutes an offence not only against the person but against God (Gutierrez, A Theology of Liberation, 1971, ch. 9). The option for the poor is not a political preference or a pastoral strategy. It is the logical consequence of taking the imago Dei seriously.

When the Dalit woman of Murshidabad carries night soil on her head, the image of God is carrying night soil. When the Santali youth of the Rarh region is displaced from his ancestral land without compensation, the image of God is homeless. When the child in peri-urban Kolkata is exploited in a factory for the production of goods that will be exported to markets where no one will ask about the conditions of their manufacture, the image of God is being exploited.

These are not rhetorical flourishes. They are the precise theological implication of Genesis 1:26-27. And the signpost's task is to point toward this reality with sufficient clarity that the people at the crossroads understand what is at stake.

Text 5 — Matthew 25:40: The Identity of the Least

We have quoted Matthew 25 in Talks 7 and 9. Each time we return to it, a different dimension becomes visible. In Talk 7 it named the prisoner as the place of Christ's presence. Here, the same logic applies to every person named by Sr. Inigo in Chapter 9: the Dalit woman, the trafficked girl, the adivasi family, the bonded labourer, the malnourished child.

Whatever you did for the least of these my brothers and sisters, you did for me (Matthew 25:40). The least is not a sentimental description. In the social context of first-century Palestine, it is a precise indicator: the one at the bottom of every hierarchy, the one with no social leverage, no political access, no institutional advocate. The one who cannot come to us. The one we must go to, if they are to be reached at all.

B.R. Ambedkar, who understood what it meant to be the least with the precision of lived experience, wrote: I measure the progress of a community by the degree of progress which women have achieved (Ambedkar, Annihilation of Caste, 1936). The same measure applies to religious communities: the degree to which the least of these are reached, served, advocated for, and accompanied is the measure of whether the community is living its mission or performing it.

Part 4 | 8 Minutes The Historical Diagnosis: How Consecrated Life Became Scenery

I want to name the specific historical process by which consecrated life in India moved from prophetic presence at the margins to institutional prominence at the center. This process is not simple cowardice. It is the product of identifiable historical forces that must be named if they are to be resisted.

Stage One: The Institutional Turn

The first religious congregations to arrive in India as part of the modern missionary movement came, in many cases, with a genuine commitment to the marginalized. The congregations that opened schools for Dalit children in the nineteenth century, the sisters who ran clinics in rural areas where no other medical presence existed, the priests who learned the tribal languages and lived among the adivasi communities: these are real and honourable chapters of the Church's history in India.

But the institution that is established to serve the poor develops an internal logic of self-preservation that gradually recalibrates it toward sustainability. The school that charges fees to fund its operations begins to attract students who can pay those fees. The hospital that requires capital investment to maintain its equipment begins to price its services for those who can afford them.
