The formation house that depends on recognition by the government and the university begins to shape its programme to produce the kind of output those institutions recognize.

This is not corruption. It is institutional gravity. Every institution that does not consciously and structurally resist this gravity will be carried by it. And very few of our congregations have the constitutional structures, the governance mechanisms, or the formational culture that enables the resistance.

Stage Two: The Caste Silence

The most specific and most damaging failure of the Indian Church's prophetic witness in the modern period is its relative silence on caste. This silence requires explanation, because it is not accidental and it is not simple.

Caste operates in the Indian Church simultaneously as social reality and as institutional embarrassment. It operates as social reality because caste discrimination exists within Christian communities: Dalit Christians are, in documented and extensively researched patterns, excluded from certain positions of leadership, denied access to certain sacramental functions, buried in separate sections of cemeteries, and subject to social discrimination by higher-caste Christian communities.

It operates as institutional embarrassment because naming this honestly would require the Church to acknowledge that its baptismal theology, its proclamation that in Christ there is neither Jew nor Greek, slave nor free (Galatians 3:28), has not in fact produced the social reality it proclaims. The gap between the baptismal formula and the caste reality in the Indian Church is, in Ambedkar's devastating assessment, the reason he concluded that Christianity had failed the Dalit in India (Ambedkar, Annihilation of Caste, 1936, Preface).

The signpost at this particular crossroads cannot be silent about caste. Not because silence is comfortable and speech is hard, though that is true. But because the caste silence is itself a form of participation in the life-destroying force that caste represents.

Stage Three: The Globalization Trap

The third historical force is the most recent and the most pervasive. Globalization has produced a global economy that rewards efficiency, scale, measurable outcomes, and market alignment. The Church's institutions, insofar as they engage with this economy as employers, as service providers, as landowners, and as institutional actors, are subject to the same pressures.

The consequence is the gradual subordination of the Gospel's social economy, which asks first how this benefits the most vulnerable, to the market's social economy, which asks first how this remains financially sustainable. These are not always incompatible. But when they conflict, the institution under globalization pressure will systematically favor the market's logic unless there is a deliberate, structurally supported prophetic counterweight.

Sollicitudo Rei Socialis 36, written by John Paul II in 1987 as a prophetic response to the logic of globalization, identifies the structures of sin: social arrangements that systematically produce outcomes in which the powerful benefit at the expense of the poor. The Church's own institutions can embody structures of sin, not through malice but through institutional gravity, through the accumulated effect of decisions each of which seemed reasonable at the time.

Part 5 | 5 Minutes The Prophetic Demand: Learning to Say Yes and No

I want to be precise about what this talk demands. Not everything. One thing.

Sr. Inigo's image of the signpost is, finally, an image of clarity. The signpost is useful not because it is complicated but because it is clear. It does not offer a nuanced discussion of the relative merits of different directions. It points. And in pointing, it serves.

The prophetic demand of this talk is a call to clarity. To the discipline of naming, specifically and without diplomatic evasion, what promotes life and what destroys it.

The NO That Must Be Learned

Gaudium et Spes 27, the Second Vatican Council's most direct statement on human dignity, names the actions that are intrinsically opposed to the dignity of the human person: murder, genocide, abortion, euthanasia, suicide, mutilation, torture, arbitrary imprisonment, deportation, slavery, prostitution, and degrading working conditions. It calls these violations of human dignity, and it calls the Church to oppose them.

In West Bengal, in 2026, this list does not describe distant historical events. It describes identifiable realities: the bonded labour of the Murshidabad district, the arbitrary imprisonment of the undertrial in Dum Dum, the slavery-equivalent conditions of the trafficked girl in Kolkata, the degrading working conditions of the tea garden labourer in the Jalpaiguri foothills. A No to these realities, from a consecrated person who understands what they are saying, is not a political statement. It is a theological one. It is the signpost doing its job.

The No must also be said to the internal structures that reproduce caste and gender exclusion within our own communities. The formation programme that has no content on caste theology. The community that does not promote Dalit women to leadership. The parish that does not celebrate the Eucharist in the language of the Santali or Sora community worshipping in it. These are internal failures of the signpost. They disqualify the signpost from pointing outward until they are addressed internally.

The YES That Must Be Embodied

Evangelii Gaudium 187 states that the preferential option for the poor is primarily a theological category before it is a cultural, sociological, political, or philosophical one. It emerges from the Trinity's own option for the vulnerable, disclosed in the Incarnation and confirmed in Matthew 25.

The Yes that this call requires is a Yes to specific, embodied, costly solidarity. Gandhi's criterion is precise: before any action, ask how it will benefit the poorest and most vulnerable. Not after the action, in the impact assessment. Before. As the primary criterion.

Sr. Inigo proposes a structural response that few congregations have taken seriously: the deliberate release of some personnel from institutional ministries to engage in non-institutional work at the margins. Not all. Not most. But some, deliberately, structurally, with the congregation's blessing and support.

Because the institutional logic will always provide a reason why no one can be spared. The school needs the teacher. The hospital needs the sister. The formation house needs the formator. These needs are real. But if they are always decisive, the consequence is that the poorest, for whom no institution yet exists, are never reached.

Until and unless the consecrated person recovers the capacity to say a clear Yes to life and a clear No to the forces that destroy it — and until that Yes and No are embodied in institutional decisions, not only in personal convictions — consecrated life will continue to be scenery in a landscape that requires a signpost.

St. Irenaeus said: the glory of God is the human fully alive. The Dalit woman of Murshidabad is a human being. The adivasi youth of the Rarh region is a human being. The trafficked girl in Kolkata, the tea garden worker in Jalpaiguri, the child in the factory, the undertrial in Dum Dum: each is a human being. Each is the gloria Dei.

Their fullness of life is the glory of God. Their destruction is the work of the thief.

We have consecrated our lives to stand at the crossroads and point. Clearly. Specifically. Without flinching.

It is time to be a signpost.

Personal Reflection | 2 Minutes Silence One Question

I want to give you one question. Not a comfortable one.

Where in my specific life, in my community, my institution, my ministry, am I practising apartheid of consciousness: the trained inattentiveness that allows the suffering of a specific person or community to exist within my reach without registering as a claim on my response?

Name it specifically. Not the suffering of the world in general. One person. One community. One situation from which you have withdrawn your gaze.

[Allow two minutes of silence]

Closing Prayer | 3 Minutes

Lord of life —

You said it without qualification: I have come that they may have life, and have it in all its fullness. You drew a line between yourself and the thief. You named the thief's work: stealing, killing, destroying. You named your own: giving, overflowing, filling to abundance.

We confess that we have stood at crossroads and said nothing. We have had schedules, institutions, donors, and a deep inherited preference for stability. We have practised an apartheid of consciousness so thorough that the cry of the Dalit woman in Murshidabad did not reach us in our common room three kilometers from her situation.

We have celebrated the beauty of our liturgy, and you heard it with Amos: take away from me the noise of your solemn assemblies. Until justice rolls down like water.

Give us the courage of the signpost: to point clearly, specifically, without diplomatic evasion, toward life. For the Dalit woman of Murshidabad whose dignity is your glory. For the Santali youth of the Rarh region whose land is your creation and whose dispossession is a theft from you. For the trafficked girl in Kolkata who is your image, being sold.

Let us say No to the thief's work — clearly, structurally, at personal cost. Let us say Yes to life in all its fullness — for all of them, not only for those whose fullness is already within reach.

In West Bengal, in its tea gardens and its bonded villages, in its trafficked corridors and its undertrial cells, in its Santali communities and its peri-urban margins: let your Church be a signpost.

Not scenery. A signpost.

Amen.

Growing Series Arc — Nine Talks

Internal Arc of Part III So Far

Talk 7 — Encounter: Who is in the cell? Go to them.

Talk 8 — Advocacy: Does the state have the right to take their life? Say No.

Talk 9 — Structural Vision: What larger forces destroy life? How do we say Yes and No clearly?

Part III continues. Talks 10, 11, and 12 will engage the specific missions of education, human trafficking, and the spirituality that sustains the whole of this demanding service. The series moves now from structural vision to the specific vocational territories that give it flesh.

Morning Star Regional Seminary | Barrackpore, West Bengal

Fr. William Anthony MSFS | Talk 9: Signum Vitae

Talk 10 — Fides in Eremo: The Faith That Finds Water in the Wilderness

Interior Sustenance as the Condition of Prophetic Mission

45-Minute Recollection for Priests, Religious, and Major Seminarians — West Bengal Region

Primary Anchor — Psalm 107:35

"He turns a desert into pools of water."

Secondary Anchor — Hebrews 11:6

"Without faith it is impossible to please God."

Time Guide

For the Speaker

Register and Position in the Series: This is the interior turn of Part III. Talks 7-9 were outward-facing: prison, execution, systemic injustice. Talk 10 is the theological basement that makes those three possible without burnout or despair. Name this explicitly in the opening. The audience needs to understand why, after three demanding outward talks, we are turning inward. This is not retreat from mission. It is the discovery of the oasis that sustains it.

Register: Lobo Register 3 (Formation Talk). The intellectual architecture is fully present: problematic, conceptual pair, etymological recovery, scriptural constellation, historical diagnosis, prophetic demand. The tone is the most contemplative of the series. More silence. More space. The stories (the old man at the clinic, Halley, the little girl on the bridge) are not illustrations — they carry the theological argument in narrative form. Honour them with pace.

Key Biblical Texts: Ps 107:35; Heb 11:1, 6; Mk 9:24; Dt 8:2-5; 1 Kgs 19:4-12; Lk 4:1-2; Lk 15:17; Gal 1:15-18; Hos 2:14; Gen 22:1-14; Mt 26:53; Jn 20:24-29 (Thomas); Rom 8:26-27.

Key Documents: Vita Consecrata 38, 93; Evangelii Gaudium 259-261 (encounter with Christ); Deus Caritas Est 1, 17; Verbum Domini 86 (lectio divina); Dei Verbum 21; Pastores Dabo Vobis 46-47; Catechism 2697-2719 (prayer).

Key Thinkers: Abraham Heschel (God in Search of Man, 1955 — faith as response to divine initiative); Paul Tillich (The Courage to Be, 1952 — faith as ultimate concern and the acceptance of anxiety); Karl Rahner (The Practice of Faith, 1983 — everyday mysticism); Henri Nouwen (The Way of the Heart, 1981 — solitude as foundation of ministry); Thomas Merton (New Seeds of Contemplation, 1961 — desert theology); Martin Buber (I and Thou, 1923 — the I-Thou encounter that grounds all authentic relationship).

Distribute at Close: The five personal reflection questions at the end of Part 5 are best given as a printed card for personal prayer during the recollection's designated silent period.

Opening | 5 Minutes The Problematic

By way of beginning, let me name the structural problem that this talk addresses — and it is a problem that sits beneath every other problem we have examined in this series.

We say that faith is the foundation of consecrated life. Vita Consecrata 38 is precise: consecrated persons are called to be living signs of the primacy of God, witnesses to the fact that God alone is sufficient. The entire architecture of the vows — chastity, poverty, obedience — rests on this single conviction: that God is real, that God is present, and that a life oriented toward God is the most fully human life available.

We say this. We teach it in our formation programmes. We celebrate it in our solemn professions. It is the founding claim of everything we are and everything we do.

And then the following things happen. Not through apostasy. Not through a dramatic crisis of faith. Through the slow, unremarkable erosion of the actual living relationship with God that the word faith is supposed to describe.

The morning prayer becomes a duty discharged rather than an encounter sought. The Eucharist becomes a ritual maintained rather than a person met. Lectio divina, where it exists at all, becomes an intellectual exercise with the text rather than a conversation with its Author. The examination of conscience is skipped on busy days, which is to say: on most days. The silence that is the precondition of genuine prayer is crowded out by the schedule that the institution requires and the digital connectivity that the culture provides.

And the result — observable, consistent, and rarely named honestly in formation contexts — is a consecrated person whose external religious life is impeccable and whose interior life with God is, in the careful language of the tradition, arid. Dry. A wilderness without water.

The problem is not primarily moral. It is not even primarily spiritual in the devotional sense. It is theological. Because a faith that has become religious routine rather than living encounter has ceased to be faith in the theological sense and has become, in Tillich's phrase, a finite concern that has displaced the ultimate one (Tillich, The Courage to Be, 1952, ch. 2).

This produces three consequences that are directly observable in religious communities.

First: the outward prophetic action demanded by Talks 7, 8, and 9 of this series becomes unsustainable. Prison ministry, advocacy against execution, naming systemic injustice at personal cost: these are not activities that can be sustained by institutional commitment alone. They require a depth of interior life that institutional religion does not automatically produce. The missionary who has no oasis burns out. And burnt-out missionaries become either cynical or merely efficient. Neither serves the mission.

Second: the quality of our accompanying presence with those who suffer diminishes precisely when it is most needed. The person in the wilderness, in grief, in failure, in illness, needs to be accompanied by someone who has themselves been in a wilderness and found God there. A person who has never genuinely wrestled with God, who has never experienced the aridity of unanswered prayer, who has never sat in the darkness without knowing whether morning will come, cannot accompany anyone through those experiences with authority. Their presence will be technically competent and spiritually thin.

Third: the communal life deteriorates in the specific ways that are most difficult to diagnose and most resistant to institutional solutions. Communities in which individual members have no genuine interior life with God become communities held together by routine, by structure, by the shared management of institutional obligations. They are functional. They are not alive. And eventually they cease to be functional too, because structure without spirit cannot sustain itself indefinitely.

In one sentence, the thesis of this talk: faith, understood as a living, tested, costly encounter with the God who redeems rather than rescues, is not one interior virtue to be cultivated alongside the prophetic commitments of consecrated life. It is the underground spring that makes the oasis possible. Without it, everything dries up.

Let us examine what this faith is, and what it is not.

Part 1 | 8 Minutes Two Models of Faith

There are two operative models of faith at work in our formation houses, our parishes, and our personal lives. They use the same vocabulary. They produce entirely different interior lives and entirely different capacities for mission.

Model A: Faith as Religious Performance

The first model of faith is transactional and performative. In this model, faith is the sum of one's religious practices: attendance at prayer, reception of the sacraments, observance of the rule, regularity at lectio, fidelity to community life. The person who does these things is considered a person of faith. The person who struggles with them is considered weak in faith.

This model has the considerable advantage of being measurable. You can observe whether a person comes to prayer. You can track sacramental practice. You can evaluate adherence to the community rule. Formation programmes built on this model can produce assessments, progress reports, and graduation criteria.

But the model has a structural deficiency that becomes critical precisely when the person's life enters a wilderness. Because performance-faith is built on outcomes. It is calibrated to the expectation that faithful religious practice produces discernible results: interior peace, answered prayers, a sense of God's presence and guidance. And when the wilderness arrives, when the prayer goes dry, when God seems absent, when the practice produces nothing but the sense of going through the motions, the performance model has no resources for that experience. It interprets aridity as failure. It produces either renewed effort to perform better or quiet despair that the performance is no longer working.

Paul Tillich identifies the specific anxiety that performance-faith produces: the anxiety of condemnation, the fear that one is not measuring up to the standard that religious performance requires (Tillich, The Courage to Be, 1952, ch. 2). This anxiety drives religious perfectionism: the endless effort to do the right things, to say the right prayers, to be seen as a person of faith. It is exhausting. And it is, finally, a form of self-preoccupation disguised as devotion.

Model B: Faith as Fiducia — The Entrusting of the Self

The second model begins from a theological distinction that the Reformation made sharp but that belongs to the whole Christian tradition: the distinction between fides quae, the content of faith, the things that are believed, and fides qua, the act of faith, the living trust of the whole person in the living God.

Fides qua is what the tradition calls fiducia: radical self-entrusting. It is the act described when Abraham left his country without knowing the destination (Genesis 12:1). It is what the father demonstrates in Sr. Inigo's story: going every morning to a wife who no longer recognizes him, not because the relationship is producing satisfying outcomes, but because he knows who she is and that knowledge is sufficient for continued fidelity.

Fiducia is not the absence of doubt. Mark 9:24 gives us the most honest prayer in the Gospels: Lord, I do have faith. Help my lack of faith. This is not a contradiction. It is the precise description of fiducia: a trust that co-exists with uncertainty, that continues in the absence of felt certainty, that persists through the wilderness precisely because it is not built on how the wilderness feels.

Abraham Heschel, in God in Search of Man (1955), reverses the usual framing of faith with a precision that is characteristic of the prophetic tradition. Faith, he argues, is not primarily humanity's search for God. It is humanity's response to God's search for humanity (Heschel, God in Search of Man, 1955, Part III). The initiative is always God's. We do not generate faith by sufficient effort. We receive it by attending to the initiative that is already underway.

The two models produce entirely different responses to the wilderness experience. Performance-faith interprets the wilderness as evidence of personal failure: I am not praying correctly, I am not faithful enough, something is wrong with my spiritual life. Fiducia interprets the wilderness as curriculum: God is doing something here that requires the stripping away of every support except the foundational trust. And the stripping is the formation.

Part 2 | 7 Minutes Etymological Recovery: Three Words to Reclaim

Let me attend to three words that are central to this talk and have each been domesticated in ways that obscure the tradition's most demanding and most consoling insights.

First Word: Fides

The Latin fides, from which we derive faith and fidelity, comes from the root fidere, to trust. But the full semantic range of fides in classical usage includes: trust, loyalty, word given and kept, the bond of faithful relationship. The Roman concept of fides governed treaties, friendships, commercial contracts, and the relationship between patron and client. To break fides was to break the most fundamental social bond.

When the New Testament uses pistis, the Greek equivalent, it carries the same relational weight. Pistis is not intellectual assent to propositions. It is the orientation of the whole person toward another in trustworthy relationship. The pistis of a soldier toward a commander. The pistis of a partner in a covenant.

What has happened to fides over the centuries is precisely the domestication that Brueggemann identifies in verbal-to-nominal conversion. Faith has become a thing you have: a body of doctrine to be assented to, a set of practices to be performed, a spiritual state to be maintained. It has moved from the relational and dynamic, trustworthy orientation toward the living God, to the cognitive and static, correct beliefs held by the individual.

Recovering fides means recovering the relational, dynamic, costly quality of the original: the trust that commits the whole person, that holds on not because of felt certainty but because the relationship itself is the ground of one's existence. Karl Rahner calls this the foundational act of human existence: the decision, made repeatedly and at depth, to be a person who trusts (Rahner, The Practice of Faith, 1983, p. 22).

Second Word: Eremos

The Greek word eremos, wilderness or desert, is used in the New Testament in two apparently contradictory contexts. It is the place where Jesus is tempted for forty days (Luke 4:1-2): the place of trial, of stripping, of the assault of every alternative to God. And it is also the place where Jesus goes to pray (Luke 5:16): eremos is the preferred location for his most intense encounters with the Father.

The same word. The place of temptation and the place of prayer. The wilderness is both.

This lexical overlap is theologically precise. The desert strips away the social supports, the comfortable identities, the distractions that allow a person to manage their relationship with God at a comfortable distance. In the stripping, the temptations become visible, because they are no longer masked by busyness. And in that same stripping, the encounter with God becomes possible in a depth that the furnished life does not permit.

Thomas Merton, the Trappist monk whose desert theology has shaped contemporary spirituality, argues that the desert fathers went to the wilderness not to escape the world but to find, at the root of their own existence, the spring from which genuine engagement with the world could flow (Merton, New Seeds of Contemplation, 1961, ch. 1). The eremos is not the opposite of mission. It is mission's underground source.

Hosea 2:14 is perhaps the most surprising use of the wilderness image in all of Scripture: Therefore I will now allure her, and bring her into the wilderness, and speak tenderly to her. God's wilderness is a place of allurement. Of tender speech. The Hebrew for speak tenderly is literally speak to her heart. The wilderness, in Hosea, is where God whispers.

Third Word: Redemptio

The Latin redemptio comes from redimere: to buy back, to restore to original ownership, to liberate by payment. It carries the economic metaphor of the slave market: the redeemer pays the price that liberates the enslaved person and restores them to their original status as free.

This word is categorically different from rescissio, the Latin for rescue, which comes from rescutere: to shake off, to pull clear of danger. Rescue removes the person from the situation. Redemption transforms the person within the situation and transforms the meaning of the situation itself.

The difference is not merely semantic. It produces entirely different theologies of suffering, entirely different approaches to prayer, and entirely different capacities for accompanying others in their pain. The rescue theology promises deliverance from the wilderness. The redemption theology promises transformation within it.

The cross is the definitive demonstration. God did not rescue Jesus from crucifixion. Twelve legions of angels were available, and none were sent (Matthew 26:53). God redeemed the crucifixion: took the most obscene instrument of Roman state power and transformed it, through resurrection, into the axis of history's meaning. The wilderness of Golgotha became, by redemption, the site of the world's liberation.

Part 3 | 10 Minutes The Scriptural Constellation

The wilderness is not a marginal theme in Scripture. It is structurally central to the whole narrative. Let me read several texts together, each adding a dimension the others do not contain.

Text 1 — Deuteronomy 8:2-5: The Wilderness as Curriculum

Moses addresses Israel on the threshold of the Promised Land and offers a theological interpretation of the forty years of desert wandering: Remember the long way that the Lord your God has led you these forty years in the wilderness, in order to humble you, testing you to know what was in your heart, whether or not you would keep his commandments (Deuteronomy 8:2).

The wilderness had a purpose. It was not punishment for the sin of the golden calf alone, though that extends the period. It was curriculum. God's stated intention: to know what was in your heart. To discover, at depth, what the Israelites were actually made of when every external support was removed. The manna, which arrived daily and could not be stored (Exodus 16:19-20), was the wilderness's most precise formation tool: it trained Israel to trust God for today's provision without securing tomorrow's independently.

The formation lesson of Deuteronomy 8 is one that every seminary and novitiate should engrave at its entrance: God does not take us around the wilderness. He takes us through it. And the through is not wasted time. It is the time when the most permanent formation occurs, because it is the time when nothing else is available as a substitute for God.

Text 2 — 1 Kings 19:4-12: Elijah Under the Juniper Tree

Elijah has just performed the most spectacular prophetic feat in the Old Testament: he has called down fire from heaven on Carmel and destroyed the prophets of Baal (1 Kings 18). And immediately afterward he collapses.

He flees into the wilderness. He sits under a juniper tree and asks God to take his life: I have had enough, Lord. Take away my life. I am no better than my ancestors (1 Kings 19:4).

The burnout of the prophet after the mountaintop. Elijah, who had just demonstrated that God was real and present and powerful, is now in a wilderness so complete that he is asking for death. The wilderness follows the mountaintop, not the valley. This is the pattern of the most serious spiritual aridity.

And what does God do? He does not speak a word of theology. He sends an angel who touches Elijah, says the journey is too great for you (1 Kings 19:7), and provides food and water. Twice. Before any theological conversation begins.

Then Elijah travels forty days to Horeb, the mountain of God, and there God speaks. Not in the wind. Not in the earthquake. Not in the fire. In a still small voice (1 Kings 19:12), the Hebrew qol demamah daqah, literally the sound of sheer silence.

God's address to the burnt-out prophet is silence. Not explanation. Not justification of the previous suffering. Not a theological framework for the wilderness experience. Silence. The sound of a presence so complete it needs no words.

Henri Nouwen, who himself experienced profound periods of spiritual desolation, argues in The Way of the Heart (1981) that the desert fathers were not fleeing the world when they went to the wilderness. They were finding, beneath the social noise that makes God inaudible, the silence in which God speaks (Nouwen, The Way of the Heart, 1981, pp. 25-30). Elijah's God speaks in silence. Which means that the cultivation of silence, genuine silence, not merely the absence of external sound but the interior quiet that allows the qol demamah daqah to be heard, is not a luxury for the contemplative specialist. It is the ordinary requirement of prophetic ministry.

Text 3 — Luke 4:1-2 and Luke 5:16: The Two Uses of Eremos

Luke places two eremos texts in deliberate proximity. Immediately after the baptism, the Spirit leads Jesus into the eremos for forty days of trial (Luke 4:1-2). And throughout the Galilean ministry, Jesus repeatedly withdraws to the eremos to pray (Luke 5:16): But he would withdraw to deserted places and pray.

The same word. The temptation and the prayer. The place of assault and the place of encounter. Jesus does not avoid the eremos after the forty days of temptation. He returns to it. It is his preferred location for the deepest communion with the Father.

This is the tradition's answer to the question of why God permits the wilderness. The wilderness strips everything that allows a person to maintain the illusion of self-sufficiency. In the stripping, two things become visible simultaneously: the depth of one's own poverty and the depth of God's provision. The temptations in Luke 4 are, structurally, offers of escape from that poverty: make your own bread, exercise autonomous power, secure your own safety. Jesus refuses each one. He chooses to remain in the poverty of the eremos, dependent entirely on God, and in that poverty he is equipped for the mission that follows.

Text 4 — Hosea 2:14: God's Wilderness Courtship

Therefore I will now allure her, and bring her into the wilderness, and speak tenderly to her (Hosea 2:14). The context is God's response to Israel's unfaithfulness. Israel has pursued the Baals, the false gods of productivity and prosperity, and has forgotten the God of the Exodus. And God's response is not primarily judgment. It is allurement. I will lead her into the wilderness and speak to her heart.

The wilderness, in Hosea, is not punishment. It is intimacy. God strips away the false securities, the comfortable alternatives, the Baals that have been substituting for genuine relationship, precisely in order to speak to the heart in a register that the furnished life cannot receive.

Sr. Inigo's insight that the prison is a wilderness, that God uses the stripping of imprisonment to speak to hearts that had been too busy for the encounter, is entirely consistent with Hosea's theology. The African inmate Halley, in Tihar, discovered in his imprisonment what Israel discovered in Sinai: the wilderness is where God allures, where God whispers, where the false securities fall away and the actual God becomes audible.

Text 5 — Romans 8:26-27: The Spirit Who Prays Within

Paul addresses the experience of prayerlessness with a precision that the tradition has often underused. Likewise the Spirit helps us in our weakness; for we do not know how to pray as we ought, but that very Spirit intercedes with sighs too deep for words (Romans 8:26).

We do not know how to pray as we ought. This is not a confession of inadequate technique. It is a theological statement: genuine prayer exceeds the capacity of the person praying. The deepest prayer cannot be articulated. The most honest intercession is a groan that the Spirit translates into language adequate to the encounter.

This text is of particular relevance for the experience of aridity in prayer. The person in a dry period, who sits before God and produces nothing, who cannot find words or images or feelings adequate to the encounter, who experiences the prayer time as silence and absence, is not failing at prayer. They may be, in Paul's framework, at the very threshold of the Spirit's deepest intercession: the prayer that is too deep for words, that the Spirit carries when the person cannot.

Thomas Aquinas, building on this Pauline foundation, identified prayer not as a human activity directed toward God but as a divine activity within the human person: it is the Spirit who prays, using the human person as the instrument and the location of the encounter. Which means that the absence of felt experience in prayer is not the absence of prayer. It may be its most genuine form.

Part 4 | 8 Minutes The Historical Diagnosis: How Faith Was Domesticated

Let me now trace the specific historical process by which the faith described in Hebrews 11, by which the vivid, costly, relational fiducia of Abraham, Moses, Elijah, and Paul, was domesticated into the religious performance that most of our institutions now produce and measure. Naming this process is essential, because it reveals that the current situation is not inevitable. It is historically contingent. And it can be reversed.

Stage One: The Intellectualization of Faith

The first distortion entered in the Scholastic period. The great systematizers of the twelfth and thirteenth centuries, doing genuinely necessary work, built theological systems of extraordinary precision and coherence. But the unintended consequence was the gradual shift of faith's centre of gravity from the relational and existential to the cognitive and propositional.

Faith began to be defined primarily as assent to revealed truths: the fides quae, the content of belief. The fides qua, the act of trust, the existential self-entrusting to the living God, receded from the formational foreground. A person was considered a person of faith if they held the correct doctrines. The question of whether those doctrines had produced a living, tested, costly encounter with the God the doctrines described was a secondary question, largely reserved for the mystical tradition.

The result, visible in our seminaries and formation houses today, is students who can define faith with theological precision and who have no living interior relationship with the God their definitions describe. They have fides quae without fiducia. They have the map without the journey.

Stage Two: The Privatization of Faith

The second distortion entered in the modern period, partly as a response to the Reformation controversies and partly as a consequence of the Enlightenment's privatization of religion. Faith became a personal, interior matter: what one believes in one's heart, practised in the appropriate religious spaces, kept separate from public life and professional conduct.

This privatization produced, over several centuries, the precise situation that the prophetic tradition of Amos and Micah had already diagnosed: a religion whose interior life and whose public life are entirely compartmentalized. One can be devout in the chapel and unjust in the institution. One can pray faithfully and remain deaf to the cry of the Dalit woman two kilometers from the convent. One can maintain an impeccable sacramental life and practise the apartheid of consciousness that Talk 9 named.

The privatization of faith is, in this sense, the theological root of the scenery problem. Scenery-religion is religion that has accepted the privatization: it believes sincerely, prays regularly, and has agreed not to be disturbed by what is happening outside the chapel door.

Stage Three: The Indian Domestication

In the Indian context, these two distortions were compounded by a third. The colonial missionary model brought with it a form of Christianity already shaped by European Christendom: strongly institutional, doctrinally precise, liturgically regulated, and culturally European. The encounter with Indian spirituality, with its rich tradition of the interior life, its contemplative depth, its articulation of the divine-human relationship in terms of darshan, bhakti, and the guru-shishya relationship, was, with notable exceptions, managed rather than received.

George Soares-Prabhu SJ, in his theology of inculturation, identified what he called the triple alienation of the Indian theologian: alienated from their own spiritual tradition, from the Indian poor, and from the global theological conversation (Soares-Prabhu, cited in Lobo, Jnanadeepa). The faith that Indian seminarians receive in formation is often alienated from the very soil in which it is meant to grow.

The consequence is visible. The depth of bhakti that animates the Santali Christian's relationship to God, the contemplative quality of the adivasi prayer that emerges from deep relationship with the land and the community, the interior richness of the Dalit Christian's faith forged in conditions of systematic oppression: these are not treated, in most formation programmes, as resources for the Church's understanding of faith. They are treated as what needs to be formed into something more recognizably institutional.

Part 5 | 5 Minutes The Prophetic Demand: Interior Sustenance as Obligation

I want to be precise about what this talk demands. Not a longer prayer time, though a longer prayer time may follow from it. Not a new devotional practice, though a new devotional practice may emerge. The demand is more fundamental.

The demand is for an honest examination of whether the faith one professes is fiducia or performance. Whether one's prayer life is an encounter or a duty discharged. Whether the wilderness one is currently inhabiting is being interpreted as evidence of God's absence or as the curriculum of God's most intensive formation.

For the Individual: The Courage of Honest Prayer

Mark 9:24 gives us the permission that most formation programmes have not explicitly given: Lord, I do have faith. Help my lack of faith. This prayer is sufficient. It is more theologically honest than most prayer. And Jesus responds to it with a healing that the prayer's imperfection does not prevent.

Pastores Dabo Vobis 46 identifies the intimate knowledge of Jesus as the foundation of priestly identity and priestly mission. Not theological knowledge about Jesus. Knowledge of Jesus. The distinction is not rhetorical. It is the distinction between fides quae and fiducia, between the map and the journey.

The demand, personally, is to find the eremos. Not necessarily the physical desert, though for some that is the call. The interior eremos: the silence that strips the noise, the prayer time that is genuinely a conversation and not a monologue, the willingness to bring to God the actual wilderness of one's life rather than the presentable version of it.

For the Community: Formation Programmes That Produce Encounter

Verbum Domini 86 identifies lectio divina as more than a method of reading Scripture. It is an encounter with the living God through the Word that becomes, for the person who practises it genuinely, a transformation of the whole manner of engagement with reality. Benedict XVI's insistence on this in Verbum Domini is not nostalgic attachment to an old practice. It is a diagnosis of what formation programmes have omitted.

The demand for communities is structural: to examine whether formation programmes are producing people of fiducia or people of religious performance. Whether novices and seminarians are being taught to encounter God in the eremos of their own interior life, or merely to maintain the external forms of a religious life that God may or may not inhabit.

For the Mission: The Interior That Sustains the Exterior

Evangelii Gaudium 259 makes the connection explicit: the primary reason for evangelizing is the love of Jesus which we have received, the experience of salvation which urges us to ever greater love of him. The missionary's effectiveness is proportional to the depth of their personal encounter with Christ. Not to their competence, though competence matters. Not to their institutional resources, though these are useful. To the encounter.

This is why Talk 10 follows Talks 7, 8, and 9 in the series and does not precede them. The sequence is deliberate. We have named the prophetic demands: solidarity with the imprisoned, advocacy against execution, structural clarity against life-destroying forces. And now we name the spring that makes those demands sustainable without destroying the person who attempts to meet them.

Until and unless the interior life of the consecrated person is rooted in a genuine, living, tested encounter with the God who redeems rather than rescues, the prophetic demands of solidarity, advocacy, and structural clarity will produce missionaries who burn out, communities that grow cynical, and institutions that lose the spirit that animated their founding within a generation.

The old man went every morning. Five years. To a wife who no longer knew his name. He could not explain it with theology. He could not demonstrate it with measurable outcomes. He simply said: she does not know who I am. But I still know who she is.

That is the interior life that this series has been building toward. Not the faith that produces results. The faith that continues in the absence of results because the relationship itself, with the God who is real and present and working in the wilderness whether or not the person in the wilderness can feel it, is sufficient.

Halley, in Tihar, prayed. And gained nothing. And lost anger, ego, impatience, depression, insincerity, insecurity, and the fear of death.

That is enough. That is the oasis. That is the spring from which everything else this series has asked of us becomes possible.

Personal Reflection | 3 Minutes Silence Five Questions for Personal Prayer

I want to give you five questions. Not for discussion. For personal prayer during the time of silence that follows. They are the questions that Sr. Inigo places at the end of Chapter 10, and they are the questions that productive wildernesses ask.

First: What do you mean by faith? Not the definition you learned. Your definition — from your own experience, including the experience of struggle with it.

Second: Who is the God you are actually encountering in your prayer — not the God you were taught to encounter? What does your real image of God reveal about the state of your relationship with God?

Third: Where, specifically, is God in the wilderness you are currently inhabiting? Not whether God is there. Where. Name the specific place.

Fourth: Is your prayer life producing intimacy with God or the maintenance of a well-managed religious routine? What would need to change for it to become the former?

Fifth: Is your community prayer a ritual or an encounter? Does it genuinely bring you into the Word — and put that Word into your hands and feet?

[Distribute printed reflection cards if prepared. Allow three minutes of silence.]

[Three minutes of silence]

Closing Prayer | 2 Minutes

Lord of the desert and the oasis —

You who led Abraham out without giving him a map. You who kept Moses in the wilderness of Midian for forty years before the burning bush. You who drove your own Son into the eremos immediately after declaring him beloved. You who spoke to Elijah not in the earthquake or the fire but in the qol demamah daqah, the sound of sheer silence.

We confess that we have preferred you in the furnished life. We have kept our prayer times at a safe distance from the actual wilderness of our interior. We have maintained the forms of faith while the living encounter you intended them to sustain has quietly dried up.

We bring you the prayer of Mark 9:24. Not the prayer of the competent religious professional. The prayer of the desperate father: Lord, I do have faith. Help my lack of faith.

Turn our deserts into pools of water. Not after the desert ends. Inside it. Now. In the dry prayer life, in the difficult posting, in the community member we cannot reach, in the ministry that costs more than it gives, in the silence where we expected you to speak and you did not.

Give us the faith of Halley, who prayed and gained nothing and lost everything that was destroying him. Give us the faith of the old man at the clinic, who goes every morning not because it feels rewarding but because he still knows who she is.

Hold our hand. We are not confident we will hold yours. But we trust that you are the kind of Father who, when something frightening happens, does not let go.

Amen.

Growing Series Arc — Ten Talks

Internal Arc of Part III

Talk 7 — Encounter: Go to the cell. Christ is already there.

Talk 8 — Advocacy: Say No to the state's taking of a life it did not give.

Talk 9 — Structural Vision: Name the forces destroying life. Say Yes and No clearly.

Talk 10 — Interior Sustenance: The faith that makes all the above possible without burning out.

Part III concludes with Chapters 11 and 12: human trafficking and the spirituality of mission. The series moves from the interior oasis to the most demanding of the specific missionary territories, with the interior sustenance of Talk 10 now established as its source.

Morning Star Regional Seminary | Barrackpore, West Bengal

Fr. William Anthony MSFS | Talk 10: Fides in Eremo

Talk 12 — The Apostolate That Became a Market: Re-visioning Christian Education

Based on: "The Ever-Evolving Consecrated Life" by Sr Inigo SSA — Part III: We Are Called to Serve, Chapter 12: "Re-visioning Our Education Mission"

Scriptural Anchors: Hebrews 5:12; John 10:10; Matthew 25:31–46; Isaiah 10:1–2

Theological Anchor: Second Vatican Council, Gravissimum Educationis, 3; Gaudium et Spes, 76; Pope Francis, Evangelii Gaudium, 49

⏱ TIME GUIDE

Section Duration
Opening Problematic — The Gap We Must Name 7 min
Part 1 — Education: Mission or Verb That Became a Noun? 9 min
Part 2 — The Scriptural Constellation: Who Is the School For? 9 min
Part 3 — The Historical Diagnosis: When Did the Reversal Happen? 8 min
Part 4 — Four Institutional Myths and the Theological Response 9 min
Part 5 — Re-visioning: The Prophetic Demand 8 min
Closing Prayer 3 min

🔷 OPENING PROBLEMATIC — THE GAP WE MUST NAME (7 minutes)

By way of introducing this talk, let me begin with a well-known problematic.

We say that Christian education is a mission. We place the word "mission" in our school's prospectus, in our congregation's constitutions, in every speech delivered on Founder's Day. We say that our schools exist for the poor, that they are the continuation of the founding charism, that they bear witness to the Gospel of Jesus Christ in the marketplace of learning.

But the lived reality shows us something different.

In Catholic schools across India today — and this is not a polemical assertion; it is a documented statistic that Sr Inigo places before us in Chapter 12 — more than fifty percent of the student body comes from well-off families. In a country where the vast majority of people live below or near the poverty line, the schools founded explicitly for the marginalized now primarily serve those who were never in danger of marginalization.

Let me be precise about what this means, because the consequences follow with logical necessity.

First: the founding charism has been inverted. The congregations that built these schools did not build them for the children of the elite. They built them facing the adivasi villages of Jharkhand, the Dalit communities of Tamil Nadu, the landless laborers of Bengal. The inversion did not happen through malice. It happened gradually, through understandable financial pressures, through the logic of prestige, through the gravitational pull of institutional sustainability. But the result is inversion nonetheless.

Second: the theological claim embedded in the word "mission" has been hollowed out. A mission directed primarily toward the already-privileged is not a Christian mission in any sense that the Gospel recognizes. Pope Francis, in Evangelii Gaudium 49, is unambiguous: "I want a Church which is poor and for the poor." What applies to the Church applies structurally to every institution that calls itself an instrument of the Church's mission.

Third: the students produced by such institutions — and this is Sr Inigo's most uncomfortable empirical observation — include many of the most powerful and corrupt actors in Indian public life. Former students of Catholic schools who today vote for legislation that persecutes Christians. Former alumni who remain silent when missionaries are attacked. Former products of Catholic formation who participate with full social ease in systems of caste discrimination and economic exploitation.

Something has gone wrong — not only with what these schools teach, but with what they form.

In one sentence, if I were to respond to this problematic: Christian education becomes a commercial venture, and ceases to be an apostolate, at the precise moment when it begins to optimize for the student who already has everything rather than for the student who has nothing.

The rest of this talk is an unpacking of that sentence — historically, theologically, scripturally, and institutionally.

🔷 PART 1 — EDUCATION: MISSION OR VERB THAT BECAME A NOUN? (9 minutes)

Let me begin with a linguistic observation — and I ask you to stay with it, because it carries the entire structural diagnosis.

The Second Vatican Council's Gravissimum Educationis opens with a remarkable formulation. It speaks of education not as a service that institutions render, but as a right — a genuine right of every human person by virtue of being human. The Latin is precise: vera educatio — "true education" — is defined there in article 1 as the formation of a person toward "the ultimate goal of the human person, and at the same time toward the good of the societies of which the human being is a member."

Notice what this is. Education is a relational act between a person and their ultimate destiny, mediated through community. It is, theologically, a participation in the creative and redemptive work of God.

Now. What has happened to this understanding in the way our Catholic institutions actually operate?

Educating — the active verb, the relational act, the participation in God's redemptive purpose — has been reduced to education — the noun, the product, the credential, the commodity.

This is the same movement that Fr. Lobo has traced in other theological concepts. Eucharistein — the active verb of thanksgiving and self-offering — became "the Eucharist" — the noun, the ceremony, the ritual to be attended. Leitourgia — the public works of the people for the common good — became "liturgy" — the private cultic exercise of a gathered community. And now: educating — the active, prophetic, liberating act of forming conscience and empowering the powerless — has become "education" — the product sold at premium rates to families who can afford the fee receipt.

This is not merely a linguistic observation. Every verb-to-noun conversion produces real, observable institutional consequences.

When education is a verb, the central question is: Who needs to be educated, and what must I do to reach them? The school goes to the child. The congregation takes its mission to the tea garden, to the adivasi belt, to the flood-plain community in the Sundarbans. The institution bears the cost of mission.

When education is a noun, the central question is: What is the quality of the product we are offering, and who will pay for it? The child must come to the school. The child must have the fee receipt. The child must have the uniform, the academic baseline, the cultural fluency to navigate the institution's social codes. If the child does not have these things, the institution is not for them.

Victor Hugo's famous line — "when you open a school door, you close a prison door" — is a profound statement about education as verb. But it only functions theologically if the school's door is facing the child who would otherwise end up in the prison. If the door has been turned around to face the child who was never realistically going to end up there, then Victor Hugo's statement becomes a slogan on a wall.

Mother Teresa — who ran schools, and understood them from the inside — said something that should be placed alongside Victor Hugo: "Don't leave your schools. But change your strategies." Not: close your schools. Not: abandon the institutions. But: change — specifically, structurally, with honest self-examination — the strategies.

And changing strategies means, first of all, recovering the verb.

🔷 PART 2 — THE SCRIPTURAL CONSTELLATION: WHO IS THE SCHOOL FOR? (9 minutes)

Now let me come to the scriptural case — and I ask you to notice how these texts form a constellation, each one adding a dimension that the others do not carry alone.

Hebrews 5:12 is our first anchor: "You need someone to teach you."

The context is significant, and I do not want us to read past it. The author of Hebrews is addressing a community that has been Christians long enough to be teachers — and is instead still requiring to be taught the rudiments. The word used for "elements" is stoicheia — basic building blocks, the foundation stones of a structure. The author is saying: you should be building by now. You are still mixing cement.

But behind the specific pastoral frustration is a universal theological claim: every human person, at every stage of development, has the inalienable right and need to be taught. Not the privileged person. Not the academically promising person. Not the person whose parents can afford the fee receipt. Every person. The word in the Greek is tines — some of you in the immediate pastoral reference, but the underlying anthropology is universal.

"You need someone to teach you." The question our institutions must answer honestly is: are we that someone? And for whom?

John 10:10 is our second text: "I have come that they may have life — life in all its fullness."

The Greek is zoe — not bios (biological existence) but the full, qualitative, relational life of God's children. And the word perisson — rendered "in all its fullness" — carries the sense of abundance, of overflow, of life beyond the minimum.

Notice whom Jesus is addressing in John 10. He is not in the Jerusalem Temple, speaking to the educated and the powerful. He is addressing people who have been described in the preceding chapters as the ochlos — the crowd, the unnamed, the socially undifferentiated mass of ordinary people. The blind man who was thrown out of the synagogue in chapter 9. The woman taken in public disgrace. The person whom the religious institution has excluded.

Zoe perisson — abundant life — is the offer Jesus makes precisely to those whom the institutions have excluded. If our schools are primarily serving those who already have bios — comfortable biological existence, social security, economic stability — and are not reaching those for whom zoe perisson would be genuinely transformative, then we are not continuing the mission of John 10:10. We are running a very respectable service organization.

Matthew 25:31–46 is our third and most uncomfortable text.

The judgment scene. You know the text. But I want to draw your attention to a detail that is easy to read past.

The criteria of judgment in Matthew 25 are entirely relational and concrete: food for the hungry, water for the thirsty, welcome for the stranger, clothing for the naked, visit to the sick and the imprisoned. These are not liturgical criteria. They are not doctrinal criteria. They are not academic criteria.

And the most theologically dense moment in the passage is verse 44: "Lord, when did we see you hungry, or thirsty, or a stranger, or naked, or sick, or in prison, and did not minister to you?"

The question from those on the left hand — when did we see you? — reveals the structural sin. They did not fail to serve Christ because they were evil. They failed to see Christ because their entire framework of attention was oriented elsewhere. They were looking in the wrong direction.

The child at the gate without a fee receipt is the one in whom, as Matthew 25:40 tells us, Christ is present: "as you did it to one of the least of these my brothers and sisters, you did it to me." The question our institutions must answer is not only what we do for the child after we let them in. The question is whether our institutional gaze is capable of recognizing Christ in the child we have turned away.

Isaiah 10:1–2 gives us the prophetic diagnosis of what happens when institutions of power stop serving the vulnerable: "Woe to those who make unjust laws, to those who issue oppressive decrees, to deprive the poor of their rights and withhold justice from the oppressed of my people, making widows their prey and robbing the fatherless."

The prophet Isaiah is not speaking of a school. But the structural logic transfers with precision. An institution that was constituted to serve the vulnerable — and that, through accumulated policies of fee structure, admission criteria, and aesthetic preference, has systematically excluded the vulnerable — is, in Isaiah's vocabulary, an institution that has made "unjust laws." The fees policy. The admission criteria. The preference for students who will not affect the rank lists. These are the decrees that Isaiah is naming.

The prophetic tradition does not allow institutions to hide behind good intentions. It judges by consequence.

🔷 PART 3 — THE HISTORICAL DIAGNOSIS: WHEN DID THE REVERSAL HAPPEN? (8 minutes)

Fr. Lobo's method — and it is a method we need to apply here — insists on identifying precisely when a theological distortion entered the tradition. This is not nostalgia. It is diagnosis. And diagnosis requires locating the specific point of inversion.

The reversal in Christian education did not happen in a single moment. But it has an identifiable logic, and that logic can be traced.

In the early period of the missionary congregations in India — the nineteenth and early twentieth centuries — the schools built by religious were genuinely frontier institutions. Sr. Inigo's chapter documents this. They were built in places that had no other educational infrastructure. The founding Sisters, Brothers, and Fathers went where no one else was going. The first students were precisely the children of the poor: tribal children, Dalit children, children of displaced laborers, children of fishing communities.

The reversal began with what sociologists of religion call the "institutionalization of charisma" — a concept that Max Weber analyzed in careful sociological terms. When a charismatic movement becomes an institution, the original drive that created it must be translated into policies, structures, and systems that can survive the death of the founders. And in that process of translation, something is always lost: the willingness to bear the institutional cost of the founding vision.

The founding vision of Catholic education in India was costly. It required going to where there was no infrastructure, no fees, no economic return. It required investing in children whose families could offer nothing in return. It required a long-term, multigenerational patience.

When the institutions became successful — when the schools became known for quality — a new pressure entered: the pressure of financial sustainability. Buildings required maintenance. Teachers required salaries. Government regulations required compliance costs. And the most efficient way to meet these costs was to admit students whose families could pay.

The first fee increase was not a betrayal. It was an adjustment. The second fee increase was still defensible. By the third generation of adjustments, the admission profile had shifted substantially. By the time the profile had shifted beyond fifty percent toward well-off families, no single decision had been the turning point — but the cumulative effect was inversion.

This is what Walter Brueggemann calls "the embrace of royal consciousness" — when the prophetic imagination of a movement surrenders to the administrative logic of the establishment. Brueggemann, in his The Prophetic Imagination, argues that the royal consciousness "achieves amnesia" regarding the founding commitment. It does not renounce the original vision. It simply stops seeing it as urgent. The language of the founding charism survives in the prospectus and on the school crest. The practice has moved entirely elsewhere.

The National Consultation on Catholic Education in India in 2004 named this clearly: education is a mission, but not every type of education is the Christian mission. If our schools empower the already empowered — we are running a profession. We are not running an apostolate.

And this distinction matters with theological precision. A profession serves those who come to it on the profession's terms. An apostolate goes to those who cannot come on any terms at all — and changes its own structure in order to reach them.

🔷 PART 4 — FOUR INSTITUTIONAL MYTHS AND THE THEOLOGICAL RESPONSE (9 minutes)

Now. Having established the problematic, the scriptural case, and the historical diagnosis — I want to address directly the four myths that are used, consciously or unconsciously, to defend the current institutional situation. I call them myths because they have the internal logic of experience, they feel like truths, and they are theologically inadequate.

Myth One: "Poor children cannot cope with a good standard — especially English medium."

This is the myth that sounds most compassionate and is, in practice, most corrosive. Because what it is actually asserting is that the incapacity is located inside the child rather than in the quality of teaching the child receives.

Gravissimum Educationis 1 speaks of every child's right to education that is "adapted to their particular destiny." Not education adapted to institutional convenience. Their particular destiny. This is the theological starting point. If the child is not learning, the first question is not "Can this child learn?" The first question is "Are we teaching adequately?"

The insight of experienced educators working with marginalized communities is consistent: given adequate remedial support, consistent investment, and the expectation of success — children from any background can learn. The failure is pedagogical and institutional. It is not located in the child.

Myth Two: "Good academic results signify a good school."

The state board ranking and the Gospel ranking are not the same list. Gravissimum Educationis 8 calls for education that forms not only the academic capacity but "a genuine sense of responsibility" and the disposition to "contribute to the common good." A school that consistently produces rank-holders who are, as adults, morally asleep — comfortable, entitled, and socially indifferent — has achieved an academic result and failed a theological one.

The question Sr Inigo asks in Chapter 12 is the question every school governing board should be required to answer annually: what is the outcome of our ministry? Not: how many students passed the board examination. Not: how many scholarships did our students receive. But: what kind of people did they become? Did they become the social reformers that Matthew 25 requires? Or did they become successful professionals who have been equipped to join the system rather than to dismantle it?

Myth Three: "The poor will dirty the place and disrupt discipline."

I want to be precise about this myth, because it is almost never stated aloud and is frequently the operating assumption behind admission decisions.

There is a well-documented phenomenon in educational sociology: when institutions begin associating the presence of poor students with a decline in institutional "culture," what they are actually expressing is a preference for the aesthetic and behavioral norms of the already-privileged. The child who arrives without cultural fluency, without the behavioral codes that middle-class schools valorize, without the quiet confidence that comes from a stable home environment — that child is read as a disruption. But theologically: that child is Christ.

Matthew 25:40. I do not need to repeat it.

Sr Cyril of Kolkata — whose educational mission for the poor in this city was one of the most remarkable of the twentieth century — said something that should silence this myth: "If you want silence, go to a graveyard. There you will have perfect silence." A school that achieves its quiet order by excluding the children of the poor is not well-governed. It is curated. And the curation is a theological failure.

Myth Four: "The poor cannot afford the fees, and the fees are necessary for institutional sustainability."

This is the most structurally serious myth, because it contains a genuine financial reality. But Sr Inigo's response is institutional and creative: build corpus funds; seek benefactors specifically for this purpose; examine where resources are currently being spent.

And here is the institutional examination she calls for: compare the money spent on the new tiled corridor, the freshly painted gate, the impressive signboard — with the cost of twenty scholarship seats. The newly tiled corridor is not wrong in itself. It becomes a theological problem when it consumes resources that could have paid the fees of children who are currently standing outside the gate.

Gaudium et Spes 69 speaks of the universal destination of goods — the principle that the goods of creation are meant for all, and that private ownership and institutional resources carry a social mortgage. Applied to our institutions: the building belongs to the mission. The mission is the child at the gate.

🔷 PART 5 — RE-VISIONING: THE PROPHETIC DEMAND (8 minutes)

I want to come now to what Sr Inigo calls "re-visioning" — and I want to be very precise about what this means and what it does not mean.

Re-visioning does not mean closing Catholic schools. Mother Teresa was right: "Don't leave your schools." The schools are needed. In the rural communities of North Bengal, in the adivasi belts of Purulia and Jharkhand, in the Sundarbans flood communities, in the urban slums of Kolkata — the institutional presence of the Church in education is irreplaceable. There is no argument here for withdrawal.

Re-visioning means three things, stated with the precision they require.

First: a structural change in admission policy. The goal is not a token scholarship programme. The goal is a fundamental reorientation of what the school exists for. This means setting a specific, measurable, annually reviewed percentage of students from marginalized communities — and holding the institution accountable to that percentage. It means building in the support structures (remedial coaching, nutrition support, counseling, fee waiver mechanisms) that make genuine inclusion rather than nominal admission possible.

Second: an honest institutional examination of the laity question. Sr Inigo raises something structurally important and institutionally uncomfortable: lay teachers and lay principals are, in many cases, better equipped than religious for the sustained daily work of school administration. They have more undivided professional attention. They are not carrying simultaneous responsibilities of community life, formation, and canonical obligation. Her proposal — bold, and I think theologically sound — is this: elevate talented lay people to leadership roles in Catholic schools, and free consecrated persons to move to the frontier ministries that no one else is doing.

The prison. The trafficking corridor. The adivasi community with no institutional Church presence. The ministry of accompaniment in the slum. These are not being done because the consecrated persons who could do them are managing institutions that a well-formed laity could manage with equal competence and greater sustainability.

Gravissimum Educationis 8 already anticipated this: it speaks of the role of the laity in Catholic education not as auxiliary but as genuinely constitutive. The post-conciliar Church has been slow to act on this. Sixty years after the Council, religious superiors still resist handing governance of schools to laypeople because the institutional trust has not been built. Building that trust is not a concession. It is a mission priority.

Third: a re-orientation of what the school measures as success. Pope Francis, in Evangelii Gaudium 49, writes of the Church's preferential option for the poor as not merely programmatic but hermeneutical — it is the lens through which we read everything. Applied to education: the preferential option for the poor is not a scholarship programme added to an existing system. It is the criterion by which every institutional decision is read. Does this decision bring us closer to the child who has nothing? Or does it take us further away?

Apply that criterion to the last five major decisions of any Catholic school governing board. The fee revision. The new construction project. The capital allocation. The admission criteria for next year. The appointment to a leadership role.

Does this bring us closer to the child at the gate?

That is the question. It is not complicated. It is costly. And the cost is what distinguishes a mission from a profession.

The post-COVID moment is, as Sr Inigo argues, an unexpected opening. The pandemic revealed with absolute clarity that the communities most devastated by educational disruption were the communities that Catholic schools were originally founded to serve: rural children without digital infrastructure, tribal children in areas with no connectivity, girls pulled out of school permanently during lockdown in communities where female education is already structurally devalued.

Female literacy in Bengal lags behind male literacy by more than two decades of accumulated neglect. The adivasi girl in Purulia who never reaches secondary school. The girl in the tea garden community of North Bengal who is told she cannot cope with English medium. The girl whose parents calculate that her marriage is more valuable than her education. These are the students for whom Jesus built the school.

They are waiting. They have always been waiting.

The question that ends Part III of our series — the same question that Moses put to Pharaoh, the same question that Matthew 25 will put to every institution on the last day — is this:

For whom is this here?

The gate of Christian education was built facing the poor. The re-visioning is not a new idea. It is a return to the founding face.

Until and unless our institutions measure their success by what happens to the child without the fee receipt — rather than by what happens to the child who already had everything — the word "mission" on our school crest remains a description of what we once were, not of what we are.

And that gap — between what we profess and what we practice — is the theological problem that this chapter, and this series, refuses to let us escape.

🕯 CLOSING PRAYER (3 minutes)

*Lord Jesus — Teacher who sat not only in the synagogues and on the hillsides but on the ground, at the level of those who had no seat of honor —

You said: I have come that they may have life, life in all its fullness. Not life for the already-full. Not abundance for those who already have enough. Zoe perisson — overflowing life — for the ones who are told they do not qualify, they cannot cope, they do not fit the culture of the institution.

We confess that we have built institutions in your name that have, slowly and with genuinely good intentions, turned their faces away from the child you placed at the center.

We confess the pseudo-satisfaction — the charity galas, the scholarship photographs in the annual report, the language of mission covering a practice of market.

Give us the honesty to look at what we have built and see it as it is, not as it was intended.

Give us the courage to do what re-visioning requires: not cosmetic changes, not new programmes layered over unchanged priorities, but structural transformation — the kind that costs something real.

Show us the face of the child at the gate. The secondhand uniform. The absent fee receipt. The Bengali accent that marks difference. The breakfast not yet eaten.

And let us see in that face what you see: your image. Your presence. Yourself.

Gravissimum Educationis calls education "a genuine right of every person." Let our institutions make that right real.

Not eventually. Now.

Amen.*

📋 SPEAKER NOTES

  • Audience: Priests, Religious Women, Major Seminarians — West Bengal Region
  • Duration: 45 minutes (Final talk of Part III — should feel like theological culmination and prophetic demand simultaneously)
  • Theological Register: This talk synthesizes Fr. Lobo's Jesuit analytical method with the pastoral formation voice appropriate for this audience. The architecture is Lobo's (problematic, thesis, conceptual analysis, scriptural constellation, historical diagnosis, institutional myths, prophetic conclusion) — the texture is recollection-warm, not academic-distant.
  • Key structural moves to execute:
  • The opening problematic must be delivered without irony — the gap between profession and practice is not comic; it is tragic. The warmth comes later.
  • The verb-to-noun move in Part 1 (educating → education) is the single most important analytical moment — take the time to let it land before moving forward.
  • The scriptural constellation (Heb 5:12, Jn 10:10, Mt 25, Is 10) — do not rush through all four. Allow each text to complete its contribution before bringing in the next.
  • The historical diagnosis (Part 3) — the Weber reference on "institutionalization of charisma" and the Brueggemann reference on "royal consciousness" are for speakers who have read these sources; if unfamiliar, the substance can be delivered without the names.
  • The four myths (Part 4) — Myth 3 (the poor will dirty the place) should be named quietly and held quietly. Its ugliness carries its own weight without dramatic amplification.
  • The closing demand (Part 5) — this is not an invitation. It is a demand. Deliver it as such. The warmth belongs in the prayer. The demand belongs in the conclusion.
  • Key questions to leave in the room:
  • "For whom is this here?" — carry this question through the entire talk; it should feel like it has been building when it arrives at the end.
  • "Does this decision bring us closer to the child at the gate?" — practical enough to take home.
  • West Bengal specifics to deploy: Tea garden educational access (North Bengal); adivasi belt (Purulia, Jharkhand border); Sundarbans community disruption post-COVID; female literacy gap in Bengal; the Kolkata slum school access issue; the cultural fluency gap between middle-class institutional norms and first-generation learners.
  • Bridge to Part IV: The final reference to female literacy in Bengal — the adivasi girl in Purulia, the girl in the tea garden — is the natural thread into Chapter 13 (Empowering Women). Part IV begins from the specific face named here.
