Part IV: Empowering Women (Chapters 13–16) begins from the face of the girl who is still waiting at the gate.

Talk 13 — Imago Dei Integra: The Suppressed Half of the Image of God

Towards the Full Equality of Women in Church and Society

45-Minute Recollection for Priests, Religious, and Major Seminarians — West Bengal Region

Primary Anchor — Genesis 1:27

"God created human beings in His own image — male and female He created them."

Secondary Anchor — Luke 10:42

"Mary has chosen the right path — and it will not be taken from her."

Time Guide

For the Speaker

Position in the Series: This is the opening of Part IV and Talk 13. It carries a different energy from the interior talks of Part III. The canvas is as wide as Talk 9 (structural vision), the emotional register is as warm as Talk 6 (mercy), and the prophetic demand is as direct as Talk 7 (prison) and Talk 8 (dignity). Name explicitly at the opening why the series has moved here: the full image of God has not yet been proclaimed if half of it is suppressed.

Tone: Prophetically sober on the diagnosis; genuinely warm on the recovery. The irony (goddess worshipped on Friday, woman dismissed on Sunday) should be named calmly, not angrily — the calm makes it sharper. The Martha and Mary Magdalene sections are moments of genuine theological wonder and should be paced accordingly. For the male audience (priests and seminarians) the tone is challenge delivered with fraternal respect, not accusation.

Key Biblical Texts: Gen 1:26-27; Gen 2:18-23; Jn 4:7-42; Jn 11:1-44; Jn 20:1-18; Lk 10:38-42; Lk 13:10-17; Lk 1:26-38; Jn 2:3-5; Mk 14:3-9; Lk 7:36-50; Rom 16:1-7; Gal 3:26-28; Is 49:14-15; Lk 15:8-10; Prov 31:10-31.

Key Documents: Mulieris Dignitatem (John Paul II, 1988), 6, 16, 30; Gaudium et Spes 29, 60; Lumen Gentium 36, 65; Familiaris Consortio 23; Evangelii Gaudium 103-104; Amoris Laetitia 54; Laudato Si 89-90; FABC Papers 129 (women in Asian Church); Letter of the Apostles, John Paul II apology (1995).

Key Thinkers: Elisabeth Schussler Fiorenza (In Memory of Her, 1983); Phyllis Trible (God and the Rhetoric of Sexuality, 1978); Sandra Schneiders IHM; Mary Daly (early work); B.R. Ambedkar on women's dignity; Rabindranath Tagore (Gitanjali); Mahatma Gandhi on women; Pope Francis (repeated interventions on women's roles); St Thomas Aquinas on Mary Magdalene as Apostola Apostolorum.

West Bengal Specifics: Tagore as Bengal's own prophetic voice on women's freedom; the Shakti tradition of Bengal (Durga Puja, Kali worship) in tension with domestic subordination of women; the Sati tradition abolished by Bengal reformers (Ram Mohan Roy) as precedent for the Church's own reform capacity; the trafficking reality of North Bengal and Murshidabad named in Talk 9 but not yet given its gendered theological analysis.

Opening | 5 Minutes The Problematic

By way of beginning, let me place before you the most foundational theological claim in the Hebrew Bible — and then show you how systematically it has been denied in practice.

Genesis 1:27 is unambiguous: God created human beings in His own image — male and female He created them. Not: male in His image and female as a derivative. Not: male as the primary image and female as its domestic complement. Male and female, together, without hierarchy, without qualification, as the single complete image of the living God.

This is the first thing the Bible says about the human being. Before sin. Before the fall. Before any distinction of role or function or social arrangement. The image of God is, in its Genesis fullness, inescapably both male and female.

We say this. We teach it in our seminaries. We celebrate it in our baptismal theology, where Galatians 3:28 confirms that in Christ there is neither Jew nor Greek, slave nor free, male nor female. We quote Mulieris Dignitatem, John Paul II's 1988 letter on women's dignity, which calls woman an equal partner in humanity with man, bearing equally and in her own way the image of God (Mulieris Dignitatem, 6).

And then we return to our institutions.

We return to diocesan finance councils where women are present, if at all, as a courtesy. We return to seminary formation programmes where gender theology is absent from the curriculum. We return to parishes where women organize the crèche, clean the sacristy, run the catechism programme, staff the pastoral council, and are thanked warmly at the annual general meeting by a parish priest who has not once asked for their theological opinion in ten years of ministry.

We return to the Church of West Bengal, where the Santali, Sora, and adivasi women whose faith is the most visceral, most tested, and most costly in the entire region are the last to be consulted about anything that happens in their own church building.

And — most sharply, because it is the closest to home — we return to religious communities where women religious run schools, hospitals, and formation houses, manage complex institutions with real competence and genuine faithfulness, and have no seat at the table of episcopal decision-making that shapes the Church within which those institutions exist.

This contradiction produces three specific consequences.

First: the Church's proclamation of the Gospel is structurally incomplete. When the half of humanity that bore Christ in her body, stood beneath the cross when the men had fled, and received the first resurrection commission has no authoritative voice in how the Gospel is proclaimed, the Gospel that is proclaimed is half a Gospel. It is the Gospel heard through one set of human ears, shaped by one set of human experiences, and it misses what only the other set of ears and experiences can receive and transmit.

Second: the pastoral credibility of the Church's teaching on human dignity is damaged by internal inconsistency. A Church that teaches the equal dignity of every human person while maintaining institutional arrangements that systematically exclude women from decision-making does not have a credible position from which to address the patriarchal structures of the surrounding culture. It has joined them.

Third: women themselves, formed within institutions that have absorbed patriarchal assumptions as though they were theological necessities, gradually internalize the conviction that their dignity is conditional, that their voice is derivative, and that the most they can hope for is to be useful to a structure that was not designed with them in mind. This interior diminishment is not merely a pastoral problem. It is, theologically, the suppression of the image of God within a living human person.

In one sentence: the Church cannot proclaim the full Gospel until it honours the full image of God. And honouring the full image of God requires the full, equal, structurally supported participation of women in every dimension of its life.

Let us examine what this requires and what is preventing it.

Part 1 | 8 Minutes Two Models of Woman in Church and Culture

Let me identify two fundamentally different models of what woman is and what her role is. They have coexisted within Catholicism for centuries. One is theologically coherent and derives directly from Genesis 1. The other is a cultural importation that has been given theological language it does not deserve.

Model A: Woman as Defined by Function and Relation

In the dominant cultural model, a woman's identity is entirely constituted by her relationships and functions. She is daughter, wife, mother, daughter-in-law. She exists in reference to others. Her worth is measured by the quality of her service to the family unit, and by her capacity to produce male children. Apart from these functions and relationships, she has, as Nirmala Vasanthakumar's workshop reveals, almost nothing left to say about herself.

This model has three sophisticated variants within Catholic institutional life, each of which is more subtle than simple sexism and therefore more resistant to naming and critique.

The first variant is pedestalization. Woman is elevated to a symbol: the Goddess, the Madonna, Bharat Mata, the embodiment of divine feminine energy. This elevation simultaneously removes her from the sphere of ordinary human dignity and redirects attention from her concrete conditions of life to her symbolic function. Bengal worships Durga and Kali with extraordinary intensity for ten days each October, and then returns in November to a social reality in which women in Murshidabad lack basic property rights. The symbol has substituted for the person.

The second variant is spiritualization. The Church acknowledges the dignity of women in theological terms — the imago Dei, baptismal equality, the equal call to holiness — while maintaining that this dignity has no institutional or governance implications. Women are equal before God. The decision-making structure remains entirely male. The spiritual equality is real. The structural exclusion is also real. And the two are maintained simultaneously without apparent awareness of the contradiction.

The third variant is functionalization. Women are genuinely valued — for what they do. For their energy, their organizational capacity, their pastoral sensitivity, their teaching skill, their administrative competence. They are thanked warmly and often. They are rarely asked what they think about the theological questions that shape the institution they are serving.

Model B: Woman as Imago Dei Integra — The Full Image

The second model derives directly from Genesis 1 and is confirmed by the pattern of Jesus's encounters with women throughout the Gospels. In this model, woman is not primarily a function, a symbol, or a relational identity. She is a person: a bearer of the divine image in her own right, with an intellectual, spiritual, theological, and governance capacity that is equal to man's and complementary to it in ways that enrich both.

This model does not deny the differences between men and women, biological, psychological, or social. It denies that those differences constitute a hierarchy. Difference and hierarchy are not the same thing. The tradition that has used difference as a justification for hierarchy has confused a description of variety with a prescription for subordination.

The consequences of Model B, where it has been implemented, are observable. Sr. Inigo SSA spent fourteen years in Tihar Jail. The women who ran the early Church's house churches — Priscilla, Phoebe, Lydia, Junia — built communities that outlasted their own lives. The women mystics of the medieval period — Hildegard of Bingen, Catherine of Siena, Teresa of Avila — shaped the Church's theological and institutional development with an authority that no official position conferred and no official structure could ultimately contain.

Gaudium et Spes 29, the Second Vatican Council's most direct statement on the equality of men and women, names it without evasion: every type of discrimination, whether social or cultural, whether based on sex, race, colour, social condition, language, or religion, is to be overcome and eradicated as contrary to God's intent.

The Council said this in 1965. The question before us is not whether it was said. The question is whether it has been implemented. And the answer, in 2026, sixty years after the Council, is: partially, inconsistently, and nowhere near completely.

Part 2 | 7 Minutes Etymological Recovery: Three Words to Reclaim

Let me attend to three words that are structurally central to this talk and have been domesticated, distorted, or erased in ways that have had direct institutional consequences.

First Word: Imago Dei

The Latin imago Dei, image of God, derives from the Hebrew tselem Elohim in Genesis 1:27. Tselem is a concrete, physical word: it means an image in the sense of a carved figure, a statue, a representation. The imago Dei is not a spiritual metaphor. It is a declaration that the human person, in their bodily, material, gendered existence, is the visible representation of the invisible God in the world.

This is what happened to imago Dei over the course of the tradition: it was progressively spiritualized and then implicitly masculinized. The spiritualization came first through Origen and the Alexandrian tradition: the image of God resides in the soul, not in the body, because God is spirit and spirit alone can bear spirit's image. The body, and especially the female body, is not the location of the imago.

The masculinization followed from the spiritualization. If the image of God resides in the rational soul, and if reason is, as Aristotle argued and the medieval theologians largely accepted, more perfectly realized in the male, then the male human being is more fully the image of God than the female. Thomas Aquinas, working within this framework, wrote that woman is a misbegotten male — mas occasionatus — a defective version of the primary human type (Aquinas, Summa Theologiae I, q. 92, a. 1).

This is not a marginal opinion from a minor thinker. It is the position of the most influential systematic theologian in the Western tradition, repeated in his commentary on the Sentences, and its effects on the Church's self-understanding of women's capacity for theological reason and institutional authority have been incalculable.

The etymological recovery of imago Dei requires returning to Genesis 1:27 and refusing the Aristotelian overlay. Tselem Elohim is given to the human being as male and female together. Not to the male alone. Not more fully to the male. Together. Equally. Completely.

Second Word: Ezer

Genesis 2:18 uses a word for woman that has been translated helper since the earliest English versions and has been read, in that translation, as indicating a secondary or supporting role. God says: it is not good for the man to be alone; I will make him a helper as his partner.

The Hebrew word is ezer. And ezer is not the word for a subordinate assistant. In the twenty-one times ezer appears in the Hebrew Bible, it is used twice to describe woman in Genesis 2, and nineteen times to describe God. God is Israel's ezer. God is the ezer of the king. God is the ezer of the one who cries out in distress.

When the psalmist writes: my help, my ezer, comes from the Lord (Psalm 121:2), he is not describing a subordinate relationship. He is describing a relationship in which the stronger party provides what the weaker party cannot provide for themselves. The ezer is the one whose presence makes possible what would otherwise be impossible.

If ezer is consistently used of God throughout the Hebrew Bible, and if God is described as Israel's ezer in contexts of military rescue, political crisis, and existential threat, then the use of ezer for woman in Genesis 2 cannot mean assistant or subordinate without making God a subordinate assistant to Israel. The word does not carry that meaning in its Hebrew semantic range.

Woman, in Genesis 2, is described by the same word used for God. This is not a concession. It is a declaration. The theological tradition that derived from this text a justification for women's subordination was not reading the Hebrew. It was reading its own cultural assumptions into the Hebrew.

Third Word: Apostola

The Latin apostola, the feminine form of apostolus, sender, one who is sent, is the title that Thomas Aquinas gave to Mary Magdalene in the thirteenth century. Apostola Apostolorum: the Apostle of the Apostles. The one sent to the sent ones. The first witness of the resurrection, commissioned by the Risen Christ himself to carry the news to the eleven.

The word apostola was also applicable to Junia in Romans 16:7, where Paul greets a couple he describes as outstanding among the apostles. The feminine name Junia was, as the textual scholar Bernadette Brooten established in 1977 through exhaustive analysis of ancient naming practices, the original reading of the Greek manuscripts. A medieval scribe, working in the twelfth or thirteenth century, added an iota to the ending and changed Junia to the masculine Junias — a name for which there is no evidence in any ancient source, Greek, Latin, or otherwise.

The name was changed. The apostola was erased. And the erasure held for approximately eight centuries, until twentieth-century scholarship recovered the original text and the original woman.

Part 3 | 12 Minutes The Scriptural Constellation

Let me now read several scriptural texts together, building a picture of what Jesus actually did with the women he encountered — and what the early Church actually looked like before the patriarchal assumptions of Greco-Roman culture began their work of reconstruction.

Text 1 — Genesis 1:27 and 2:18-23: Two Creation Texts, One Theological Claim

Genesis 1:27 establishes equality. Genesis 2:18-23 develops relationship. Together they give us the complete theological foundation.

In Genesis 2, the creation of woman takes place while Adam is in a deep sleep. The narrative detail is theologically loaded. Adam does not participate in the decision. He does not select from a catalogue of options. He does not give permission. God acts, and when Adam wakes he recognizes in the woman not a creation he possesses but a person who is bone of his bone and flesh of his flesh (Genesis 2:23). The language of identity, of identical substance, of radical kinship.

The feminist biblical scholar Phyllis Trible, in God and the Rhetoric of Sexuality (1978), argues that the traditional reading of Genesis 2 as establishing male precedence and female derivation is a misreading on multiple counts. The adam of Genesis 2 before the creation of woman is not yet a gendered being: the earth creature becomes fully human only in relation to another who is equally human (Trible, God and the Rhetoric of Sexuality, 1978, ch. 4). Woman is not created from a pre-existing gendered male. She is created from the undifferentiated human creature, and in her creation both become what they are: gendered, relational, equal.

Text 2 — Luke 13:10-17: The Daughter of Abraham

A woman has been bent double for eighteen years. She is in the synagogue. In first-century Jewish practice, women sat separately, in a section at the back or along the side, not among the main congregation. She has not approached Jesus. She has made no request.

Jesus sees her. He calls her forward. He lays his hands on her. And he says: Woman, you are set free from your ailment (Luke 13:12).

The synagogue leader objects — to the Sabbath violation, not to the miracle itself. Jesus responds: you hypocrites, do you not untie your ox or donkey from its stall on the Sabbath to lead it to water? And this woman, a daughter of Abraham whom Satan has kept bound for eighteen years, ought she not to be set free from this bondage on the Sabbath day? (Luke 13:15-16).

Daughter of Abraham. This title had no precedent in Jewish tradition. Sons of Abraham, yes — the male heirs of the covenant. Daughter of Abraham is Jesus's own creation, his deliberate extension of the covenant's full promise to a woman in a culture that had quietly assumed the promise was male. He gives her a theological status the tradition has not given her. He gives her a name for who she is before God, not only who she is to the household.

Text 3 — John 4:7-42: The Theological Conversation Jesus Should Not Have Had

The encounter at the well in John 4 breaks every protocol simultaneously. Jesus is Jewish; she is Samaritan. Jesus is male; she is female. Jesus is respectable; she is the woman who comes to the well at noon, when no one else is there, which is its own statement about her social standing.

The disciples return and find him talking with a woman. The text notes their astonishment and then records that none of them asked why he was speaking with her (John 4:27). The question would have been reasonable by their cultural standards. They do not ask it. Which suggests that by this point in their experience of Jesus, the question has already been rendered unavailable by repeated observation.

The conversation that precedes the disciples' return is the longest single conversation Jesus has with any individual in all four Gospels. He discusses worship, the nature of God, the distinction between true and false religious practice, and his own identity. He reveals himself as the I AM — the name of God — to this woman before he reveals it to any of his disciples. She becomes the first evangelist in John: she returns to the city, she tells everyone what has happened, and it is her testimony that triggers the first mass conversion recorded in the Gospel.

The Apostola Apostolorum is not the only woman in John's Gospel with an apostolic commission. The Samaritan woman, unnamed, is the first evangelist in John's Gospel. Jesus chose for this first evangelism a woman with a complicated past, from a people the Jews despised, who had come to the well alone because she was not welcome among her own community. He chose her specifically.

Text 4 — John 11:21-27: Martha's Confession

Lazarus is dead. Jesus arrives four days after the burial. Martha runs to meet him on the road. She is not gentle. She says: Lord, if you had been here, my brother would not have died (John 11:21).

Jesus responds: your brother will rise again. Martha demonstrates that she understands the conventional theology: I know that he will rise again in the resurrection on the last day (John 11:24). And Jesus then delivers the most cosmologically significant self-revelation in the entire Gospel of John: I am the resurrection and the life (John 11:25).

He delivers this to Martha. On a dusty road outside Bethany. In response to a woman's grief and a woman's challenge. Not in the Temple. Not to the twelve. Not in a formal theological setting. To a grieving woman who ran to meet him and told him honestly what she felt.

And Martha responds with a confession of faith that is the theological equal of Peter's confession at Caesarea Philippi: Yes, Lord, I believe that you are the Christ, the Son of God, the one coming into the world (John 11:27). Peter's confession is in Matthew 16:16: You are the Christ, the Son of the living God. The content is identical. The occasion is equally significant. Martha is the first person in John's Gospel to make a complete confession of Christian faith.

The tradition remembers Peter's confession. The tradition does not remember Martha's with comparable weight. This is not because John records it with less gravity. It is because the tradition that transmitted and interpreted these texts had already decided that Peter's voice was normative and Martha's was incidental.

Text 5 — John 20:1-18: The First Commission of the Risen Christ

On the morning of the resurrection, Mary Magdalene comes to the tomb while it is still dark. She finds the stone rolled away. She runs to Peter and the beloved disciple, who come, look, and return home (John 20:10). Mary remains. Weeping. Outside the tomb.

Jesus appears. She does not recognize him at first. Then he calls her by name: Mary. And in that moment of recognition — the Good Shepherd calling his own sheep by name (John 10:3) — the encounter that will change the world begins.

Jesus gives her the commission: Do not hold on to me, because I have not yet ascended to the Father. But go to my brothers and say to them, I am ascending to my Father and your Father, to my God and your God (John 20:17). Go. And say to them. These are the words of an apostolic commission. Go and tell. This is precisely what Jesus says to the apostles when he sends them out. He says it first, and explicitly, to Mary Magdalene.

Thomas Aquinas, whose theology of women was in other respects shaped by the Aristotelian framework we examined above, could not avoid what John 20 says. He wrote, in his commentary on this passage: Mary Magdalene was made the apostle of the apostles because Christ chose her, in preference to the male disciples, to announce the good news of the resurrection to those who would become the foundation of the Church (Aquinas, Commentary on John, ch. 20, lect. 3).

Apostola Apostolorum. Thomas Aquinas said it in the thirteenth century. Pope Francis elevated her feast day to the rank of the apostles in 2016. The seven centuries between those two moments are a measure of the institutional inertia that separates theological recognition from structural consequence.

Text 6 — Romans 16:1-7 and Galatians 3:28: The Early Church That Was Erased

Paul's letter to the Romans closes with a list of greetings that reads, when its implications are taken seriously, like a description of a Church that the subsequent tradition chose not to remember. Phoebe: a deacon of the church at Cenchreae, a benefactor of many, including Paul himself (Romans 16:1-2). Prisca (Priscilla), named before her husband Aquila in four of six biblical references, who risked her neck for Paul and runs a house church (Romans 16:3-5). Mary, who worked very hard among you (Romans 16:6). Junia, outstanding among the apostles (Romans 16:7).

Elisabeth Schussler Fiorenza, in In Memory of Her (1983), argues that the early Jesus movement was, in its structural reality, a discipleship of equals: women and men in a community of mutual service and shared mission, the social arrangements of the surrounding Greco-Roman world deliberately subverted in the name of a different social logic (Schussler Fiorenza, In Memory of Her, 1983, ch. 4). The Pauline communities, at their best, embodied this vision. The post-Pauline letters, particularly the Pastoral Epistles, begin the process of accommodation to the Greco-Roman household codes that will eventually produce the institutional structure we have inherited.

Galatians 3:28 is Paul at his most foundational: There is neither Jew nor Greek, slave nor free, male nor female; for all of you are one in Christ Jesus. This is the baptismal formula of the early Church. It is the theological claim that is logically prior to every other claim about Christian community. And its implications for the role of women in the Church have been, for seventeen centuries, systematically not followed.

Part 4 | 8 Minutes The Historical Diagnosis: How Half the Image Was Suppressed

The suppression of women's full participation in the Church's life is not a single event. It is a process with identifiable stages, each of which built upon the previous and produced the structure we have inherited. Naming this process is not an act of anti-Church polemic. It is the prerequisite for honest reform.

Stage One: The Philosophical Inheritance — Aristotle in the Baptistery

The early Church existed within a culture whose dominant philosophical framework, Aristotle's, defined woman as a defective male, a misbegotten human being whose purpose was the reproduction of the species and the management of the household. This was not a fringe view. It was the consensus of Greek philosophical anthropology, and it shaped the social and legal arrangements of the entire Greco-Roman world.

The Church Fathers did not receive the Scriptures in a cultural vacuum. They received them in a culture that already knew what women were for. And while some Fathers, notably in their theology of the soul's equality before God, resisted the Aristotelian framework, the dominant trajectory moved toward accommodation. Tertullian's description of woman as the gateway of the devil — woman, you are the devil's doorway — is extreme and was not universally accepted. But the underlying assumption that women's bodies were a source of disorder and that their intellectual capacity was inferior to men's was sufficiently widespread to shape the emerging institutional Church's practical arrangements.

Stage Two: The Gregorian Reform — Closing the House Churches

The house churches of the early centuries gave women legitimate leadership space. Priscilla, Lydia, Phoebe: women whose homes were the meeting places of early Christian communities, who exercised a de facto pastoral leadership that the community recognized and Paul acknowledged. These were not exceptions to the rule. They were the structure of early Christian community.

The Gregorian Reform of the eleventh century, which sought to regularize and centralize the Church's life, also progressively clericalised its leadership. Leadership became synonymous with ordination. Ordination was male. And the house church, in which a woman could exercise recognized pastoral authority by virtue of her hospitality and her faith, was absorbed into a parochial structure in which authority was formally clerical and therefore formally male.

The reform was necessary in many of its dimensions: it addressed real corruptions, established real disciplines, and laid the groundwork for medieval Christendom's institutional coherence. But its unintended consequence was the systematic exclusion of women from the structures of recognized leadership that the house church had provided.

Stage Three: The Indian Compounding — Patriarchy Received, Patriarchy Amplified

In India, the Church received a culture whose patriarchal structures were, in certain respects, more deeply entrenched than those of Mediterranean Christianity. The laws of Manu, the institution of sati, the practice of child marriage, the identification of woman with inauspiciousness in widowhood: these were not peripheral features of Indian culture. They were central to the social arrangements of the Hindu majority and, by cultural osmosis, affected the Christian communities that lived within them.

The missionary model brought European patriarchy. The Indian context offered indigenous patriarchy. The encounter between the two did not produce a critique of either. It produced an amplification of both, with the added complication that certain forms of Indian patriarchy were given a spurious theological dignity by being described as cultural respect rather than structural exclusion.

Bengal itself contains the irony in its sharpest form. The tradition of Shakti worship, of Durga and Kali as supreme divine power, coexists with a social reality in which women of the Santali and Rajbongshi communities exercise no legal ownership of land, in which the girl child continues to be aborted in the Murshidabad district at rates that the Health Commission has described as a public health crisis, and in which the trafficking of women from North Bengal to Kolkata and Mumbai continues to be facilitated by networks that the Church has named in its social justice documents and not yet structurally opposed in its pastoral priorities.

John Paul II, in his 1995 Letter to Women, wrote something that deserves to be quoted in full and then followed up institutionally: I am sorry that many women, in many circumstances, have not been respected and honoured. If objective blame, especially in particular historical contexts, has belonged to not just a few members of the Church, I am truly sorry for this (John Paul II, Letter to Women, 1995, no. 3). An apology is a beginning. Implementation is the substance.

Part 5 | 5 Minutes The Prophetic Demand: Institutional and Personal

I want to state the demand of this talk with the same precision that Sr. Inigo brings to her ten institutional proposals. The demand is not vague encouragement toward better attitudes. It is specific, structural, and addressed to named groups within this room.

For the Institutional Church: Ten Non-Negotiable Steps

First: introduce gender theology as a mandatory component of seminary and novitiate formation programmes. Not an elective. Not an occasional lecture. A structural part of the formation curriculum, taught by women theologians who speak from their own experience of the tradition.

Second: provide scholarships specifically for women in biblical and systematic theology. The feminine understanding of God is a missing dimension of the Church's proclamation. The solution is not to note this regretfully at conferences. The solution is to fund the women whose vocation is to provide it.

Third: include women as co-leaders in diocesan councils, parish governance structures, and institutional decision-making bodies. Evangelii Gaudium 103 is clear: the presence of women in ecclesial and social contexts must be ensured. The word ensure implies structural commitment, not goodwill.

Fourth: sensitize male religious and seminarians on gender equality through workshops, formation inputs, and ongoing accountability structures. The cultural patriarchy that seminarians bring from their families, communities, and cultures does not evaporate upon entry to the seminary. It requires deliberate, sustained, structurally supported formation.

Fifth: announce from the pulpit, specifically and repeatedly, that discrimination against women is a sin. Not a social problem. Not a cultural reality to be gradually addressed. A sin. Which means it is a matter for the confessional as well as the committee.

Sixth: involve women in the ministry of the Word. The Samaritan woman was the first evangelist. Martha was the first to confess full Christian faith in John's Gospel. Mary Magdalene was commissioned to tell the apostles. The feminine theological voice is not a supplement to proclamation. It is a constitutive element of it. Its absence is a deformation of the Gospel.

Seventh: use inclusive language in liturgy and theological education. God is not only Father. God is the woman who searches for her lost coin (Luke 15:8). God is the mother who cannot forget the child she nursed (Isaiah 49:15). These images are scriptural. They belong in our homilies, our catechesis, and our prayer.

Eighth: address the trafficking of women from North Bengal and Murshidabad as a Church priority, not only through social work agencies but through the prophetic naming of the economic and cultural structures that make trafficking possible — including structures of female expendability within which it operates.

Ninth: collaborate with women's rights movements at the grassroots level, as Sr. Inigo proposes. The women who are working on the ground in Birbhum, in the Santali communities, in the tea garden belts of the Jalpaiguri foothills — they do not need the Church to supervise them. They need the Church to walk alongside them.

Tenth: review our community constitutions and governance structures specifically for the ways in which they have encoded patriarchal assumptions as though they were theological necessities. Vita Consecrata 73 calls consecrated life to be a prophetic sign. A community whose governance excludes the full participation of women is not a prophetic sign of the Kingdom. It is a reflection of the culture that the Kingdom is meant to transform.

For Women: The Claim That Cannot Wait for Permission

The institutional conversion is necessary. And it will be slow. In the meantime, the demand that this talk makes of the women in this room is the one that Sr. Inigo makes in Chapter 13 with characteristic directness: claim your dignity. Not when the institution finally grants it. Now.

Nirmala Vasanthakumar's workshop revealed that ninety percent of women, asked to describe themselves without reference to others, had almost nothing to say. The formation that produces that result is deep, multigenerational, and reinforced by every cultural structure the woman inhabits. Reversing it requires a deliberate act of re-formation: learning to say I exist, I think, I have theological convictions, I am the image of God — not because my father says so, not because my congregation says so, not because the bishop says so, but because Genesis 1:27 says so and Jesus confirmed it in every conversation he had with every woman who came to him.

Until and unless the Church honours the full image of God — structurally, in its governance, its theology, its proclamation, and its pastoral priorities — it will continue to preach a half-Gospel to a whole world. The word of Jesus stands: she has chosen the right part. And no one will take it from her.

Rabindranath Tagore, in Gitanjali, wrote of a heaven of freedom toward which his country should awake. Bengal knows that line. It belongs to this soil. Sr. Inigo paraphrases it for us, and I want to end Part 5 with her paraphrase:

Into that heaven of freedom, our Father, our Mother — let women and men awake. Together. As one image. Of one God.

Personal Reflection | 2 Minutes Silence One Question — Two Versions

I want to give you one question in two versions, depending on who you are.

For the women in this room: Where, specifically, in your own life and your own community, have you accepted a definition of your worth that comes from the culture rather than from Genesis 1:27? And what would change if you refused it?

For the men in this room: Where, specifically, in your parish, your seminary, your diocese, your congregation, is the full image of God being suppressed by structural arrangements that you have the power to change — and have not changed?

[Two minutes of silence]

Closing Prayer | 3 Minutes

Lord Jesus, who created us in your image — male and female, whole and undivided —

You who spoke to the Samaritan woman when the disciples would have passed her by. You who called the bent woman a daughter of Abraham in a synagogue that had never given her that name. You who gave Martha the I AM revelation on a dusty road outside Bethany. You who stood in an empty garden and said: Mary. And in saying her name, gave the resurrection its first human voice.

We confess the centuries. The scribe who changed Junia's name because a female apostle was too inconvenient to transmit. The philosopher whose authority we received into our theology and whose diminishment of women we did not examine for a thousand years. The institutions that worship the Goddess in the sanctuary and dismiss the woman in the governance meeting. The seminary that teaches the imago Dei in the classroom and does not notice its absence in the curriculum committee.

Raise up, in West Bengal, in the seminaries and the congregations and the parishes and the diocesan offices, the Priscillas who teach theology with authority. The Marthas who confess faith without apology. The Marys who choose the better part and are not moved from it.

And raise up the Aquilas: the men who are secure enough in their own identity to walk alongside gifted women rather than in front of them. The bishops who read Galatians 3:28 and ask what it requires of their governance. The formation directors who put women theologians on the faculty and not only on the cleaning roster.

For the woman in Murshidabad whose wages were stolen. For the Santali woman whose land was taken. For the trafficked girl from North Bengal whose dignity was sold. For the religious sister who runs the institution and is thanked for her service and not asked for her theology.

She has chosen the right part.

No one will take it from her.

Into that heaven of freedom, our Father, our Mother — let women and men awake. Together. As one image. Of one God.

Amen.

Growing Series Arc — Thirteen Talks

Part IV: Empowering Women

Talk 13 opens Part IV with the foundational theological claim: the image of God is male and female together, and the suppression of women's full participation in the Church's life is a deformation of the Gospel. Talks 14, 15, and 16 will develop the specific dimensions of this claim — the spirituality of women's empowerment, the confrontation with trafficking, and the vision of the fully renewed Church.

Morning Star Regional Seminary | Barrackpore, West Bengal

Fr. William Anthony MSFS | Talk 13: Imago Dei Integra

Talk 14 — The Magnificat as Social Manifesto: Women's Dignity as Theological Category and Ecclesial Obligation

"All generations shall call me blessed." (Luke 1:48)

⏱ TIME GUIDE

Section Duration
Opening: The Problematic of Ceremonial Equality 7 min
Part I — Two Models of Women's Dignity: Ceremonial and Structural 10 min
Part II — Etymological Recovery: What Megalynei and Tapeinōsis Actually Claim 8 min
Part III — The Scriptural Constellation 9 min
Part IV — The Historical Diagnosis and the Indian Paradigm Shift 8 min
Prophetic Demand & Silence 3 min

OPENING: THE PROBLEMATIC OF CEREMONIAL EQUALITY

(7 minutes)

By way of introducing this fourteenth reflection in our series, let me begin with a theological affirmation that commands virtually universal assent — and then with a reality that contradicts it in ways so structurally embedded that they have become largely invisible.

We say: woman and man are equally made in the image of God. Genesis 1:27 is unambiguous — "male and female he created them" — and the image of God is not distributed between the sexes in unequal portions. The theological anthropology of the creation narrative places no qualification on the imago Dei as it applies to women. Paul, in Galatians 3:28, draws the full New Covenant consequence: "There is neither Jew nor Greek, there is neither slave nor free, there is neither male nor female, for you are all one in Christ Jesus." And the Second Vatican Council in Gaudium et Spes 29 states explicitly that "every type of discrimination, whether social or cultural, whether based on sex, race, color, social condition, language, or religion, is to be overcome and eradicated as contrary to God's intent."

That is the claim.

And now the reality.

Every year, on March 8, religious communities across West Bengal and India observe International Women's Day. Masses are offered for women. Special blessings are given. Women's groups do the readings. Roses are distributed. Warm words are spoken about the irreplaceable dignity and contribution of women to the family, the Church, and the society. And on March 9 — the structural conditions that occasioned the celebration are, in the overwhelming majority of institutions, entirely unchanged. The same decision-making rooms have the same demographics. The same formation programmes carry the same unexamined assumptions about the proper sphere of women's authority. The same community cultures practice the same subordinations, sometimes gently, sometimes not.

The consequences of this gap — between the annual celebration and the structural reality — are not trivial.

First: the Church's moral credibility on questions of women's dignity in the broader society is compromised by the gap between its doctrinal affirmation and its institutional practice. A Church that proclaims the equal imago Dei of women while maintaining structures that functionally subordinate women cannot speak with prophetic authority on the full range of women's rights questions — trafficking, domestic violence, wage equality, political representation — without the world hearing the structural contradiction beneath the proclamation.

Second: the women within our communities and institutions absorb the message of the structural reality as surely as they hear the message of the annual celebration. And the structural message is frequently: your dignity is affirmed in principle and limited in practice. You are celebrated one day per year and managed the other three hundred and sixty-four.

Third: and this is the most theologically searching consequence — the woman who has internalized the structural subordination of her community, and who now perpetuates it toward women under her own authority, is the evidence that oppression does not require an external oppressor. Once it has been sufficiently normalized, it reproduces itself from within. Sr Inigo names this reality directly: women have sometimes aided in perpetuating the very structures that oppress them — not from malice but from the comprehensiveness of a formation that presented subordination as natural order.

In one sentence, if I were to state the thesis of this reflection: The Church's celebration of women's dignity will remain ceremonial rather than structural until it is grounded in the full theological weight of the Magnificat — Mary's declaration of the overthrow of every arrangement that diminishes the human person — and until that theological grounding produces specific, institutional, non-cosmetic change in the communities that sing it every evening at Vespers.

Let me develop this across four movements.

PART I — TWO MODELS OF WOMEN'S DIGNITY: CEREMONIAL AND STRUCTURAL

(10 minutes)

There are two operative models of women's dignity in the contemporary Church — and indeed in contemporary society — that produce radically different institutional and pastoral realities. The difference between them is not the sincerity with which dignity is affirmed. Both models affirm women's dignity. The difference is the depth at which the affirmation is allowed to operate.

I shall call them the ceremonial model and the structural model.

The ceremonial model understands the affirmation of women's dignity as a matter of recognition, celebration, and rhetorical emphasis. In this model, the Church honours women by naming their contributions, venerating their exemplars — particularly Mary and the women saints — conducting special observances, and ensuring that language about women is respectful and appreciative. The ceremonial model is not insincere. It is genuinely convinced of what it affirms. But it locates the affirmation at the level of sentiment and symbol rather than at the level of structure and power.

The specific consequence of the ceremonial model is what I would call the dignity gap: the distance between the elevation of women in symbol — Mary as Queen of Heaven, woman as the heart of the family and the Church, the irreplaceable gifts of the feminine genius — and the exclusion of women from the structural processes by which the Church governs itself, makes its decisions, allocates its resources, and shapes its institutional culture. The ceremonial model can sustain an extraordinarily high rhetoric of women's dignity while presiding over an institutional reality in which women have no voice in the rooms where consequential decisions are made.

And the March 8 rose distribution is its liturgical expression: a genuine gesture of appreciation that, by its very ceremonial containment, releases the remaining three hundred and sixty-four days from the obligation of structural change.

Pope Saint John Paul II — who developed an extensive theology of the genius femininus and who wrote a deeply appreciative apostolic letter Mulieris Dignitatem on the dignity and vocation of women — was nonetheless capable of the honest admission, in his 1995 Letter to Women, that "women's dignity has often been unacknowledged, undervalued, and ignored." The admission is significant precisely because it comes from the author of an eloquent theology of women's dignity. The gap between the eloquence and the institutional reality was something he felt compelled to name.

The structural model takes the theological affirmation of women's dignity seriously enough to allow it to interrogate the institutional arrangements of the communities that profess it. In this model, the question is not merely: do we celebrate women? The question is: do the structures of our community — its decision-making processes, its allocation of authority and responsibility, its formation practices, its culture of relationship — actually embody the equal imago Dei that we affirm in principle?

The structural model does not require that all institutional differentiation be abolished. It requires that every institutional arrangement be evaluated against the theological criterion of the equal dignity of the human person — and that arrangements which fail this evaluation be named as failures and changed, rather than justified by appeal to tradition, natural law, or the complementarity of roles.

Sr Inigo's framework from the National Conference of Religious India — the paradigm shift from "inward-looking women withdrawn from the world" to "women with a global vision for prophetic mission," and from "appendix-daughter of a colonial mother" to "consecrated woman of and for her Motherland" — is a structural programme, not a ceremonial one. It calls for the reorganization of the institutional self-understanding, the mission priorities, and the formation culture of religious women in India. It is asking: what does it mean for a religious woman to be genuinely Indian, genuinely rooted, genuinely prophetic — rather than a faithful replication of a European template that was never designed for this soil?

This is the shift from the ceremonial to the structural model. And it is a shift that the Magnificat demands.

PART II — ETYMOLOGICAL RECOVERY: WHAT MEGALYNEI AND TAPEINŌSIS ACTUALLY CLAIM

(8 minutes)

Let me now perform the etymological recovery that this argument requires — returning the Magnificat to its original verbal and social force, before centuries of liturgical domestication converted it from a social manifesto into a devotional text.

The Magnificat opens with "My soul magnifies the Lord" — megalynei in Greek, from megas (great) and the verbal suffix lynō (to make, to cause to be). The soul of Mary makes great the Lord — it amplifies, it enlarges, it gives visible expression to a reality that might otherwise remain invisible. The Magnificat is not a private act of personal gratitude. It is a public declaration — an enlargement of the divine reality into the social and political space.

And the social content of that enlargement is named with precision in Luke 1:51-53: "He has shown strength with his arm; he has scattered the proud in the thoughts of their hearts. He has brought down the powerful from their thrones, and lifted up the lowly; he has filled the hungry with good things, and sent the rich away empty." These are not metaphors for spiritual states. They are social realities — the redistribution of power, the reversal of the arrangements that concentrate privilege and deprivation along the same social fault-lines as every unjust society.

But the word I want to recover is tapeinōsis — usually translated as "lowly estate" or "humble condition" — in Luke 1:48: "He has looked with favour on the tapeinōsis of his servant." The Greek tapeinōsis means, literally, a state of humiliation — not the spiritual virtue of humility, but the social condition of one who has been pressed down, subordinated, made small by the operation of social arrangements that assign lower status by virtue of birth, gender, or class.

Mary is not celebrating her spiritual humility. She is naming her social condition — the condition of a young woman in first-century Palestine, with no legal standing, no political voice, no economic independence, and a pregnancy that placed her at the mercy of her betrothed and her community. And she is declaring that God has seen that condition — has looked with favour on the tapeinōsis — and has chosen, from within it, to inaugurate the overthrow of every social arrangement that produces it.

The Magnificat is sung every evening at Vespers in communities across the world. It has been so thoroughly absorbed into the liturgical texture of the Church that its social radicalism has been largely neutralized. We hear the words. We do not hear what they are saying.

What they are saying is this: the God who looked upon Mary's tapeinōsis sees the tapeinōsis of every woman who has been pressed down, subordinated, made small — by the operation of social arrangements in her family, her community, her Church. And the divine response to that tapeinōsis is not spiritual consolation. It is structural reversal.

Until the communities that sing the Magnificat allow its tapeinōsis logic to interrogate their own institutional arrangements — to ask "where are women being pressed down by the operation of structures we maintain?" — they are singing a revolutionary text as a devotional exercise. They have converted a verb into a noun: megalynei, the active making-great of God's justice, into Magnificat, a liturgical category harmlessly contained within Vespers.

PART III — THE SCRIPTURAL CONSTELLATION

(9 minutes)

I want to bring four scriptural texts into this argument — each adding a dimension the others do not supply.

The first witness: Luke 1:46-55 — the Magnificat entire.

The Magnificat must be read whole and read politically before it can be read devotionally. Mary is not a passive recipient of divine favour who expresses her gratitude in elevated poetry. She is a young woman in a politically occupied, economically stratified, gender-hierarchical society who has received, in the Annunciation, a vocation of world-historical consequence — and who responds to that vocation with a declaration of what God's entry into history means for every social arrangement that diminishes the human person.

The structure of the Magnificat is significant: it moves from the personal ("my soul magnifies") to the cosmic ("he has scattered the proud") to the political ("he has brought down the powerful") to the economic ("he has filled the hungry"). This is not an accidental sequence. It is a theological argument: God's action in Mary is the particular instance of God's universal pattern of intervention on behalf of those in tapeinōsis — and that pattern has consequences for every level of human social organization.

Walter Brueggemann notes that the prophetic imagination always contains both a critical and an energizing function: it names what is wrong in the present arrangement, and it opens a vision of what God intends instead. The Magnificat does both simultaneously. It names the arrangements that keep the humble down and the powerful enthroned. And it announces their overthrow — not as a future aspiration but as a present reality already inaugurated in the divine choice of Mary.

The second witness: Galatians 3:28.

"There is neither Jew nor Greek, there is neither slave nor free, there is neither male nor female, for you are all one in Christ Jesus."

Paul is not making a statement about the irrelevance of difference. He is making a statement about the irrelevance of hierarchy — the ordering of persons into superiors and inferiors on the basis of birth, class, or gender. The distinctions remain. The hierarchy is abolished in Christ.

The history of the interpretation of this text within the institutional Church has been a history of progressive narrowing: the abolition of Jewish-Gentile hierarchy was accepted; the abolition of slave-free hierarchy took eighteen centuries of moral struggle; the abolition of male-female hierarchy remains, in many of its institutional expressions, incomplete. The interpretive principle that allowed the earlier hierarchies to be abolished — the equal dignity of persons before God — is the same principle that the remaining hierarchy cannot ultimately withstand.

The third witness: Luke 8:1-3.

"The twelve were with him, as well as some women who had been cured of evil spirits and infirmities: Mary, called Magdalene... and Joanna... and Susanna, and many others, who provided for them out of their resources."

This text is almost never preached as a text about women's ministry. It is mentioned in passing as background to the preaching tours of Jesus. But its structural significance is considerable: the women are named, they are travelling with Jesus and the Twelve as a named part of the movement, and they are funding it — "out of their resources." The mission of Jesus was financially sustained by women. The movement that became the Church was literally resourced by women whose names Luke carefully preserves.

And Mary Magdalene — named first among the women here, and given by John's Gospel the first resurrection appearance and the commission to proclaim the resurrection to the Eleven — is, in the most literal sense, the apostle to the apostles. The resurrection of Jesus, the central event of Christian faith, was first communicated by a woman, to men who did not believe her (Luke 24:11: "these words seemed to them an idle tale, and they did not believe them").

The institutional consequences of this pattern in Jesus's own practice — women named, resourced, commissioned — have been drawn with remarkable inconsistency across the tradition.

The fourth witness: John 4:7-26 — the Samaritan woman.

Jesus's conversation with the Samaritan woman at the well is his longest recorded conversation in the Fourth Gospel. His interlocutor is a woman, a Samaritan (twice marginalized), and someone whose personal history is publicly known and would have placed her outside the circle of respectable religious discourse. And Jesus engages her — not as a pastoral case, not as an object of mercy — as a theological interlocutor. It is to her, before anyone else in the Gospel of John, that he reveals himself explicitly as the Messiah: "I am he, the one who is speaking to you" (John 4:26).

The person who receives the most explicit Christological self-disclosure in the Fourth Gospel, before the passion narrative, is a Samaritan woman of compromised social standing. This is not accidental. It is the Magnificat logic operating in the specific texture of Jesus's ministry: God looks with favour on the tapeinōsis — and speaks, to precisely those the dominant social order has made most small, the most profound words.

PART IV — THE HISTORICAL DIAGNOSIS AND THE INDIAN PARADIGM SHIFT

(8 minutes)

The historical diagnosis I want to offer is this: the gap between the Church's theological affirmation of women's equal dignity and its institutional practice is not the product of individual failure or malice. It is the product of a specific, historically locatable theological synthesis that imported into Christian anthropology the hierarchical gender assumptions of Graeco-Roman social philosophy — and then generated theological justifications for those assumptions that had the effect of subordinating a theological datum (the equal imago Dei) to a cultural inheritance (the natural inferiority of women).

Aristotle, in the Politics and the Generation of Animals, held that women were, by nature, incomplete men — defective in reason, emotion, and social capacity. This was not merely a philosophical opinion. It was the dominant biological and political framework of the Graeco-Roman world into which early Christianity was born. And the Fathers of the Church — who were formed intellectually in that world, who absorbed its categories before they converted to Christianity — did not uniformly subject those categories to the radical critique that the theological datum of the equal imago Dei required.

The consequence is a tradition that contains, in permanent tension, the Magnificat and Aristotle's defective-male thesis — the radical theological affirmation of women's dignity and the cultural subordination of women's authority — and that has, across most of its history, managed this tension by allowing the cultural inheritance to govern institutional practice while the theological datum was assigned to the domain of spiritual aspiration.

The International Women's Day history that Sr Inigo narrates is the secular expression of the same tension and the same demand for resolution: fifteen thousand women marching in New York in 1908 were demanding, in the political register, what the Magnificat had declared in the theological register more than nineteen hundred years earlier. The world was not done with Mary's announcement. The tapeinōsis was not yet reversed. The megalynei was still needed.

Now, the Indian consequence of this historical diagnosis must be stated in two registers.

First: the colonial inheritance. The phrase "appendix-daughter of a colonial mother" — introduced in the NCRI framework that Sr Inigo cites — names a specific form of the ceremonial-structural gap as it operates in Indian religious congregations with European founding origins. These congregations were established with genuine charity, genuine sacrifice, and genuine faith. Their founders came and served at real personal cost. This is not in question.

What is in question is the institutional form in which the charism was transmitted. The constitutional documents, the spiritual culture, the aesthetic preferences, the mission priorities, the formation assumptions — all were designed for a European social context and imposed on an Indian one. And the consequence, for Indian women religious in particular, has been a form of consecrated life in which genuine Indian spiritual resources — the rich contemplative traditions of this subcontinent, the prophetic wisdom of its marginalized communities, the ecological attentiveness of its adivasi spiritual heritage, the theological creativity of its Dalit Christian thinkers — were systematically marginalised in favour of a European template that was never designed to hold them.

To become a "consecrated woman of and for her Motherland" is not to abandon the founding charism. It is to inhabit it fully enough to incarnate it appropriately — as the Incarnation itself incarnated the divine Word in a specific culture, language, and social context rather than in a transcultural abstraction.

Second: the Lenten convergence. Sr Inigo observes that International Women's Day — March 8 — falls invariably within the Season of Lent. This convergence is, she argues, providential. Lent is the season of honest self-confrontation, of naming the gaps between what is professed and what is practiced, of identifying the places where conversion is structurally required rather than merely spiritually desired. The Lenten examination of conscience, as it has been traditionally practiced, asks: where have I failed in prayer, fasting, and almsgiving? It rarely asks: where has my community failed in its structural treatment of women?

The convergence of Lent and Women's Day is an annual invitation — which most communities have not yet accepted — to widen the Lenten examination of conscience to include the structural question: does the life of this community embody the equal imago Dei that Genesis 1:27 and Galatians 3:28 declare? Does it honour, in its decision-making culture, its formation practices, and its allocation of authority, the woman who funded the mission of Jesus and received the first resurrection commission?

If the answer is no — and in most communities, the honest answer is a qualified no — then Lent is the season in which that answer can be faced, named, and responded to. Not with guilt, which is unproductive. With the metanoia that Lent is designed to produce: a turning, structural and personal, in the direction of what the Gospel actually requires.

PROPHETIC DEMAND & SILENCE

(3 minutes)

Let me close with a demand — and then with the silence the demand requires.

Mary sang the Magnificat. She was a teenager, unmarried, in a culture where her pregnancy could have resulted in social destruction. From that position of maximum tapeinōsis, she opened her mouth and declared the overthrow of every social arrangement that diminishes the human person. And she declared it in the present tense: "He has scattered. He has brought down. He has filled. He has sent away." Not: he will do these things in the eschatological future. He has done them. The inauguration is complete. The structure of the new world has been established in the divine act of entering human history in a woman's womb.

The communities that sing this text at Vespers every evening are singing a present-tense declaration of structural reversal. They are, in the most literal sense, committing themselves every evening — by the act of singing it — to the social programme it describes. The question is whether the daily singing of the Magnificat is allowed to interrogate the daily practice of the community that sings it.

Until and unless the communities that sing the Magnificat allow tapeinōsis — in whatever form it operates within their institutional life — to be named and addressed structurally rather than celebrated ceremonially, the Magnificat will continue to be the most revolutionary text in the New Testament sung as the most domesticated devotion in the liturgy.

The question I leave with you is one:

In your community's institutional life — its decision-making processes, its formation culture, its allocation of authority — what is the specific structural arrangement that your nightly singing of the Magnificat has not yet been allowed to interrogate? And what would it cost your community to allow it?

(Sustained silence — minimum four minutes. Do not fill it.)

CLOSING PRAYER

Mary — you sang it before you understood what it would cost you to mean it. You sang it as a teenager from the position of maximum tapeinōsis*: unmarried, pregnant, politically occupied, in a world where your testimony would not have been accepted in court.

And from that position you declared the overthrow of every arrangement that pressed you down. Not as future hope. As present reality.

We have sung your song every evening for centuries. We have venerated your image in every chapel. We have named our schools and hospitals and congregations after you.

And we confess: the world your Magnificat described — where the humble are lifted, where the hungry are filled, where the tapeinōsis is seen and reversed — that world is still being waited for by the women in our congregations, our parishes, our formation houses, our decision-making rooms.

Forgive us for the ceremonial equality that releases the structural inequality from the obligation of change. Forgive us for the Magnificat sung at Vespers that has not yet been allowed to reorganize the morning meeting. Forgive us for the roses distributed on March 8 that did not grow into anything by March 9.

Give us the structural courage to match the theological conviction. Give us the institutional honesty to allow the Magnificat to ask its questions in the rooms where it has never been heard.

And make every day, in every community that carries Your name, a day when Your daughters are called blessed — not in the liturgy only, but in the parish council, in the seminary timetable, in the formation programme, in the decision-making room, in the field and the slum and the jail corridor —

Every day, Lord. Every day.

Amen.*

SPEAKER NOTES

Register: Academic formation talk — the register of Fr. Joseph Lobo SJ. This talk engages a subject on which the audience may hold a range of views, some of them resistant. The Lobo method of grounding the argument in the Church's own theological tradition — Genesis 1:27, Galatians 3:28, the Magnificat, Gaudium et Spes 29 — is essential here. The speaker is not importing an external feminist programme. They are holding the Church accountable to its own most foundational texts.

Key theological moves:

  1. The ceremonial vs. structural model of women's dignity — with the ceremonial model traced to its specific institutional consequence: the dignity gap between the elevation of women in symbol and their exclusion from the structures of power and decision-making. The March 8 rose distribution is its liturgical expression — named with warmth and irony, not contempt.

  2. Megalynei and tapeinōsis recovered etymologically: megalynei from a devotional text back to its active verbal force (making-great, amplifying the divine into social and political space); tapeinōsis from a spiritual virtue (humility) back to a social condition (structural subordination, being pressed down). Together, they establish that the Magnificat is a political declaration of structural reversal — not a private act of piety.

  3. The scriptural constellation: Luke 1:46-55 (the Magnificat entire — read whole and read politically, with Brueggemann's criticizing/energizing structure), Galatians 3:28 (the abolition of hierarchy, not difference — with its unfinished interpretive history), Luke 8:1-3 (the named women who funded the mission of Jesus), John 4:7-26 (the Samaritan woman as primary recipient of the Johannine Christological self-disclosure — the Magnificat logic in the texture of Jesus's ministry).

  4. The historical diagnosis: the importation of Aristotle's defective-male thesis into Christian anthropology via the Fathers — creating the permanent tension between the imago Dei datum and the cultural subordination practice — with the March 8 movement as the secular expression of the same demand for resolution that the Magnificat had already made in the theological register.

  5. The two-register Indian consequence: the colonial inheritance (appendix-daughter condition — named with pastoral care as an invitation to self-examination, not a condemnation of founding congregations) and the Lenten convergence (the annual invitation to widen the examination of conscience to include the structural treatment of women — an invitation most communities have not accepted).

Secular and theological thinkers deployed: - Aristotle's Politics and Generation of Animals: the philosophical genealogy of the subordination (named precisely so the argument has a historical location, not a timeless inevitability) - Walter Brueggemann: criticizing and energizing functions of prophetic imagination (applied to the Magnificat's dual structure) - The 1908 New York march and its NCRI framework: secular and ecclesial expressions of the same demand for structural resolution of the ceremonial-structural gap

Church documents cited: - Genesis 1:27: equal imago Dei of male and female - Galatians 3:28: abolition of hierarchy in Christ - Gaudium et Spes 29: discrimination based on sex contrary to God's intent - Pope Saint John Paul II, Letter to Women (1995): honest admission of ecclesial failure - Pope Saint John Paul II, Mulieris Dignitatem: theology of genius femininus — cited precisely because the gap between its eloquence and institutional practice is itself evidence of the ceremonial-structural problem

Pacing: - The Opening must establish the three consequences of the ceremonial-structural gap with precision before the thesis. The gap between annual celebration and unchanged structures must be named without being harsh — it is a diagnosis, not an accusation. - Part I (Two Models) — the ceremonial model must be treated with genuine respect for its sincerity before its inadequacy is named. The rose distribution is not contemptible. It is insufficient. - Part II (Etymological Recovery) — tapeinōsis is the key. The recovery of Mary's social condition as the ground of the Magnificat's political content is the move that reframes everything. Take time with it. - Part III (Scriptural Constellation) — the Samaritan woman receives the Johannine Christological self-disclosure before Peter. This is not peripheral. Name it with the weight it deserves. - Part IV (Historical Diagnosis) — the Aristotle-to-Fathers genealogy should be stated precisely and briefly: the point is not to condemn the Fathers but to locate the distortion historically, giving the community permission to name it as historically contingent rather than theologically necessary. - The Lenten convergence paragraph is the creative heart of the chapter's contribution. Slow down here. - The silence after the closing demand is non-negotiable. Four minutes minimum.
